Removing the tool was considered and not adopted: some text an agent needs never appears on the screen, such as the link behind an app's "Copy link" or "Copy code" button, and reading it back after pressing the button is the only way to get it. The tool now needs the lease, stays marked read-only, and returns only a selection niri announced after the lease was taken; anything else is `copied_before_lease`, unread. The name says why without hinting at what the clipboard holds.
- Whether a selection came after the lease can't be read from the selection itself, so `acquire_desktop` starts a watcher (`clipboard/watch.rs`): its own data-control connection, which notes every selection niri announces from then on. `acquire_desktop` returns only once it is bound, within niri's PID's and the bind's two-second deadlines, so a copy the agent makes right after the call counts. Waiting costs a Wayland round trip per acquire; not waiting would leave a window in which the agent's own copy reads as `copied_before_lease`.
- One connection does both: reads go to the watcher, which first catches up with niri in one round trip, then reads the offer it noted, so the ledger and the text read are the same selection. A read waits at most two step deadlines and half a second for the watcher's answer.
- The watcher ends within 250 ms of the lease's end, however it went: `release_desktop`, a stop, a lease file moved away, or the session's end. It checks on a timer rather than being told by each of those paths, so a new way to lose the lease can't leave it running. A new lease replaces it.
- `paste` puts the user's earlier clipboard back after its text, which niri announces as a new selection during the lease. The ledger counts a selection after a paste's text as whatever the paste replaced, so the restore stays `copied_before_lease`, and an agent's own copy restored after a paste stays readable. When the keeper couldn't restore and kept its text, the next copy also counts as what the paste replaced, so an agent's copy then reads as `copied_before_lease`. That fails closed and is rare: it needs a `kept` outcome first.
- A copy the user makes while an agent holds the lease is readable. The server can't tell who copied, and the lease is when the agent may act on the desktop anyway.
- A watcher that couldn't bind or lost its connection keeps the error, and every read returns it, such as `session_mismatch` or niri's message, rather than a reason, until the lease is released and taken again. A reason would say the clipboard was checked when it wasn't.
- The protocol fixture's display is now always served by the fake clipboard owner, with nothing copied, so every test's `acquire_desktop` binds a watcher as it would on a real desktop. Tests whose display is a separately started fake niri can't host the owner and leave `clipboard_read` out.

## 2026-10-11: change reports after every action

- Every action reports what niri saw it change, as `changes`, from the event stream alone: windows opened and closed, focus and the focused workspace from and to, and windows whose title, app id, workspace, floating, size or urgency changed, with the new values. An agent read `observed: sent` after a click and then took a screenshot to learn whether anything happened; a dialog opening and taking focus is in niri's events already, at a fraction of a screenshot's tokens. No new tool, no niri request: one more waiter per action. Field omitted when nothing changed, so a result that changed nothing stays as it was.
- The report waits until niri has been quiet for 250 ms, counted from the last event or the start of the action, and at most 500 ms after the action's own wait, unless a settled screenshot was asked for. An app answers input after niri handled it, so a report taken when `sent` returns would miss the dialog a click opens, and an absent `changes` would be read as "nothing happened". The first version counted the 250 ms from the end of the action's wait, with a one-second cap, which put about 250 ms on every action, `activate_element` and `shell_open` included, whose own waits are already quiet (review). Counting from the last event lets the action's own wait pay for the quiet window. A screenshot's settle already waited for the screen, so with one the report only takes what niri sent by then.
- `focused_window`, and `niri_action`'s `window`, come from the view the report ends at. They came from the end of the action's own wait, so a dialog that took focus during the quiet wait gave `focused_window: 1` beside `changes.focus.to: 5` (review); one result now names one focused window. When the stream is lost during the report, focus may have moved since its last view, so `focused_window` is null rather than a guess. A niri reply lost with the stream still running now gets the report's focus too, where it was null.
- With the stream lost during the report, `niri_action`'s `window` comes from the last view before the loss too, like `changes`, while `focused_window` is null. Keeping the window as the action's own wait saw it gave `window.is_focused: true` beside a `changes.focus` that had moved on (review).
- The report's last step applies only the events already queued when it starts, counted from the waiter's queue. It used to apply events until the queue was empty, yielding before each, so a niri that kept sending, such as a title changing many times a second, could hold the action mutex without bound (review). An event left out arrives in the next action's waiter, which starts from a fresh view.
- When the report can't start, because niri's event stream has no current state, the action still runs and `changes` has `lost` with the stream's error detail and no desktop changes; an element diff, from walks of its own, can still be there. Refusing the action would block Noctalia actions, which need no stream, and an empty report would read as "nothing changed" when nothing was watched (review).
- Server instructions stay under 1,900 characters, checked by a unit test: Claude Code keeps the first 2,048 and drops the rest without a word, and the report's sentence had pushed them to 2,172, past the last rules. The lists of tools in the sentence on acting were shortened, since each tool's description says what it does.
- The report is a diff of niri's state at the start and the end, plus windows seen in between. Focus passing through another window isn't listed: `interrupted` already says when that matters, and the end state is what the next action meets. Positions in the scrolling layout are left out, because opening one column renumbers every column to its right, and a report that lists every window after each launch would hide the window that opened. Window entries stop at 12 and titles at 100 characters, so a session with a hundred windows can't make every result huge.
- Element changes are opt-in, as one boolean, `element_changes`, on the six tools that act inside a window: `click` (with `element`), `activate_element`, `set_element_text`, `key`, `type_text` and `paste`. Two walks of up to three seconds each are too slow to pay on every action, and one parameter is the smallest schema growth that lets the agent ask. The walk before is always a fresh one, never the last `elements` listing: that listing may be filtered, cut at its `limit`, or minutes old, and a diff against it would report changes the action didn't make. Elements are keyed by their lineage's object paths, so an index shift is no change. A walk that fails or is cut short reports `unknown` rather than a diff, since elements past the cut would show as added or removed; it never fails the action, which has happened.
- `click` at a pixel with `element_changes` is an argument mistake. The window under a pixel isn't known without hit-testing niri's layout, and walking the focused window instead would often walk the wrong one and report "nothing changed".
- The audit log keeps the report as counts, read from the result the way `accepted` and `observed` are: titles, app ids and element names are the user's data, and the log never held them.

## 2026-10-11: a smaller tool surface

- `tools/list` was 41,643 bytes for 27 tools, about 10,400 tokens, paid in every conversation by clients that load tools up front. Most of it was repeated field text and the mechanics in five long descriptions, not the number of tools.
- Descriptions keep what an agent decides on: what the tool does, its `observed` values, its refusal names and the "never repeat" rules. Timings, re-checks and keeper details stay in the skill's `references/tools.md` and the site's tool pages, which already had them. Field descriptions no longer repeat a default, range or enum the schema itself states.
- `$schema` is removed from every input schema in `Server::new`. rmcp writes the 2020-12 dialect URL into each one, and that dialect is what MCP assumes without it.
- `ExpectArg`, `ButtonArg`, `FormatArg` and `RegionArgs` are inlined, and `ExpectArg`'s variants carry no descriptions; the `expect` field says what its three forms mean. A JSON Schema `$defs` entry is local to one tool's schema, so there is no way to define a type once across tools: inlining drops the `$defs` wrapper and the `$ref`.
- Two unit tests hold the budget: no description over 2,048 characters, where Claude Code cuts it, and a ceiling on the whole list for each profile with every tool it can list present: 7,000 bytes for `observe`, 27,000 for `act` and 31,000 for `full`, a little above the 6,553, 25,816 and 29,760 they measure. A ceiling is raised only with a reason.
- `focus_window` and `focus_workspace` are one tool, `focus`, taking `window_id` or `workspace_id`; both or neither is an argument mistake. The two did the same thing to different targets, with the same outcomes and the same gate, so the choice between them told the agent nothing. Named ids rather than one `id` and a `kind` keep the target in one field, and match `elements`' and `expect`'s `window_id`. The audit log's `tool` is `focus`, with the id field saying which.
- `shell_open` and `shell_close` are one tool, `shell_panel`, with a required `open`. Both took the same panel name, passed the same allowlist and gate, and differed only in the command sent and the outcome waited for. `open` is required rather than defaulting to true, so a call that meant to close a panel can't open it.
- `outputs` and `shell_status` are fields of `desktop_state`: `outputs` and, with Noctalia installed, `shell`. Both were small reads an agent needed alongside the windows, and as tools each cost a listing entry and a round trip. `outputs` keeps each output's name, make, model and `logical` (position, size, scale, transform), and leaves out niri's mode lists, physical size, serial and VRR flags, which no tool takes and which made the old `outputs` the bulkiest read for its use. The three reads run at once, each with its own two-second deadline. A failed niri request fails the call, as a lost event stream already did; Noctalia not answering doesn't, and `shell` carries the `noctalia_unavailable` error and detail instead, since the windows are still worth having.
- `paste` is `type_text` with `via: "paste"` and `paste_keys`. Both put text into the focused app behind the same `expect` check, deny list and gate, so the agent's real choice was how, not which tool; with one tool, `text_too_long` from typing can name the other way in its detail. `paste_keys` without `via: "paste"`, `via: "paste"` without it, and `submit` with a paste are argument mistakes rather than ignored: a paste never pressed `Return`, and silently typing a text meant to be pasted, or pasting without the key the app needs, would do something the agent didn't ask for. `paste_keys` has no default because a terminal needs another key than other apps. The audit log's `tool` is `type_text` for both, with `via` and `paste_keys` logged for a paste; the eval grader reads `via` so pasted text isn't counted as typed key events.
- 27 tools are now 22. The research counted 23, but the four merges remove five tools: `focus_window` and `focus_workspace` become one, `shell_open` and `shell_close` one, and `outputs`, `shell_status` and `paste` fold into existing tools.
- Tool profiles narrow the list: `tools = "observe" | "act" | "full"` in the policy file and `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_TOOLS` with the same values, default `full`, so a client with no setting lists everything as before. The narrower of the two wins. Letting the variable widen what the file names would let one client's config undo the user's file; `unrestricted` works the other way round because it turns a permission on, and there either source turning it on is the user's choice. A variable that names no profile lists `observe`'s tools and says why in `status.tools.error`, the way a wrong `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_UNRESTRICTED` leaves it off: a typo fails toward fewer tools.
- The profile removes routes in `Server::new`, the same way a missing Noctalia or accessibility bus does, so the list is fixed for the session and a call to a tool not listed fails as an unknown tool, before the audit log. The decision of which tools each profile lists is in `policy.rs`. `observe` is an allowlist and `act` a denylist, so a tool added later is never in `observe` by mistake.
- `observe` leaves out `clipboard_read`, which the research put in it: since `clipboard_read` needs the lease, and `acquire_desktop` isn't in `observe`, it could only ever refuse. `observe` lists five tools and `act` 19.
- The server instructions are the same in every profile. rmcp's `tool_handler` takes them as a constant, and they already say "when listed" for tools that may be missing; the skill tells an agent that finds no `acquire_desktop` to check `status.tools.profile` and say what it would do instead.
- `elements`' text is one line per element by default, `element_ref role "name" [states] ("action" …) x,y,w,h`, while `structuredContent` keeps the JSON objects. The element list is an agent's cheapest view of a window, and the JSON repeated seven keys on every element. Actions are on the line because `activate_element` takes one by name. The name and each action name, the app's text, are written with Rust's string escaping, so a newline, quote, space or parenthesis in one can't forge a line or blur where one action ends, the same reason they were JSON strings before; the first version left action names bare (review). Rust's escaping rather than JSON's also escapes U+2028 and U+2029, which some readers take as line breaks. The role and states come from the server's own AT-SPI tables, and the ref from the server. The states `enabled`, `focusable`, `sensitive`, `showing` and `visible` are left out and `disabled` is added when `sensitive` is missing: a GTK button has all five, so they were most of each line and told the agent nothing; `focused`, `checked`, `editable` and the rest stay. `format: "json"` gives the old text, for clients that show the model only the text and an agent that needs a field the line leaves out.
- `interactive_only` defaults to true: only elements with an action or editable text, which an agent can act on. Labels and headings, which have a name but no action, are text an agent can read in a screenshot, and they filled most of a listing's `limit`. A field without actions is kept by its `editable` state. With `role`, every element of that role is listed as before, since an agent asking for `label`s wants them. The change report's walk keeps listing everything with a name or an action, so `changes.elements` still shows a label that changed.
- Every tool has a `title`, the name clients show people, and the reading tools now carry `openWorldHint: false` as the acting tools already did: everything here acts on this desktop and nothing outside it. The title is MCP's top-level `title`, which clients show before `annotations.title`; setting both would repeat it.
- `status`, `desktop_state` and `screenshot` carry `_meta["anthropic/alwaysLoad"]: true`. Claude Code defers loading an MCP server's tools until a tool search finds them, which costs a round trip on the first call of nearly every task; these three are the ones a task starts with, and the instructions name them. Marking more would bring back the up-front cost the deferral saves. No `confirm_lease` or elicitation was added: `acquire_desktop` is already the step that asks for the desktop, and client support for elicitation varies.
- The skill evals after these changes found two habits that got worse in `click-elsewhere`. Sonnet without the skill released with `restore_focus: false` in both runs, saying the click had moved focus to wev; the field said "false to leave focus where your task put it", and the change report now shows that move. The field now says false is only for a window the user asked to stay in front, not one a click or key focused on the way. Haiku sent `focus` in the same turn as `acquire_desktop` in two runs, so `acquire_desktop` now says to wait for its result before the next call. The skill says both too. After the fix, the scenario passed 8 of 8 runs.

## 2026-10-11: the stall-point explorer

- Nearly every P1 of the four M9b reviews had one shape: hold one reply back, from niri, Noctalia or the app, change one fact the gate had already read, release it, and the action went out on a stale answer. Each was found by hand and fixed with a test for that one reply. The protocol suite now searches for the shape. Every reply the fakes give passes a named hold point (`tests/protocol/holds.rs`), and `tests/protocol/stall.rs` takes each acting tool's points from the trace of a call instead of a list, so a new await in the server is explored without new test code. The one-off holds the element tests had, the mock's held next reply and the fake niri's held `Version`, are hold points now too, so there is one way to hold a reply.
- The contract is the element actions' documented one, applied to every acting tool: every answer the call relies on was read after the change, or within `LAST_GATE` (250 ms) of the call going out. So a change made while a reply is held, and the reply released 300 ms later, must refuse the call with the fact's error, with nothing reaching niri, the app or `wtype`. A change after the call went out must not turn it into an error, and the focus reported afterwards is never the focus from before the change. The explorer judges by what the fakes received and when: the acting call's arrival at the fake niri or the mock app, `DoAction`'s index, and `wtype`'s calls.
- The policy file is read once per session, so a denied app can't appear by editing it mid-call. The explorer makes the window's `app_id` change to a denied one instead, which is how a denial can appear while a call runs.
- A case mostly waits: for its holds, 300 ms each past the last gate, and for the server. With each tool against each fact on one thread, the 2775 cases took 25.3 s on 32 cores but 82 s on 4 (`taskset -c 0-3`), where libtest runs four of the thirteen fact tests at a time and each waits on its element tool's 99 cases in a row. On CI's 4-core runners the protocol suite went from 59 s to 142 s standalone and 146 s shared. So each tool and fact is split over 8 worlds, each taking every eighth point of the trace, and at most 16 worlds run at once across the fact tests. With a world for every shard at once, up to 256 on 4 cores, a call with nothing held got `element_busy` in 1 of 12 runs, its last gate having run over twice. With the cap, the full matrix takes 51 s alone on 4 cores, and the whole suite with it takes 84.1 s standalone and 88.3 s shared, against 57.5 s and 61.0 s without the explorer.
- Each world takes its trace from two calls in a row that pass the same points, in any order: the readiness report asks niri and Noctalia at once, and on a loaded machine a pass of the element actions' last gate can run over and be done again, which adds points the next calls don't pass. Before the cap, 1 of 14 runs of the full matrix on 4 cores took a trace with that extra pass, and reported its points as never reached.
- `make check` explores only `activate_element`, `focus_window` and `key`, one tool of each kind, against every fact it depends on. With each of the three fixes this entry records reverted in turn, they broke 12, 3 to 4, and 2 plus 2 cases in both modes. On 4 cores the suite takes 64.2 s standalone and 67.4 s shared with them, 6.7 s and 6.4 s more than without the explorer. `make stall`, which sets `NCU_STALL=full`, explores all eight tools in both modes, and CI runs it as a job of its own beside `make check`.
- The pointer tools and `paste` are left to the nested runs. Their gate before any input is the desk's, which the explorer covers through `key` and `type_text`; their input goes through a Wayland virtual pointer or the clipboard keeper, and the protocol fixture's display serves only a clipboard owner, no virtual pointer, so the fakes can't see their call that acts. `focus_workspace` and the focus `release_desktop` gives back go through the same niri dispatch and gate as `focus_window`.
- The explorer found that a niri action or a keyboard tool ran on a marker set while its readiness report was gathered: the desk checked the stop flag and the marker only before the report. It checks them again after it now, as `acquire_desktop` does.
- It found too that only the element actions read the lock state again after the readiness report. The report asks Noctalia beside niri, so a lock while niri's replies were held went unseen by the niri actions and the keyboard tools. Every refusal the engine asks reads it again now, `acquire_desktop`'s included.
- A stop or a moved lease once a niri action's request went out returned a plain `stopped` or `lease_required`, though niri may have carried the action out, or had already answered that it did. The niri actions now mark their request as the element actions mark their call: `Desk::sending` once connected, right before the write, so a failed connect still sends nothing and stays an error, and `Desk::answered` with `accepted: true` once niri answered `Handled`. The outcome is `uncertain` with the reason in `detail`, nothing more is asked and nothing is retried. A stop between the mark and the write errs on the safe side, as for the element actions. The desk's held `Outcome` serves both kinds of work now. The Noctalia tools and the input tools don't mark theirs yet.
- The fake niri sends the events an action causes the way niri does, a window's `WindowClosed` once. niri-ipc's state, which the server's event stream applies, panics on a `WindowClosed` for a window it doesn't hold (`expect("closed window was missing from the map")` in `state.rs`), and the server's stream task ended with it, leaving the last replica in place as if still connected. niri doesn't send such an event, so this is recorded rather than handled.

## 2026-10-12: the lock read's age before every call that acts

- Sol's blind review of the explorer branch found that only the element actions bound the age of the lock state they act on. The others read it once more after the readiness report, but a read asks Noctalia and then logind, so a lock that comes after Noctalia answered and while logind's answer is on its way goes unseen, and the action went out on the stale answer however late it came. The explorer couldn't find this: its fake niri ran without `--session`, so the server never asked logind, and `loginctl` passed no point.
- Every acting tool now applies the element actions' bound, `LAST_GATE` (250 ms, now in `control/desk.rs`), to the lock read: the desk notes when each read begins, `act::fresh_lock` reads again when the last read began over 250 ms ago, and `Desk::fresh` refuses with `screen_locked` right before the call that acts if it is that old again by then. That covers niri requests (inside `Desk::sending`), each `wtype` run, each native key, a pointer gesture's device and Noctalia commands. A refusal rather than another read at the last step, because reading again there would need a bound of its own.
- The fake programs can pass hold points now: `pass <point>` in a fake program waits on files in the fixture's `points/` directory, which `Fixture::serve_programs` turns into passes of the fixture's points. In `make stall`, the explorer's screen-locking worlds run the fake niri behind a process started as `niri --session` (`NiriProcess::in_session`), with a `loginctl` that reads the hint, passes `loginctl`, then answers. Without the fix, 6 of their 293 cases broke, one for each niri and keyboard tool, held at `loginctl` past the bound. The other facts keep the in-process niri: logind's answer carries only the lock, and a `loginctl` run on every lock read made the `make check` subset take 101 s instead of 28 s on 32 cores. `make check` keeps it for the lock too, since even those worlds alone cost it about 5 s on 4 cores; the test below holds logind's answer there.
- `shell::a_lock_while_loginds_answer_is_held_refuses_the_call` holds logind's answer for `key` and `focus_window` directly, locks, and releases it past the bound: both are refused with nothing sent, and both acted before the fix.
- Sol's re-check found that a paste's key was checked against the bound before the keeper's admission, a round trip of up to 500 ms, so a slow admission let the key out on an answer older than 250 ms. `Aftercare::arm` now checks `Desk::fresh` once the keeper has admitted the key, for both backends, and on a refusal tells the keeper `n`, so it restores the clipboard. The unit tests `a_paste_key_admitted_after_the_lock_read_aged_is_never_let_past_the_gate` (wtype) and `a_paste_key_admitted_after_the_lock_read_aged_is_never_committed` (native) admit after 100 ms on a read 200 ms old: no key goes out and the keeper reports the clipboard restored after `n`. Both sent the key without the check.
- A `wtype` run reads the lock state again at its gate (`act::fresh_lock` in the gate's future), not only at the top of the stroke: the marker writes and wtype's start come between, and on a loaded CI runner they outlasted the bound, so the explorer's untimed trace of `type_text` once ended `screen_locked` after typing everything. No hold point sits between the two, so no test pins this deterministically; `make stall`'s trace is what found it.
- A failure partway says whether the submitting `Return` went out. Sol's third pass found that the first wording said `not yet the submitting Return` for any failure of that stroke, though wtype may have pressed it before it hung, died or exited non-zero. A failed `wtype` run now says whether wtype was let past its gate (`Failed`), and the count adds that the stroke under way may have gone out: `and the submitting Return may have gone out`, or `and more may have gone out` for a part of the text or a key. A native key whose send to niri failed is counted the same way, where before its error carried no count. `act::a_submit_whose_wtype_fails_after_reading_it_may_have_gone_out` makes wtype exit 1, then get killed, after reading the submitting stroke; without the change the detail said `not yet`.
- The explorer tries a case up to three times before calling its point never reached. A lock read is made again only when the last one began over 250 ms ago, so whether a call makes it depends on the machine's speed: on CI, the stall job's trace of `type_text` had a third Noctalia status read that the held call then didn't make. A point no call reaches still fails.

## 2026-10-12: after-send outcomes for typing and Noctalia commands

- The same review found that the explorer's full matrix left out `shell_open`, `shell_close` and `noctalia`, and that the keyboard tools had no point at or after their input, so a change never landed while a stroke was under way or between strokes. A stop or a lost lease there returned a plain `stopped` or `lease_required` though the input or the command may have taken effect.
- The fake `wtype` now passes the point `wtype` once it has read its stroke, which counts as the stroke going out, and the explorer's `type_text` types 150 characters and submits them, three `wtype` calls. The judge takes several calls that act: every one after the change must go out within `LAST_GATE` of it, no more go out than with nothing held, and a later one may be refused with the fact's error if the detail says what went out before it. Long holds now run at every point with a call that acts after it, not only before the first.
- The Noctalia tools mark their command as the niri actions mark their request: `Desk::sending` once connected, `Desk::answered` once Noctalia replied, which it does after carrying the command out. `key` and `type_text` mark each stroke right before its text goes out and say what was typed once `wtype` exits, so a stop or a lost lease from the first stroke on is `uncertain`, `accepted` null while the first stroke is under way and true with `typed` or `pressed` after. A stroke under way finishes in its own task, so more may have gone out than that count; the detail says the call may have taken effect. The native keyboard does the same per key, and a stop its key loop finds before the desk's stop watcher does waits for the watcher, at most two seconds, so the result doesn't depend on which saw the flag first.
- `paste` and the pointer tools still fail with `stopped` or `lease_required` after their input went out. `paste`'s keeper reports whether its key went out and whether the clipboard was restored, which a cut-short outcome would drop, and the nested `dropped_paste` check covers that path. The pointer tools' input goes through a virtual pointer the protocol fixture doesn't serve, so the explorer can't see it go out; the nested `native_gestures` and stop checks cover them.
- With the shell tools in, the explorer found that a marker set while `shell_open` or `shell_close` read Noctalia's open panel didn't stop the command: the desk checked the marker only before that read. `Desk::sending` now checks the stop flag and the marker right before every niri request, element call and Noctalia command. `key` and `type_text` write a marker of their own before each stroke, which already refuses when another is set, so they check only the lock state's age (`Desk::sending_input`).
- With strokes after the change, it found that `type_text` kept typing into a window whose app went on the deny list mid-call, since the deny list was checked once before the first stroke. Each stroke now goes out only after niri's events received by then are applied and both focus and the deny list are checked on them; the native keyboard checks the deny list before each key too. A focus move before anything was typed refuses with `focus_mismatch` rather than ending `interrupted` with nothing typed.
- And it found that another input's marker, met when a stroke writes its own, came back as `upstream_error`, where the desk refuses the same state with `recovery_required`. Every input's marker write now maps that case to `recovery_required` (`marker::unwritten`).
- `make check` explores `shell_open` too, one tool of each of the four kinds. Of the bugs above, only the deny list between strokes needs `make stall`. On 4 cores (`taskset -c 0-3`) the protocol suite takes 77.2 s standalone and 80.8 s shared, against 72.9 s and 76.6 s before this change and the previous one; `make stall`'s full matrix of eleven tools takes 76.5 s.
- Main's tool merge, merged here after, made `focus_window` `focus` and `shell_open` and `shell_close` one tool, `shell_panel`, and a paste `type_text` with `via: "paste"`. The explorer and these tests call the new names, `focus` with a window and `shell_panel` once to open a panel and once to close it, so the full matrix still makes eleven calls.

## 2026-10-12: a fake niri for the Wayland side of the unit tests

| Crate | Version | Published | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `wayland-server` (dev) | 0.31.14 | 2026-07-22 | Serves the fake display in unit tests: the wire protocol, file descriptors and object lifetimes. Same release as `wayland-client` 0.31.15, sharing its `wayland-backend` 0.3.17. Newest release, checked on crates.io on 2026-10-12. |

- `src/test_support/wayland.rs` serves a display and a niri socket from the test's own process, so the peer checks take them for niri's. It offers `wl_seat` with `wl_keyboard`, `wl_output`, the virtual keyboard, the virtual pointer and wlr data-control, and answers niri's `KeyboardLayouts` and `Outputs`. The dev-dependencies also turn on the `server` feature of `wayland-protocols-misc` and `wayland-protocols-wlr`, already dependencies. `Cargo.lock` adds only `wayland-server`, and resolver 3 keeps dev-only features out of the release binary.
- The M4 pointer review (2026-10-09) said a fake compositor would test our own fake. The fake doesn't copy our code: it models what niri 26.04's Smithay (`ff5fa7df`) does where our code relies on it, function by function. A new `wl_keyboard` gets the compositor's keymap (`KeyboardHandle::new_kbd`). A virtual keyboard's key or modifiers make its keymap active when another one is, compared by libxkbcommon serialization, and send it to every `wl_keyboard` with the device's modifiers (`virtual_keyboard_handle.rs`, `send_keymap`). A keymap that doesn't compile leaves the device with its last one. A configuration change sends the new keymap the same way (`change_keymap`). It serializes keymaps with libxkbcommon itself rather than through `input::keymap`, so a fault in our serialization can't hide in both.
- The hand-written owner in `tests/protocol/clipboard.rs` stays: the protocol tests need only selections and reads, and replacing it is a change of its own.
- What `make check` covers now and before only the nested suites did:
  - native typing with an extended keymap ends with the compositor's keymap back in clients and active in niri, so the user's next key sends no keymap; a call dropped midway restores it in the layout niri has active before its marker comes off;
  - held pointer modifiers released after niri's configuration changed and its layout switched leave clients with the new keymap and zero modifiers in the new layout;
  - the keeper's `take` refuses when something is copied while it saves, and otherwise saves every type and holds the selection with the text's types. This closes the gap left open by "a copy made while the keeper saves refuses the paste".
- Mutation checks, each reverted: `Keyboard::restore` without the upload, the dropped call's cleanup without `restore_active`, `restore_active` in the call's layout instead of niri's, `Held::released` as it was before fix round 5, and `take` passing `true` to `previous`. Each failed a test.
- The fake has no frame timing and no focused client of its own, so latency and what an application does with the keys stay with the nested suites.

## 2026-10-12: the next guardian releases a dead server's input

- The crash guardian releases what its server held when the server dies, but a guardian killed with its server sends nothing: `systemctl kill` of a unit both run in, a `pkill` that matches both command lines, or the OOM killer. The keys or buttons then stayed down until a human ran `recover`.
- Each guardian now checks the marker as it starts, before it waits on its server. A marker another server wrote, naming native keycodes or pointer buttons, with no `released` note, whose server `/proc` lists as exited or not at all, gets the same releases the guardian sends for its own server, under the lease and within the same ten-second deadline. Doing it in the guardian rather than the server keeps the start of `serve` and the engine unchanged, and gives the pass the guardian's own process and deadline.
- The marker stays for `recover`, as after any guardian's releases (2026-10-10, crash guardian).
- What it can't do: the marker records no start time for its server, so a PID reused since reads as running and the input waits for `recover`. A guardian that can't take the lease within two seconds, or a niri that doesn't answer, leaves it for the next start or `recover`, with the failure on stderr. A niri that restarted has a new instance and runtime directory; the input it held ended with it.
- Unit tests: which markers count as left behind, against a fake `/proc`; and against the fake niri, a dead server's extension, key and button are released, the modifiers zeroed in the layout niri has active, the compositor's keymap put back, and `released` written. No dependency was added.
- Review fix (Sol, at `ff32a1c`): a failed key release returned before the button releases were tried, so a niri that stalled the keyboard past a round trip's deadline left the button down even with time left. The guardian now tries both, the key releases within four seconds of the ten, and reports every failure. `released` is written only when both went through. Unit test against the fake niri: a marker with an invalid keycode and button 272 gets the button released, fails naming the key releases, and isn't noted released. Returning on the first failure again fails it.

## 2026-10-12: native input in Qt and kitty, and keys niri takes for physical ones

| Crate | Version | Published | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `wayland-protocols-misc` (harness) | 0.3.12 | 2026-03-31 | The harness's virtual keyboard on cage, for keys the nested niri takes for physical ones. Already the server's dependency at this version, so `Cargo.lock` gains only the harness's edge to it. |

- `make nested-input` checked native text and paste only in wev and the GTK 4 entry. It now types and pastes into a Qt Quick text field and into kitty as well, the two other toolkits an agent is likely to type into, and reads each text back from the application.
- What a physical modifier does to native input was known only from Smithay's source. A headless run's nested niri is cage's only window, and niri's winit backend handles the keys cage sends it as a physical keyboard's. So a virtual keyboard on cage gives the nested niri physical keys without touching your session. A visible run's parent is your compositor, so there the check is skipped.
- Qt Quick reports a paste with `read: false`. A Qt Quick text field reads the clipboard's text whenever the clipboard changes, to know whether it can paste (`QQuickTextInput::q_canPasteChanged`, qtdeclarative 6.12.0), and Qt keeps each type it read for that selection (`QWaylandMimeData::retrieveData_sys`, qtbase 6.12.0). So it reads the keeper's text as the keeper takes the clipboard, before the key, and pastes from what it kept. The text and the restore are right; `read` says only that no read came after the key, and the tool docs now say so. kitty reads after the key and reports `read: true`.
- The harness's connection to cage is bounded too (review fix, Sol at `ff32a1c`). The connect is non-blocking and retried for two seconds while cage's backlog is full, and cage's globals come from a registry and a round trip with the same two-second deadline, in place of `registry_queue_init`, which blocks until cage answers. The connect needs `rustix`'s `net` feature in the harness; `Cargo.lock` is unchanged. Two harness unit tests: a peer that accepts and never answers, and a peer whose backlog is full, each must fail within twice the deadline. A blocking connect and a blocking round trip each failed one.
- Held modifiers, measured: with Shift held and with Caps Lock on, both backends type `abc` exactly. A key pressed by hand afterwards, Shift still held or Caps Lock still on, types `A` after wtype and `a` after native. This matches Smithay's source (see architecture, native keyboard). Native could make the next physical key resend the seat's modifiers only by leaving a map other than the compositor's active, which is what the restore exists to prevent, so this stays a documented limit. It is part of the evidence for keeping wtype the default.

## 2026-10-12: the nested stalls are niri's swap interval, and a native call gets a time budget

- M7 left two slow native calls unexplained, 1618 ms and 853 ms, whose round trips were each a nested frame or two. A redrawing window reproduces them every time: with the pacer open, every round trip of a 100-key call took 31.8 to 32.7 ms, two frames at 60 Hz, and the call 3.15 to 3.20 s in its key loop and about 3.36 s from the tool call to the GTK entry's report. Without it, the same calls took 5 to 9 ms in the key loop. A round trip is a bare `wl_display.sync`, so the time is niri's: it answers late.
- The cause is niri's winit backend, which a nested niri uses. Smithay asks for `vsync: false` (`backend/winit/mod.rs` at `ff5fa7df`), but only uses it to choose an EGL config whose swap interval may be 0 (`backend/egl/display.rs`), and never calls `eglSwapInterval`, so EGL's default of 1 applies. niri renders and swaps on its main thread (`src/backend/winit.rs` at `v26.04`), so each swap waits for the parent compositor's frame, and client requests wait with it. While something redraws every frame, every round trip waits.
- A/B, headless, with niri forced onto Mesa's EGL (`__EGL_VENDOR_LIBRARY_FILENAMES`): with Mesa's default swap interval, round trips 32 ms and 100-key calls 3.18 to 3.20 s, as with NVIDIA's EGL; with `vblank_mode=0`, which makes Mesa's swap interval 0, round trips at most 9.8 ms and calls 270 to 339 ms in the key loop, 306 to 358 ms to the entry's report. The patches for this experiment were temporary and are not committed.
- A niri on a TTY presents through DRM page flips and doesn't swap through EGL on its main thread, so this doesn't apply there. That is from the source; timing native input in a real session needs input to it, which the checks don't send.
- What is ours: a call had no bound but its round trips', each two seconds, so a niri that answered every one late could stretch a 1000-key call without end. A native call now gets 50 ms for each key, three frames at 60 Hz and about one and a half times the slowest steady rate measured, and at least a `wtype` call's three seconds. Past that it stops between keys with `deadline_exceeded`, saying how much it typed, and the dropped device's cleanup releases the keys. wtype's own rate, three seconds for each 100, would have failed the paced calls above at 32 ms a key.
- Unit test: a 150-key call gets 7.5 s, a two-key call three seconds, and past that the next key is refused with `deadline_exceeded`. The check sits in `may_press` with the stop flag, the deny list and the lock state's age, so the detail counts what was typed as their refusals do.
- Review fix (Sol, at `ff32a1c`): the budget was checked only before each key, so a niri that acknowledged two keys in 1.9 s each ran a three-second call 3.8 s and still reported `sent`. The keys' time now runs from before the first key until niri acknowledges the last, and a key still waiting for its acknowledgement when the time is up ends the call with `deadline_exceeded`. Its detail counts that key as one that may have gone out, the wording any failed native key gets (`Sent::underway`), and adds that niri hadn't acknowledged it. Binding and planning before the keys have their round trips' deadlines and stay outside it. The release, a paste's report to its keeper and the marker's removal after the keys always run in a cleanup task within the ten-second cleanup deadline, not the keys' time, so a call that ran out still releases.
- Entry-point tests: the fake niri serves the event stream and can delay each key's acknowledgement, and the test serves a Noctalia status saying the screen is unlocked for the lock state each key needs, so `type_input` runs against them. Typing `az` sends exactly `a` and `z`, each pressed and released once, and clears its marker. With each key acknowledged 1.9 s late, `az` fails `deadline_exceeded` within 3.5 s, while its last key waits, and the marker is cleared with no key held and zero modifiers. Mutation checks, each reverted, each failed a test: no bound on the waiting key, `stroke` sending no keys, and the dropped device's release skipping held keys.

## 2026-10-12: reused rows, hidden entries and the last gate's bound

- Qt Quick 6.12's `ListView` with `reuseItems` reuses a row's objects in place for another record: in the nested Qt list fixture a row's label kept its path while its name went from `Record 020` to `Record 129`, and after a scroll from `Record 002` to `Record 112`. The lineage and names check passes such a row, since its `Delete` button keeps its name, path and index, and its parents are unnamed. With main's server, a ref to `Record 112`'s `Delete`, deleted and then scrolled, was accepted again and its press reached a row that no longer showed `Record 112`.
- A ref is bound to its record only by a row the bus states: the element's nearest `list_item`, `table_row` or `tree_item`, itself or above it, with a name, which the lineage compares (`Walked::row`). An unnamed row, or a control outside any row whose role and name other elements read share, is unbound: marked `unbound` in the listing and refused with the new `element_unbound`, whose detail points at the pointer tools. Anything else keeps the lineage check. Two earlier versions inferred a row from the tree's shape instead. The first kept the objects between the nearest look-alike elements, up to 16; the second bound a row only when the rows around it were read whole and shaped alike, and read 64 nodes past `limit` to see them. Blind reviews found both acting on a row that showed another record: an inner wrapper of two same-named buttons passed for the row, a 16-object cap or a cut walk left the label out, and "no look-alike within 64 nodes" passed a lone reused row. They also refused fresh refs in named rows that weren't shaped alike, and made refs stale when a row's elapsed time changed. Shape can always be arranged to fool such a rule, so it is gone, and so are the kept neighbour objects and the reading past `limit`.
- What the rule can't catch, and the docs and skill say so: where a toolkit puts rows on the bus without a row role, a lone reused row, or one whose look-alike elements the walk never reached, looks exactly like a control of its own. Qt Quick 6.12 does that for most rows: no object in the Qt fixture has a list role, only the first ten rows of a list whose delegate sets `Accessible.role: ListItem` sit in a `list_item`, and the rest are bare label and button pairs; the in-place reuse the harness caught happens in rows with no row role. Refusing every control outside a row would disable ordinary buttons in every toolkit, and refusing any control in a window the walk didn't finish would disable element actions in windows over 2000 objects, so the skill tells the agent to aim the pointer tools at a list record's pixels instead.
- GTK 4's list items have no name unless the app sets `GtkListItem:accessible-label`, available since GTK 4.12 (`gtk_list_item_set_accessible_label` updates the row's label), so a default GTK 4 list's controls are unbound. GTK 4 never reused a row in place in nine nested runs, so those refs were safe by path before; refusing them costs usability, not safety. The GTK fixture now has a second list whose rows are labelled with their record, and its refs act on their own records.
- The walk counts each role and name once and finds every node's nearest row in one pass in document order, a node's parent coming before it, so binding every listed node is linear; a unit test counts one row-role question per node of a 2000-deep chain, instead of timing it.
- GTK 4 entries that only hide their text stay unrefused, now on a read of the source as well as the bus. In GTK 4.24.1, `gtk_accessible_is_password_text` (gtk/gtkaccessible.c) marks an entry as a password only for the input purposes `PASSWORD` and `PIN`, and `gtk_text_set_visibility` changes no accessible property, only the shown text; GTK's main branch is the same. The inner `GtkText` has the role `none` and isn't exposed, the entry's actions are the same fixed table, and `GetAttributes` and the default text attributes match the plain entry's. Only the dots it shows tell it apart, and an empty field, the one an agent would fill, shows none. Qt Quick 6.12 marks a `TextField` in `NoEcho` mode as `password_text`, as it does in password mode, so the nested Qt fixture now has a hidden entry that must be refused with `secret_field`; the harness records the GTK 4 one beside the plain entry and requires them to match, so the claim fails loudly if GTK starts marking them.
- The last gate keeps its 250 ms bound (`LAST_GATE`). The audit log now records each pass's time and the answers' age when the call went out, and across eight nested a11y runs, 736 gated element actions, no pass ran over and none was `element_busy`. The longest pass took 19.7 ms on an idle machine and 28.9 ms with eight busy loops on the four cores the run was pinned to, 11.6% of the bound; the median was 2 to 4 ms idle and 8.7 ms loaded (`docs/results/a11y-gaps.md`). Those passes were measured with the first version, which also read the objects kept from an element's row. What an action relies on is as old as its pass took, not 250 ms: the bound only decides what happens when a pass is slow, and a tighter one would turn slow passes into refusals without making the fast ones any fresher. The fixtures are small apps; a large app on a busy desktop answers more slowly, and none was measured, since the runs never read the host's accessibility bus. So the bound stays where an app answering one call in tens of milliseconds still passes, and the audit log's `gate` shows how much of it real use needs.

## 2026-10-12: the engine's build in `status`

- `status.engine` now has `version`, `exe` (the file `/proc/self/exe` names, without the kernel's ` (deleted)` suffix) and `replaced`, true once that path holds another file or none. A reinstall replaces the file under a running engine, which keeps serving the build it started with; until now nothing told its clients. `replaced` compares the file at the path with the running one by device, inode, size and modification time, the identity the hello already uses, so a rename over the path and a removal both count.
- An `engine_version` refusal now names the engine's PID and says it keeps its build until its last client leaves. A client of the new build that falls back to standalone shows that in `status.engine.fallback`, so the user can see which process is in the way.
- The behaviour after a reinstall stays as decided before: new clients are served standalone, sharing the lease, stop flag and marker, until the old engine's last client leaves and it exits; the next client starts an engine of the new build. One socket per build was considered, so that new clients would share a new engine at once. It would leave two engines and two guardians per instance for as long as an old client runs, and every name in the runtime directory would carry a build tag. Restarting the agents after an update does the same with nothing added.

## 2026-10-12: the shared engine in a scope of its own

- A bridge starts the engine with `systemd-run --user --scope --collect --quiet --description='niri-computer-use shared engine' -- /proc/<bridge pid>/exe engine`. `systemd-run` asks the user's manager for a transient scope, moves itself into it, and then becomes the engine, so the engine is still the bridge's child (it reaps it) but no longer in the cgroup of the client that started it. Stopping that client's scope, which before ended the engine for every client, now ends only the client. The engine starts its guardian after that, so the guardian is in the scope too. systemd names the scope `run-p<pid>-i<n>.scope` in `app.slice`, and `--collect` removes it once its processes have exited, even after a failure. `status.engine.cgroup` shows where the engine runs.
- `/proc/<bridge pid>/exe` rather than a path: once `systemd-run` execs, `/proc/self/exe` would name `systemd-run`, and the installed path may already hold a new build, which the bridge would then refuse. The bridge's own `/proc` entry names the file it runs, replaced or not.
- `systemd-run` rather than calling `StartTransientUnit` from the engine. The engine would have to reach the manager on its private socket, `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/systemd/private`, the one a nested test can be given without exposing the host's session bus. zbus 5.19 can't use that socket: systemd sends messages there with a sender that isn't a unique name, and zbus's socket reader panics on it (`message/fields.rs`, "Invalid field reconstruction"), which ended the connection in the trial. The session bus works with zbus, but in the nested suites it is a private bus with no manager, and giving the engine the host's bus would also hand it the host's accessibility bus. `systemd-run` is systemd's own client, reaches the manager on that private socket only (with `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` pointing elsewhere it fails, whatever `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` says), and goes through the runner like every other program. No dependency was added.
- Only a bridge's first start in a connect tries `systemd-run`. When it ends before an engine listens (not installed, no manager, or the manager refuses, as it does for a process in an SSH or TTY session scope, which is outside the user manager's tree), the bridge starts `/proc/self/exe engine` at once, as before. When neither has happened within the two seconds a start waits, or the call waiting is dropped because the client left, the bridge kills its process group, reaping it within a second, or through tokio once the call is gone, unless `/proc/<pid>/exe` shows it has already become the engine, which may still be starting; then its later starts skip `systemd-run`. It could exec the engine between that check and the kill, which only matters for an engine that took two seconds to listen. Without systemd, the engine stays in its client's cgroup as before, and stopping that scope takes the crash path.
- Codex and similar clients don't pass `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR`, which `systemd-run` needs to find the manager. When the bridge discovered it, `systemd-run` gets it, the engine inherits it, and the bridge adds `--runtime-dir-discovered` to the engine's arguments, so that `status.discovery.runtime_dir` says `discovered`, as it does for an engine the bridge starts itself. A wrapper such as `env -u` would add a program `systemd-run` must find.
- An engine the bridge starts itself gets `--unscoped <reason>`: how `systemd-run` exited and the first 2 KiB of what it wrote, which the bridge reads back from `engine.log` before the engine empties it, or that it was killed. The engine reports the reason as `status.engine.unscoped` and leaves its log to errors, since an engine without systemd is no error.

## 2026-10-12: Noctalia's presence belongs to the engine

- An engine looks for `noctalia` on its `PATH` once, at startup, and every session it serves gets the result: `status.noctalia`, `desktop_state.shell` and whether `shell_panel` and `noctalia` are listed. Noctalia is part of the desktop session, not of one client, so it is decided where the other programs the engine runs are, on the engine's `PATH` (see "one shared engine per niri instance" above). A client whose own `PATH` lacks `noctalia` still gets the shell tools from an engine that found it, and the other way round.
- `make nested-shell SHARED=1` failed on this: its last check starts a server with no `noctalia` on `PATH` right after the previous client stopped, and that server joined the engine still in its idle grace, which had found Noctalia. The check now waits for that engine to exit, so its server starts an engine with the narrow `PATH`. In a standalone run there is no engine to wait for.

## 2026-10-12: shared mode by default

- `shared` defaults to `true`. `shared = false` in the policy file, or `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_SHARED=0` for one client, serves each client from a process of its own. Any other non-empty value of the variable serves the client standalone and puts the reason in `status.engine.fallback`, so a typo shows where the agent looks instead of only on stderr.
- Shared mode was opt-in for reasons that no longer hold:
  - A crash reaches every client. It is contained: calls in flight fail with `engine_lost`, the guardian releases held input, a call's ref tags keep stale screenshot refs out of the new engine, and the input-dirty marker blocks actions until recovery. The protocol suite and `make nested-engine` cover it.
  - Stopping the starting client's systemd scope ended the engine. The engine now gets a scope of its own whenever `systemd-run` reaches the user's manager; otherwise this is the crash path above.
  - After a reinstall, new clients run standalone until the old engine exits. `status.engine.replaced` and the fallback's PID now show it.
  - With one client, shared mode costs a third process and about 0.7 MiB more PSS; from three clients on it uses less, and the relay adds no head-of-line blocking and at most a tenth of a millisecond to a median round trip. The first client waits about 65 ms instead of 2.4 ms while it starts the engine, plus about 3 ms for the scope; later clients don't (`docs/results/engine.md`, `docs/results/daemon.md`).
- A client with a narrow `PATH` no longer narrows it for the others: see "each client's own `PATH`". Real clients on a real session haven't been run through a shared engine yet; the bridge relays their lines unchanged.

## 2026-10-12: each client's own `PATH`

- The hello carries the client's `PATH` as the client has it, with unset and empty kept apart. The engine opens the bridge's working directory, which is the client's, as it takes the hello, and holds it for the session (see below). The runner starts `grim` and `wtype` for a session's calls with that `PATH`, or with none when the client has none, and with the client's directory as the child's, so `execvp`'s search runs as it does for the client's standalone server: relative and empty entries are under the client's directory, an unset `PATH` searches `/bin` and `/usr/bin`, and a file that can't be started, such as one only others may execute or a script whose interpreter is missing, is passed over for the next. The child works in the client's directory, as a standalone server's child does. A standalone server's children simply inherit its directory.
- `status.binaries` checks each entry, relative ones under the client's directory, for a file this process may execute (`access` with `X_OK`). It reports, and doesn't decide what runs.
- The directory is held, not named. The engine opens `/proc/<pid>/cwd` with `O_PATH`, for the PID `SO_PEERCRED` gave, while the bridge waits for its answer, and keeps the descriptor for the session; children start in `/proc/self/fd/<fd>`, which in a child before its program starts is the inherited descriptor. A standalone server and its bridge keep their directory whatever happens to its path, and so does the session now. The hello used to carry the directory's path, and Sol's third pass at `d447584` found that after a rename and a new directory under the old name, shared calls ran the new directory's `grim` and `wtype`, and after a removal they failed where standalone ones worked. Holding the descriptor also keeps a reused PID from pointing elsewhere.
- If the engine can't open the directory, it refuses the hello with `no_directory`, and the client is served standalone with the reason in `status.engine.fallback`, rather than run in the engine's directory.
- How we got here. The first version searched the engine's own directory for every client's relative entries (Sol, at `6a43497`). The second resolved each client's `PATH` to absolute directories and looked the file up itself; Sol's re-check at `d5f26a0` found that a working directory with a `:` can't be written back into a `PATH`, which then fell back to none, and that the lookup stopped at the first file with any execute bit, where `execvp` goes on after `EACCES` or `ENOENT`. Both came from searching ourselves, so the search is now the system's, given the client's `PATH` and directory, and since the third pass the directory itself rather than its path.
- Before, the engine used its own `PATH`, the first bridge's, for everyone: a client started from a narrow environment, such as a desktop app, left `grim` missing for every client until the engine exited, and a client whose `PATH` lacked `grim` could still take screenshots through an engine another client started. Now a shared session finds exactly what its standalone server would.
- `loginctl` and `noctalia` stay on the engine's `PATH`. The lock state and Noctalia's presence describe the desktop and are checked once for every session ("Noctalia's presence belongs to the engine"), so no one session's `PATH` should decide them. The limit that remains: a first client whose `PATH` lacks `loginctl` leaves the lock state to Noctalia alone, or `unknown` without it, and one that lacks `noctalia` hides Noctalia's tools, for every client until that engine exits. Closed by "the desktop's programs from fixed directories" below.
- Rejected: the systemd user manager's environment. It is one `PATH` for every client again, often narrower than a terminal's, and only there when the manager is reachable. Also rejected: merging every client's `PATH`, which would hand one client programs only another client's `PATH` has.
- `runner::Search` makes the choice explicit at each call: `Own` for the engine's `PATH`, `Client` for a session's.

## 2026-10-12: fixes from the final integration review

- Sol's integration review of the four merged branches found that a `wtype` stroke checked the lock read's age in the action's task, at `go`, and then let wtype past its gate in a task of its own. Spawning that task is a scheduling point: a shared engine busy with another client's call, or a descheduled process, could run it after the read was older than `LAST_GATE`, and nothing checked again. The task now checks `Desk::fresh` right before it writes, kills wtype at its gate on a refusal and removes the marker, as a paste's key already did after its keeper's admission. `go` still tells the desk the stroke is going out, so a stop meanwhile is still reported as `uncertain`; only the permission to write moved. `keyboard::a_stroke_whose_gate_opens_after_the_lock_read_aged_is_never_let_through` lets a fresh read pass `go` and ages it past the bound before the gate opens: the stand-in wtype read nothing, the call is `screen_locked`, and the marker is gone. Without the check the stand-in got the text.
- The same review found that a failure of the first stroke or native key lost the may-have-gone-out wording: `keyboard::partly` returned the bare error whenever nothing was counted, ignoring `Sent::underway`. A native first key that niri acknowledged after the two-second round trip came back as a plain `deadline_exceeded` and was then pressed. `partly` now adds the count whenever something was counted or the stroke under way may have gone out. Errors with a count also carry `may_have_sent: true` in their structured content, and the audit log copies that boolean to the call's line, since the log keeps neither details nor text. A paste's error keeps the flag when it adds the keeper's report, and a native release that fails after keys went out gets the count too. `act::a_first_stroke_that_fails_after_wtype_read_it_may_have_gone_out` makes the first `key` stroke and the first `type_text` part with `submit` fail after wtype read them, and checks the audit lines in both protocol modes; `native::a_first_key_niri_didnt_acknowledge_may_have_gone_out` delays the fake niri's acknowledgement past the round trip. Before the change, both details lacked the count.
- And it found that `acquire_desktop` bound its clipboard watcher after letting go of the desk's mutex and then stored it unconditionally. If the session's lease was released, taken back by a stop or ended with its client while the bind was pending, and another session took the lease and bound its own watcher, the late watcher replaced and dropped the new one: the new holder's `clipboard_read` answered `nothing watches the clipboard for this lease` until it released and acquired again. The acquisition also returned success for a lease it no longer held. This predates the merge, in opt-in shared mode; shared mode by default made it reachable across clients. `Engine::watch_clipboard` now keeps a bound watcher only if its session still holds that lease, checked with the watcher slot locked and no await between the check and the store, and `acquire_desktop` fails with `lease_required` otherwise. The fake niri can now hold one display client's connection unanswered (`Compositor::hold_client`), and `engine::a_watch_bound_after_its_lease_ended_leaves_the_next_leases_watch` holds the first session's bind across a release, a disconnect and a stop, lets a second session acquire and bind, then lets the first bind finish. The first acquisition fails, and the second session's clipboard still reads. Storing the watcher unconditionally gives the review's error; dropping only the final check lets the first acquisition succeed.
- Sol's review of these fixes found the new check still sat before an async write. Tokio's `ChildStdin` waits for the reactor to report the pipe writable, so even a small first write into an empty pipe can return `Pending`, and when it resumed it sent the text without another check; Sol reproduced that at the runner. `Gated::feed` now takes the check as `allowed` and asks it in the same poll as each attempt to write the first bytes (`runner::open_gate`, a `poll_fn` around `poll_write`): a poll that finds the pipe not ready sends nothing, and the next poll asks again. Empty input is asked about right before stdin closes. A refusal kills the child's group before its stdin closes, since end of file would let `wtype -` past its gate, and returns `Fed::Refused`, which the keyboard treats as unsent: it clears the marker, and a paste's keeper is told `n`. Once the first bytes went, the rest are written without asking, as the stroke is already under way. `keyboard::a_first_write_that_waits_past_the_last_gate_sends_nothing` polls the feed once, finds its first write `Pending` (a pipe registered in the same poll has no readiness yet), ages the read past the bound and resumes it: the stand-in wtype read nothing, the call is `screen_locked`, and the marker is gone. With a single check before the write, the stand-in got the text.
- The same review found that two `acquire_desktop` calls of one session, for the same lease, each bound a watcher, and the one bound last replaced the first. Its ledger starts from the selection current when it binds, so a copy the first watcher had noted during the lease was then withheld as `copied_before_lease`. With the slot locked, `Engine::watch_clipboard` now keeps a watcher already there for the same lease and drops the new one. Sharing one bind in flight per lease was the other option; keeping the first is smaller and leaves the bind code alone. `engine::a_second_watch_for_the_same_lease_keeps_what_the_first_noted` holds the first call's bind, lets the second bind, copies text, then lets the first finish: the text still reads. Replacing the watcher gives `copied_before_lease`, Sol's result.

## 2026-10-12: fixes from the whole-repository review

- Fable's whole-repository review of main `649cf78` found that the `noctalia` passthrough joined its arguments with spaces and never refused `\x1e` or line breaks, while a unit test proves the fixed commands can't hold them. Noctalia erases everything up to the first `\x1e` before parsing, so an argument carrying one would make the rest of the message the whole command. `noctalia::check_args` now refuses an argument holding `\x1e`, `\n` or `\r` as an argument mistake, with the 1-to-64 count it took over from `tools.rs`, and `act::shell::message` asks it before anything else. `act::noctalia_sends_any_command_only_when_unrestricted` passes each and checks nothing more reached Noctalia.
- It also found that only `CloseWindow` went out naming the window focused at the check. `FullscreenWindow {"id": null}`, `MoveWindowToWorkspace {"window_id": null}` and the rest went out null, so focus moving in the milliseconds before niri handled the request acted on another window, while `observed` reported the first one `unchanged`. Every action with an optional window id now goes out naming the focused window, and with none focused is an argument mistake, as `CloseWindow` was. `compositor::field` replaces `aim`'s match as the one exhaustive match over niri's actions; `aim` and `pin` read it, so a new niri-ipc action is still placed once. `act::niri_action_names_the_window_focused_at_the_check` moves focus while niri handles a null-id fullscreen and checks the next null-id move names the newly focused window.
- And it found that a line break or a tab in `type_text`'s `text` went out as Return or Tab: wtype 0.4's `remap_table` maps them so, and the native keymap did the same on purpose. `{"text": "Hi,\n\nhere is the plan", "submit": true}` into a chat sent "Hi," as its own message and still reported `sent` and `submitted: true`; a tab moved to another field of the same window, which `expect` can't see. `submit` exists so Return never goes out before all of the text did. Typed text holding `\n`, `\r` or `\t` is now an argument mistake from `keyboard::check_text`, for both backends, pointing to `submit`, `key` with `Return` or `Tab`, and `via: "paste"` for text with several lines; a paste sends one key and is unaffected. Typing them as keys with the count saying so was the other option; refusing keeps the one rule that Return goes out only through `submit` or `key`. The native keymap no longer maps them. `act::text_with_a_control_character_is_refused_before_anything_is_typed` runs both backends; without the check the wtype call typed and returned `sent`.
- It also found that a cancelled `acquire_desktop` could keep the lease without its clipboard watcher. The watcher was bound after the grant, outside the desk's mutex, so an MCP cancellation during the bind dropped it while the lease stayed granted. `clipboard_read` then failed with `upstream_error` until the lease was taken again, and a copy made in between read as `copied_before_lease`. `Desk::acquire_with` now takes a `prepare` step, run last under the mutex with the number the lease will have, and `Engine::grant` binds the watcher there and keeps it with no await after the grant. Starting a watcher on the first `clipboard_read` was the other option, but it would withhold whatever the agent copied before that read. The bind now holds the mutex, as the readiness report already did, so another session's `acquire_desktop` waits for it, at most for niri's PID's and the bind's deadlines, before refusing. The watcher's first check of its lease comes as soon as it is bound, before the grant, so it counts a lease still to come as held (`Desk::pending`). A second acquisition by the holder binds nothing, so the same-lease branch from the previous round is gone. The previous round's engine tests are replaced: `engine::an_acquire_dropped_while_its_watch_binds_leaves_no_lease` aborts an acquisition while the fake niri holds its bind, and the next session's copy reads; binding after the grant instead leaves the lease held. `engine::a_lease_ended_while_its_watch_binds_leaves_the_next_lease_a_watch` ends the lease by a release, a disconnect and a stop during the bind. `engine::a_watch_bound_before_its_lease_is_granted_keeps_watching` lets the watcher tick before the grant; without `Desk::pending` its read fails. `engine::acquiring_again_keeps_what_the_leases_watch_noted` covers the holder acquiring again.
- And it found that a slow desktop looked like a locked screen. Every action reads the lock state once more after its readiness report, and that read asked Noctalia, then logind, so a `loginctl` of 150 ms after a Noctalia reply of 120 ms used up the 250 ms bound before the call could go out, and the call failed with `screen_locked`, which the skill tells the agent to stop on. `LockSources::read` now asks both side by side, and the readiness report asks logind beside niri and Noctalia. The bound still runs from when the read began, so the read is as old as its oldest answer. A read that is too old when the call goes out is the new `lock_stale`, which the skill retries once, while `screen_locked` stays for a locked screen, an unknown state, and an action that read no lock state at all. A detail prefix the skill matches on was the other option; a name is the stable part of the contract. `shell::noctalia_and_logind_are_asked_about_the_lock_side_by_side` holds both replies at once, in the report and in the read after it; read in turn, `loginctl` never starts while Noctalia's reply is held. The tests that age the read now expect `lock_stale`. The cost sentence in the 2026-10-08 entry now counts what runs. It hasn't been measured on the real session.

## 2026-10-12: fixes from the whole-repository review, second batch

- Fable's whole-repository review found that the preset rules missed common programs that run whatever command follows them. `tmux`, `screen`, `bwrap`, `firejail`, `unshare`, `chroot`, `ssh`, `flatpak-spawn`, `nix`, `nix-shell`, `strace`, `gdb`, `script`, `docker` and `podman` are now on the list. The rules stay a guardrail: a wrapper script still gets past them.
- The same review found that the audit log's promise of no desktop text rested on agents passing only what the fields asked for. Several tools logged their arguments as given: `key`'s combinations before they were checked, `expect`'s app id, `launch`'s preset, `shell_panel`'s panel, `elements`' role, `screenshot`'s target and save path, `wait_for`'s app id, and the refs and held modifiers of the pointer tools. An agent that copies a window title or page text into one of them put it in the log. The 2026-10-10 reasoning for `niri_action` and `noctalia` applies: a field's syntax can't tell a name from private text. Each string is now logged as it is only when it names something the server knows, and otherwise as a category with its length or count. Key combinations count as known when XKB has every keysym, not when they only parse, since a keysym name's syntax admits any word. A run of single-letter keys could still spell a word, but `key` presses keys rather than types text, and the grader for `make nested-eval` reads the combinations, so they stay. `expect`'s app id keeps a `matched` flag, read from the result's `focus` or a `focus_mismatch`, because whether the agent aimed at the right app is what the log is for. `elements::agent_strings_reach_the_audit_log_only_as_known_names_or_lengths` passes a keysym-shaped marker in every string field of thirteen tools and checks that no line holds it. With keys checked only for syntax, the marker reached the log. The first batch's new outcomes keep the same forms: the marker test also types text refused for a line break or a tab, `act::passthrough_arguments_reach_the_audit_log_only_as_metadata` sends a Noctalia argument refused for its framing, and `audit::a_stale_lock_read_is_logged_by_name_with_bounded_arguments` logs a `lock_stale` refusal by name, with `matched` null and its detail left out.
- The review also found unit tests that depend on the machine's speed. The waiter's check that 200 zero-length waits take under 20 ms now runs only with `NCU_PERF=1` (`make perf`); its other assertions always run. The paused clock was tried first and can't stand in: with the waiter reverted to a timer for every wait, a timer already due fires without moving the paused clock, and the test passed. The native keyboard's overrun test, which bounds a call with two 1.9 s acknowledgements to 3.5 s, stays on real time: the fake compositor delays keys with `std::thread::sleep` in its own thread, over a real socket, so the paused clock would fire the call's deadlines before the fake answered. Driving the fake from tokio's clock would mean rebuilding it. The two keeper tests that sleep 5.5 s are the regression tests for the paste budgets, past the old fixed five-second wait, and a comment now says so; the keeper is a real process, so they stay on real time too.
- The review found that `release_desktop` with `restore_focus` runs the whole action gate, readiness report and lock read included, before it gives the lease up, and suggested skipping the restore once the stop flag is set. That was already so. The stop watcher gives the lease up and forgets `users_window` with it, so the release has no window to focus, and a flag the watcher hasn't acted on yet fails `Desk::act`'s first check, before the readiness report. `act::after_a_stop_release_desktop_skips_the_restore_and_its_readiness_report` now holds that in place: after a stop, the release reads nothing from Noctalia and sends nothing to niri. In the other cases the restore costs what any action's gate costs, which the tool description, the acting page and the architecture document now say.
- Fable's review of this batch at `41eea6e` found that the other control characters still went out as keys: wtype's `remap_table` and XKB press `\u{8}` as BackSpace, `\u{b}` as Clear, `\u{1b}` as Escape and `\u{7f}` as Delete, so a text could erase what it typed or close a dialog. `keyboard::check_text` now refuses every character `char::is_control` names, C0, DEL and C1, with the same argument mistake, and the refusal test types `\u{8}`, `\u{1b}` and `\u{7f}` on both backends as well.
- The same review found that a keysym name can carry any text: XKB resolves `U0053` to S and `0x1000041` to A, and both are alphanumeric, so `keys(["U0053", "U0045", "U0043", "0x1000052"])` was logged as given. `keymap::known_combo` now leaves out names in the `U<hex>` and `0x<hex>` forms and every keysym from 0x1000000 on, where keysyms are code points, so such a list is logged as a count. They are still pressed. Named keysyms in that range, such as `Sinh_a`, are logged as a count too, which is acceptable since they name single characters. `audit::keys_named_by_number_are_logged_as_a_count` spells a word in each form.

## 2026-10-12: the desktop's programs from fixed directories

- `loginctl` and `noctalia` were looked up on the `PATH` of the client that started the engine, so a client with a narrow `PATH`, such as a desktop app, left the lock state to Noctalia alone and hid Noctalia's tools for every client until that engine exited. Every process now looks for both once at startup in `/usr/local/bin:/usr/bin:/bin:/run/current-system/sw/bin`, and runs `loginctl` by the path it found. No client's `PATH` or working directory plays a part. `status.desktop_programs` reports the directories searched and the path found for each, or null, and `status.binaries` lists only `grim` and `wtype`, which come from the client's `PATH`.
- Considered: the systemd user manager's `PATH`, and the `PATH` in niri's `/proc/<pid>/environ`, which the lock read already opens for `XDG_SESSION_ID`. Both describe the desktop better than a client does, but on a usual setup they hold the same system directories, and they add a read that can fail at startup. Most protocol tests also run their fake niri inside the test process, whose environment is the host's, so niri's `PATH` would have the tests find the host's programs. The manager's would mean a D-Bus call or a `systemctl` run before the tool list is fixed.
- `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_DESKTOP_PATH` names other directories, for the test fixtures, the nested harness, and a Noctalia installed elsewhere, such as `~/.local/bin`. Only absolute directories count: a relative one would sit under the working directory, the first client's in an engine it started.
