- Until wtype's feeding task takes it, the marker is held by `Unsent`, whose drop removes it in a cleanup task, as a dropped pointer or native device does. The call's wtype is a later local, so it is dropped first and killed at its gate before the removal starts. `cleared`, which removes the marker of a call refused before anything was typed, now runs its removal in a cleanup task too, since it also waits for the marker's lock.
- Regression test: `input::keyboard::tests::a_call_dropped_at_the_gate_removes_its_marker`, which fails without the drop.

## 2026-10-13: open items for 0.1.0

- The drag follower gap (a window that opens under the pointer during a drag, then grows or moves over the end in the last steps): accepted. Nothing niri reports tells it from a torn-out tab, and it needs a dialog to open mid-drag and move within the last step or two.
- `/proc/<pid>/comm` in the `confirm_lease` prompt: accepted, not added. A process sets its own `comm` (`prctl(PR_SET_NAME)`, or the name of whatever it executes), so it is no more checked than the MCP name, and the prompt already shows the PID, which the server does know.
- Logging a client name that changes within a session: accepted, not added. Every audit line carries the label read from that request's own client info, so a change already shows from one line to the next, and nothing decides on the name.
- The cancel-unsafe window around the input-dirty marker: fixed, see "a wtype call dropped at its gate removes its marker".

## 2026-10-13: fixes after Sol's release review

- Input could go out after the stop flag was set. Before each batch of input (a gesture's first input, the part after each wait, its held modifiers, each step of a held drag, each wtype stroke at its gate, a paste's key once its keeper admits it), the gate checked only the lock read's age through `Desk::fresh`. The stop flag reached the action through the desk's stop watcher, which cancels the running action once inotify tells it, and a flag set while the gate awaited niri's events was not yet seen when the gate let the input through. `Desk::fresh` now reads the stop flag file itself, with nothing awaited between that read and the input, as it already did for the lock read's age. It does not check the input-dirty marker, since gated input has written its own by then. The watcher still cancels an action that is waiting. Input already handed to niri can't be taken back; the check only keeps new input from going out once the flag exists.
- A pointer gesture refused before any input removes its input-dirty marker. When that removal failed, the call returned only the removal's error, so the agent learned that the marker stayed but not why the gesture was refused. `Device::discard` now takes the refusal and returns it with its name and detail kept and the marker's failure and the `recover` advice added, as wtype's `cleared` already did. The native keyboard drops its device on a refusal, which removes the marker in a cleanup task, so its refusal was never replaced.
- A held drag to an element went on when the element's floating window became hidden: a tiled window as large as the output became the active window of its workspace, or the active one grew to that size. niri then draws none of the workspace's floating windows, so the release lands on the tiled window. The look before the drag already refused that case through the placement's fullscreen test; the looks within the drag didn't repeat it, since that window is tiled and isn't relaid over the element, and a change of active window stops a drag only before its press. The placement's test is now `model::hides_floating`, and `End::lost` asks it at every look about the active window of the element's workspace, so the drag stops, releases its button and ends `uncertain`. It is asked about the output the screenshot showed, where the element was placed.
- `activate_element` sent `DoAction` the action's place in `GetActions`' list. In GTK 4 that list leaves out disabled actions while `GetName` and `DoAction` count them (`action_handle_method` in `gtk/a11y/gtkatspiaction.c`, GTK main as of 2026-10-13, for the button, switch, expander, colour swatch and entry tables; `NActions` counts only the enabled ones there too, so it can't tell either). So when an action listed after a disabled one was asked for, a protocol mock modelling that sent another action's index. GTK 4's widget actions filter `GetName` and `DoAction` the same way as the list and were never affected. The server now finds the index before the last gate. It keeps the action's place when the app names the action there, by `GetName` or by `GetLocalizedName`. `GetLocalizedName` is asked because GTK and at-spi2-atk list translated names, and a translated desktop must keep working. Otherwise it asks indices from 0 until the app answers with an error, at most 32, for the one whose action has the listed name. No such index, or several, refuses the call with `element_unmappable` and `action_unmapped`, with nothing sent. The last gate asks again that the app still names the action at the index found. That costs a `GetName` before the last gate and one in it, twice that on a translated desktop, and a search only when the list and the indices disagree. Mapping by `NActions` and position was the other option; GTK's `NActions` counts the filtered list, so it can't show the gap. This was found by Sol's release review with a mock; no GTK app with a disabled leading action was run.

## 2026-10-13: the action index after Sol's re-review

- Sol's re-review found two gaps in the action index mapping above, both with protocol mocks. An index was taken when either `GetName` or `GetLocalizedName` gave the listed name there, so a translated name that matched another action's `GetName` ran that other action. And a `GetName` error was read as the end of the actions before `GetLocalizedName` was asked, so an app that refuses `GetName` but answers `GetLocalizedName` got `action_unmapped`, where it worked before that fix.
- Which method's names `GetActions` lists, read in the toolkit sources on 2026-10-13:
  - GTK 4, `action_handle_method` in `gtk/a11y/gtkatspiaction.c` (main): `GetActions` lists `g_dpgettext2 (…, action->localized_name)` for each enabled action, the string `GetLocalizedName` returns; `GetName` returns the untranslated `name`. Both answer `G_IO_ERROR_INVALID_ARGUMENT` past the last action. Widget actions (`widget_handle_method`) give one name to all three methods.
  - at-spi2-atk, `impl_GetActions` in `atk-adaptor/adaptors/action-adaptor.c` (at-spi2-core main), which GTK 3 and Chromium go through: lists `atk_action_get_localized_name`. `GetName` and `GetLocalizedName` answer `""` for a missing name, past the last action too, and never answer with an error.
  - Qt 6, `AtSpiAdaptor::actionInterface` and `AtSpiAdaptor::getActions` in `src/gui/accessible/linux/atspiadaptor.cpp` (qtbase dev): lists `effectiveActionNames`, the names `GetName` gives. It has no `GetLocalizedName` branch, so QtDBus answers that call with an error, as it does `GetName` past the last action. The error is `org.freedesktop.DBus.Error.UnknownObject` (`QDBusConnectionPrivate::activateObject` in `src/dbus/qdbusintegrator.cpp`), which the server otherwise reads as the object being gone; `make nested-a11y` showed it on the Qt fixture. So an error from a naming method, that one included, only means the app doesn't name the action that way. An object really gone shows in the list read before the names and in the last gate.
  - Chromium, `atk_action::GetLocalizedName` and `atk_action::GetName` in `ui/accessibility/platform/ax_platform_node_auralinux.cc` (main): the localized name is null for Blink's own actions, so they are listed empty, and is the author's name for `aria-actions`. `GetName` gives Blink's names, such as `click`.
- So a name the list holds is compared only with what `GetLocalizedName` gives at each index, or, in an app that refuses `GetLocalizedName` at every index as Qt does, with what `GetName` gives. When some indices answer `GetLocalizedName` and others refuse it, the names would come from both methods, and the call is refused. A name the list leaves empty, which `elements` filled from `GetName`, is compared only with `GetName`. An index whose name is unknown, because the app refuses the method that names it, refuses the call too, since it might have the same name. All of these refusals are `element_unmappable` with `action_unmapped`, with nothing sent.
- The listed place no longer wins when the app names the action there: the server asks both names at every index from 0 and needs exactly one index with the listed name. It stops past the listed actions at the first index where neither method names an action, both answering with an error (GTK 4, Qt) or an empty name (at-spi2-atk). Inside the list, an error from both is not the end; that index's name is unknown. More than 32 actions refuses the call, as before. Actions that change during the search can look like several indices with the name, so when the search finds none the server reads the list again, and an action no longer at its place is `element_stale`, not `action_unmapped`; the stall explorer's reordered-actions case found this. The last gate asks both names at the index found again, and needs the same name from the same method and the list's entry from the same source.
- What this leaves open. An app that leaves actions out of its list and, past the listed ones, has an action neither method names, followed by another with the listed name, is mapped as if its actions ended at the nameless one. None of the toolkits above does that. An app that lists translated names but refuses `GetLocalizedName` everywhere is matched by `GetName`, as Qt is; nothing on the bus tells the two apart. And an action listed unnamed is named by `GetName` at its place in the list, which in an app that also leaves actions out of the list can be another action's index; at-spi2-atk, where empty names come from, lists every action.
- Cost: two calls per action and two more for the end before the last gate, and two in it. A GTK 4 button with one action asks four names before the gate, where the earlier fix asked one or two.

## 2026-10-13: the last gate searches the action index again

- Opus's release review found that the last gate accepted a table the search refuses. It checked the list entry and the name at the index found, but not that the name was still unique. An app that gave a second index the listed name between the search and the gate had the first index run, though the search would now give `action_unmapped`. The review's mock: GTK 4's shape, `open` disabled and unlisted, `toggle` listed and found at index 1, then index 0 renamed `toggle`; `DoAction(1)` went out with `accepted: true`. No toolkit read for the entry above renames actions like that.
- The last gate now runs the whole search again (`search` in `elements/actions.rs`) and needs the same index, source and name; anything else is `element_stale`, with nothing sent. The other option was to record the case as accepted, beside the three residuals above. The search is the rule the index comes from, so the gate asking that rule again leaves no table it accepts that the search refuses.
- Cost: `2·(n+1)` naming calls in the gate for `n` actions, where it asked two. A GTK 4 button with one action asks four. Measured with `make nested-a11y`, 87 gated element actions per run, none ran a second pass and none was `element_busy`:

  | Server | Pass ms, median | p95 | max | Share of 250 ms |
  |---|---|---|---|---|
  | main, idle | 2.6 | 3.2 | 25.5 | 10.2% |
  | this change, idle | 2.6 | 4.9 | 13.1 | 5.2% |
  | this change, idle | 2.8 | 9.1 | 14.0 | 5.6% |
  | this change, eight busy loops on the four cores the run was pinned to | 6.6 | 11.1 | 22.5 | 9.0% |

  The extra calls cost a millisecond or two at the p95. The longest pass varies more between runs of one server than between the two.
- `elements::a_name_another_index_took_before_the_last_gate_is_never_run` is the review's reproduction: it went out with `accepted: true` before the change and gets `element_stale` with nothing sent now, in both protocol modes.

## 2026-10-13: a lock read the stall explorer can't reach

- Main's CI run 38346152411 failed `stall::a_marker_set_while_a_reply_is_held`: `type_text` held at `noctalia:status` #3, Short and Long, never reached, with the call succeeding. Idle, `type_text` reads the lock twice before its strokes, in the readiness report and once after it. A third read comes only from `act::fresh_lock` at a stroke, when the last read began over `LAST_GATE` before it. The world's two baseline calls had both been that slow, and the six case runs after them, three per hold, weren't. Trying each case three times ("the lock read's age before every call that acts") can't help when the machine stays fast.
- Reproduced deterministically with a temporary change that held the baseline's first `wtype` stroke for 300 ms and nothing in the case runs: the same two cases failed with the same message. CPU load alone didn't reproduce it. Three runs of the full matrix pinned to 4 cores with 8 busy loops, and eight runs of the marker test on 2 cores with 12 busy loops stopped and resumed at random, all passed, since a stroke still came well inside 250 ms of the last read.
- A case whose point no run reached is now no break when all of this holds. The point is a lock read, `noctalia:status`, or `loginctl` where niri runs in a session. The call succeeded and acted as many times as with nothing held. Each call that acts went out within `LAST_GATE` of the lock read before it in that run. The last condition keeps a real break visible: a server that should have read the lock again and acted on the old read instead fails as before, with the read's age in the message. Any other point left unreached still fails. A reached case is judged as before.
- `stall::only_a_lock_read_a_call_had_no_need_to_make_goes_unreached` checks the rule on made-up traces: the missed third read with strokes within 12 ms passes, and fails with the last stroke 300 ms after the read or with the point being `niri:Version`. With the temporary change applied, `stall::a_marker_set_while_a_reply_is_held` passes in both modes.
- A read's age counts from its first answer. A read asks Noctalia and logind side by side, so in the session worlds one read passes both points; taking the later answer as the read's time let a logind reply 300 ms after Noctalia's make a stale read look fresh (Sol's review after the release candidate). A point from a source that already answered starts the next read. `stall::a_late_second_lock_source_doesnt_make_the_read_younger` is that trace.

## 2026-10-14: every action pinned to a commit SHA

- A tag can be moved to other code by whoever controls the action's repository; a commit SHA can't. The release workflow builds the tarball users install and `draft` holds a token that can write releases, so the code each action runs is fixed. Fable's release-path review raised it.
- Each `uses:` names the commit and keeps the version as a comment, which Dependabot reads and updates with the SHA, still after its 7-day cooldown. The repository's Actions settings require SHA pins (`sha_pinning_required`), so a tag or branch pin fails the run instead of slipping in.

  `setup-node` v7.1.0 (2026-10-08), `install-action` v2.87.27 (2026-10-09), `upload-artifact` v7.0.2 and `download-artifact` v8.0.2 (both 2026-10-07) are under 7 days old and fix no security issue.

## 2026-10-14: rmcp 3.5.1

| Crate | Version | Published | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `rmcp` | 3.5.1 | 2026-10-05 | The newest release at least 7 days old, which the version rule asks for. `rmcp-macros` moves with it (3.5.1, 2026-10-05); no other crate in `Cargo.lock` changes. |

- What 3.5.1 changes, from its release notes and pull requests. A session that starts with `server/discover`, as Claude Code 2.1.296's do, picks its lifecycle from the first ordinary request rather than from `discover` itself (#1248). `-32602` errors map to HTTP 400 only when required `_meta` is missing (#1322); the server speaks stdio, so this doesn't reach it. Results to 2026-07-28 peers get `ttlMs` and `cacheScope` when the handler left them out (#1308); `#[tool_handler]` already sets them on `tools/list`, and the server has no resource or prompt handlers. The other two entries are the `object!` macro without the `macros` feature and OAuth credential stores, neither of which the server uses.
- `audit::a_client_named_only_in_each_requests_meta_is_logged_by_that_name` sends `server/discover` and then `tools/call` without `initialize`, the shape #1248 changes, and passes on 3.5.1.

## 2026-10-14: CI caches cargo's builds

- `Swatinem/rust-cache` restores `~/.cargo` and `target/` in the CI jobs that compile, so a run builds only this repository's crates, not every dependency. It keys the cache on the Rust toolchain, every `Cargo.toml` and `Cargo.lock`, and the job's key. It also sets `CARGO_INCREMENTAL=0`, which CI builds don't benefit from.
- Only runs on `main` save a cache (`save-if`). Branches, pull requests and tags restore `main`'s, so their pushes can't evict it from the repository's 10 GB of cache. GitHub already keeps a branch's caches from runs on `main`.
- `cache-bin: false` leaves `~/.cargo/bin` out, so the gate tools always come from `taiki-e/install-action`, never from a cache.
- The release workflow's `build` job has no cache and stays that way: the binary users download is built from a clean checkout and the registry alone, so a poisoned cache can't reach it. Only CI's own jobs read a cache.

| Action | Version | Published | Commit |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Swatinem/rust-cache` | v2.9.2 | 2026-08-06 | `6323deb102c322ba6fcbdcafc7e3dddab59af2b6` |

v2.9.2 is the newest release; it is over 7 days old.

## 2026-10-14: CI runs `make check` and `make stall` in parallel parts

- The protocol suite takes about two minutes in each mode, and the stall explorer's full matrix the same, so CI ran four two-minute suites in two jobs. `make check` is now `check-static`, `test` and `test-shared`, and `make stall` is `stall-standalone` and `stall-shared`. CI runs each as a job of its own, and each job calls its make target, so CI and `make check` still run the same commands.
- `test` saves the test build's cache; `test-shared` and the two stall jobs build a subset of it and only restore it. `check-static` keeps a cache of its own, since Clippy and rustdoc build check metadata rather than test binaries.

## 2026-10-14: a newer push cancels CI's older run

- CI has a concurrency group per ref with `cancel-in-progress`, so a push to a branch or pull request cancels the run of the push before it, which nobody reads any more.
- A called workflow's `github` context is its caller's, so when `release.yml` calls CI, `github.workflow` is `Release` and `github.ref` is the tag. CI keys on that: under `Release`, its group is the release run's ID and it cancels nothing. A release's gate can't be cancelled by a push, and doesn't wait behind one.
- Checked on a throwaway branch with a second workflow that calls CI as `release.yml` does: a second push cancelled CI's run of the first, and both calling workflows' runs went on to pass.

## 2026-10-14: cargo-about gets every platform's crates

- The release's Package step failed in a dry run on GitHub's runners: `cargo about generate --frozen` stopped with "failed to download `android_system_properties v0.1.6` … --frozen was specified". cargo-about reads `cargo metadata` for every platform before it filters by target, and the build had fetched only the crates its own target needs. The earlier checks of the step (the release entries above, Fable's review, the first release branch) ran with a local cargo cache that already held every crate, so they passed. The same failure reproduces locally with an empty `CARGO_HOME` after `cargo fetch --locked --target x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, whether the target comes from `about.toml` or from `--target`.
- Package now runs `cargo fetch --locked` before cargo-about: 137 crates, 15 MB, against the 114 the x86_64 build fetches. cargo-about still runs `--frozen`, so it reads only crates the lock names. The license list it writes is unchanged.

## 2026-10-14: the landing page's text face is cut to its characters

| Package | Version | Published | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| `subset-font` | 2.9.0 | 2026-09-20 | A build step, `site/scripts/subset-font.mjs`, cuts Archivo down to the characters the site's text uses. BSD-3-Clause, development only. |

- The redesigned home page sets its headline in Archivo's narrow widths, which need Fontsource's width-axis file: 88 KB of WOFF2 for Latin, where the weight-only file was 35 KB. Lighthouse's simulated LCP for the headline went from 1.73 s to 2.10–2.40 s, over the site's 1.8 s budget. Cut to printable ASCII and the punctuation the copy uses, and to widths from 62% to 100%, the file is 37 KB, and LCP measured 1.726, 1.726 and 1.727 s.
- The cut face is declared after Fontsource's faces under the same name and the same weight and width ranges, so any other character falls back to Fontsource's full face, which a page downloads only when it has such a character. Chrome downloaded both faces when the ranges differed.
- It brings in `harfbuzzjs` 1.6.3 (2026-10-06), `fontverter` 2.0.0, `wawoff2` 2.0.1, `woff2sfnt-sfnt2woff` 1.0.0, `pako` 1.0.11, `p-limit` 3.1.0 and `yocto-queue` 0.1.0, all MIT, BSD-3-Clause or zlib, all older than seven days. The output goes to `site/src/generated/`, which git ignores; `npm run dev` and `npm run build` write it first.

## 2026-10-14: aarch64 release builds

- The release builds `aarch64-unknown-linux-gnu` beside `x86_64-unknown-linux-gnu`, each on a GitHub runner of its own architecture: `ubuntu-24.04` and `ubuntu-24.04-arm`. GitHub made its arm64 Linux runners generally available to public repositories on 2025-08-07, at no cost, with the labels `ubuntu-24.04-arm` and `ubuntu-22.04-arm` ("arm64 hosted runners for public repositories are now generally available", GitHub changelog). The `ubuntu-24.04-arm` image (20261004.142.1 in `actions/runner-images`) has rustup and Rust 1.99.0, so `rust-toolchain.toml` applies as on x86_64. A native build needs no cross linker or foreign libxkbcommon, and its tests run on the architecture they ship for.
- `ci.yml` runs `make test` and `make test-shared`, the tests of `make check` in both server modes, on `ubuntu-24.04-arm` in one job of its own on every push. It restores and, on `main`, saves a build cache of its own (`shared-key: test-aarch64`), as the other test jobs do, since an aarch64 build can't use theirs. The release runs `ci.yml` before `build`, so the aarch64 tarball is built only after the tests passed on aarch64, and a failure there shows on the commit that caused it rather than on tag day. The rest of `make check` (formatting, Clippy, rustdoc, cargo-deny, cargo-machete, typos, ShellCheck) doesn't depend on the architecture and stays on x86_64, as does `make stall`'s full matrix; the aarch64 tests include the stall explorer's default run, one tool of each kind.
- Each build job checks that its stripped binary prints its version before packing it, and writes `THIRD-PARTY-LICENSES.txt` for its own target with `cargo about generate --target`. For 0.1.0 the two lists are the same byte for byte, since no dependency is specific to either architecture.
- The build jobs stay uncached, with `contents: read` and `persist-credentials: false`. Each uploads its tarball and `.sha256` as the artifact `release-<arch>`, and the x86_64 job also uploads `notes.md` as `release-notes`. `draft` downloads every `release-*` artifact into one directory and attaches both tarballs and their checksums to the one draft. No action was added: `download-artifact`'s `pattern` and `merge-multiple` inputs are in the pinned v8.0.1.
- Both binaries need glibc 2.39 or newer (`objdump -T`) and link `libxkbcommon.so.0`, `libgcc_s.so.1`, `libm.so.6` and `libc.so.6` (`ldd`), checked on the dry run's binaries on each runner.
- The workflow ran as a dry run on a throwaway branch (`tmp-release-dryrun`, run 38501942377, since deleted): this release workflow with a branch trigger in place of the tag, without `verify`, `ci` and `draft`, and a job on each runner that took the artifacts as `draft` does, checked them and installed them. Both workflows pass actionlint 1.7.12 (2026-03-30, the newest release), the release binary this time rather than `actionlint-py`.

## 2026-10-14: the install script

- `install.sh` does the README's manual steps for the machine it runs on, and nothing else: no `sudo`, and it writes only to the binary's directory (`~/.local/bin`, or `NCU_BIN_DIR`), `~/.local/share/niri-computer-use` and the two skill links. It is POSIX sh, so it runs under `dash`, Ubuntu's `/bin/sh`, as in CI and the dry run, and under bash.
- It finds the latest release by following the redirect of `releases/latest` to the release's page, not through the REST API, which allows 60 requests an hour without a token. Each download has a 15-second connect and a 300-second total deadline.
- It runs the downloaded binary's `--version` before installing it, so a system whose glibc is too old gets the loader's error and keeps what it had. The binary is copied to a temporary name beside the old one and renamed over it, so a running server keeps the file it started from. Running it again replaces the skill directory, so files removed upstream go, and the links it made; a skill path that isn't a link is left alone.
- The checksum catches a broken or cut-off download, not a tampered release: the `.sha256` comes from the same place as the tarball.
- The release attaches it as a fifth file: the x86_64 build job puts it in `release-notes` beside `notes.md`. `releases/latest/download/install.sh` then always names the latest release's copy.
- `tests/install.rs` runs the script against fake releases that a thread of the test serves over HTTP, with a fake `uname` and the test's own directory as `HOME` and `TMPDIR`. Each of eight changes to the script (skipping the checksum, a wrong latest version, keeping the `v` of `NCU_VERSION`, not replacing the skill, accepting any machine, leaving the temporary directory, linking for one agent only, ignoring `NCU_BIN_DIR`) failed at least one of its five tests.
- `tests/` stays out of the crate, so `cargo publish` now also warns that it ignores the `install` test, as it does for `protocol`.

## 2026-10-14: the install script and release workflow after Fable's review

- `install.sh` copies the new binary and the new skill beside the old ones (`.niri-computer-use.new` in each directory) before replacing either, then renames each into place. Before, the binary went in first and the skill was removed and copied after it, so a copy that failed there left a new binary with no skill and both agent links dangling. A failed copy now names the two directories and says the previous install is unchanged; the exit trap removes whatever was staged.
- It resolves the binary's directory to an absolute path, and the steps it prints quote it, so they work with a `HOME` that has a space and with a relative `NCU_BIN_DIR`, which niri's `spawn` would look up from its own working directory.
- It can't tell a link it made from one the user made, so it replaces any link at the two skill paths and prints the old target when that wasn't the installed skill. The README and the install page say so: the earlier README told users to link those paths to a clone.
- When `--version` of the downloaded binary fails, the message gives the exit status and names both causes seen: a glibc older than 2.39, and a `TMPDIR` mounted `noexec`.
- The README and the install page say what the `.sha256` is for: a download that is broken or cut off. Whoever can change the release's files can change the tarball, its checksum and the script together, since all three come from the release. Signed build provenance (`actions/attest-build-provenance`, checked with `gh attestation verify`) would cover that, and is left for a later release.
- The manual update now removes `~/.local/share/niri-computer-use/skills` before copying the new skill, as the script does, since `cp -r` into the old directory keeps files the new release dropped.
- `tests/install.rs` gained five tests for paths no test reached: a binary whose `--version` fails, a release without its `.sha256`, a failed skill copy after a first install, a link and a directory the user made at the skill paths, and a link at the binary's path, which is replaced by a file while its target stays. The test home's name has a space, and one test passes a relative `NCU_BIN_DIR`. Each of seven changes to the script (the old install order, a silent link replacement, a relative binary path, an unquoted path in the printed steps, copying through a link at the binary's path, replacing a user's directory, ignoring a failed `--version`) failed the test written for it, and the unchanged script passed all ten.
- `build` has `timeout-minutes: 30`; the dry run's builds took about three and a half minutes. `draft`'s two steps have 5 minutes each rather than the job: the job waits for the environment's approval, and GitHub's documentation doesn't say whether a job's timeout counts that wait, while a step's timeout counts only its own run.
- `.github/release/about.toml` lists both targets. The workflow passes `--target` for each build, so the list only decides what a local run without `--target` writes; with both, that is the same list as each build's.
- From source, `cargo install` runs inside the clone (`cd niri-computer-use`, `--path .`): rustup takes `rust-toolchain.toml` from the current directory, not from `--path`, so the README's `cargo install --locked --path niri-computer-use` from the clone's parent built with the default toolchain. `rustup show active-toolchain` gives `stable` outside the clone and `1.99.0` inside it. Sol found this on the site.

## 2026-10-14: wtype loses spaces, punctuation and emoji in Discord

- A Claude Code session typed two Discord messages into Vesktop with the default wtype backend. One arrived with every space missing from its second 100-character part, the other with `probably.` cut to `probabl` and its emoji shown as a CJK character. Reproduced in the same window, with no suggestion list open, so the 2026-10-13 entry understated the limit: it isn't only VS Code, and not only with a binding active.
- The cause is the one recorded there. wtype gives the `n`th distinct character of a call evdev code `n`, and Chromium takes the DOM `code` from the evdev code. A non-letter on code 1 (Escape) is dropped every time it occurs in that part: a part beginning with a space, as the second part of a message did, loses all its spaces, and `. a. b, c` arrived as ` a b, c`. A non-letter on code 14 (Backspace) deletes the character before it: `abcdefghijklm.n` arrived as `abcdefghijkln`, and in the message the period, its fourteenth distinct character, deleted the `y` before it. Letters on those codes arrive as themselves. Code 28 is Enter and wasn't tried against a message box.
- Characters outside the Basic Multilingual Plane arrive cut to 16 bits with either backend: `🤖` (U+1F916) as U+F916 and `🌙` (U+1F319) as U+F319, a private-use box. That is Chromium's handling of the keysym, not the keymap, so only `via: "paste"` sends an emoji there.
- The native backend typed the same three probes into the same box exactly, emoji aside, since it uses the layout's own codes. Whether it becomes the default is still the separate decision of 2026-10-13; until then the skill and the `via` description tell the agent to paste in Chromium and Electron apps rather than type.

## 2026-10-14: the install script registers the server through the agents' own commands

- The step people get wrong after installing is the `mcp add` with the binary's full path. The script now runs that command itself for each of `claude`, `pi` and `codex` it finds on `PATH`, using exactly the commands the client setup page gives, so it still edits no config file of any agent, which keeps the plug-and-play rule: the agent's own CLI owns its config.
- It asks per agent, reading the answer from `/dev/tty`, because `curl | sh` leaves stdin as the pipe. It asks only when stdout is a terminal; otherwise, and with `NCU_REGISTER=0`, it registers in none and prints the commands as before, so a scripted install changes nothing it wasn't told to. `NCU_REGISTER=1` is the unattended yes. Any other value is refused before anything is registered.
- Claude Code's `mcp add` exits non-zero when the name exists, so the script asks `claude mcp get` first and reports an existing registration rather than an error. Pi's and Codex's are tried as they are; a refusal is reported with the agent's last output line and the command is listed for the user, and the install still succeeds.
- The stop-key binding stays manual: that is an edit to the user's niri config, which the script never touches.
- Tested in `tests/install.rs` with fake agent programs that record their arguments: registration in every agent found, an existing registration left alone, a refusing agent, no terminal and `NCU_REGISTER=0`, and a bad value. The prompt itself isn't under test, since the tests have no terminal.

## 2026-10-14: the native keyboard is the default

- With `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_KEYBOARD` unset, the server now types through its own virtual keyboard. `wtype` selects wtype, `native` is still accepted, and any other value still refuses input. The reason is the Discord evidence above: with wtype, a 100-character part that began with a space lost every space, a period on wtype's fourteenth code deleted the letter before it, and VS Code's `ctrl+s` arrived as Ctrl+Escape. The native backend typed the same probes into the same Vesktop box exactly, because it uses the layout's own keycodes. Chromium and Electron apps are where agents type messages, so the old default failed where it matters most.
- The 2026-10-09 entry named the blockers for making native the default. Arbitrary Unicode is solved by spare keys (2026-10-10) and now by splitting: text with more distinct missing symbols than the map has spare keycodes, 14 in a US layout, goes out in parts, each with its own extension of the compositor's map, uploaded right before the part's first key, after that key's checks. Between parts the next extension goes up directly, and the compositor's map is restored once at the end. Every key goes through the same checks, so interruption, stop, the counts, `may_have_sent` and the marker work the same across parts. A call that ends between parts never uploads the next map. `keymap.rs` does the splitting, still without I/O. The nested suite types 30 missing symbols and sees three extensions, then the compositor's map byte for byte. Releasing input after a crash is solved by the crash guardian (2026-10-10) and, when the guardian dies with its server, the next server's guardian.
- A `key` combination, a paste's key included, goes out in the active layout when it has the keysym, and otherwise in the first other layout of the compositor's map that has it, with that layout's level modifiers. wtype brought its own map, so `ctrl+v` pasted whatever layout was active; looking only in the active layout would have made `key ["ctrl+s"]` and `via: "paste"` refuse with `us,ru` and Russian active. The zero modifiers after each key go out in the active layout, so the focused client is back in it before the next combination, and niri's active layout stays the user's. niri's active layout doesn't follow a virtual keyboard's group, so such a key doesn't end the call as a layout change. The nested suite measured it with `us,ru`: ``M7 native key from another layout: ctrl+a with the Russian layout active went out as a with Control in wev, which was left in the Russian layout, and niri's active layout stayed Russian``. A combination none of the layouts has is still refused before anything is pressed.
- Held physical modifiers and locks are accepted as a documented limit (2026-10-12). Shift held, Caps Lock on or Num Lock on doesn't change what a call types. Every native key and the restore at the end send zero modifiers and no lock, and niri sends the seat's again only when a modifier or lock key changes, the keymap changes or focus moves. So a lock that was on before a call reads as off in the focused application after every call, with nobody touching the keyboard: letters come out lowercase with Caps Lock on, and keypad digits move the cursor with Num Lock on. A held modifier is lost the same way, which needs someone holding it while a call types. The nested suite measured it in a Qt field, through a virtual keyboard niri handles as a physical one: `` M7 held modifiers in the Qt field: `abc` typed exactly with Shift held, with Caps Lock on and with Num Lock on by both backends; `a` by hand afterwards typed `A` after wtype and `a` after native; with Num Lock on, keypad `1` then `a` typed `1a` after wtype and `a` after native ``. The limit stays accepted: a lock reading as off until the next modifier or focus change is a smaller failure than lost spaces in a sent message, and nothing the server can read tells it which locks the user had on.
- Characters outside the Basic Multilingual Plane still arrive cut to 16 bits in Chromium with either backend, so emoji there still need `via: "paste"`. The server doesn't refuse them. It doesn't know a window's toolkit: `elements::identify` only checks an element's identity, and finding the toolkit would mean an AT-SPI call on every `type_text`.
- Measured by the owner on the real desktop, typing into Vesktop (Discord) with this branch's release build and the default backend: text the layout has arrives exactly, the two Discord probes that wtype broke included. Symbols the layout lacks don't: Chromium drops a key whose evdev code has no DOM code, and of the US map's 14 spare keys (AB11, JPCM, I120, AE13, I149, I154, I168, I178, I183, I184, I219, I222, I230, I248) only AB11 (evdev 89, `KEY_RO`) and AE13 (evdev 124, `KEY_YEN`) were delivered. `é` alone and `café ok` arrived; `ab é ab ß ab → ab` arrived as `ab é ab  ab  ab`; 14 Greek letters arrived as `αδ`. So in Chromium and Electron apps a native call types at most two distinct symbols the layout lacks, and the rest are lost while `observed` says `sent`. The skill and the keyboard page tell agents to paste such text. Choosing spare keys Chromium delivers is left for the next change; this one keeps the spare-key choice as it is. Superseded by "spare keys Chromium knows" below, which puts missing symbols on keys Chromium delivers; the numbers here are the measurement from before it.
- The nested harness doesn't set `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_KEYBOARD` in its session, so every target runs the default. Only the servers that are the wtype baseline start with `wtype`: the M4 keyboard and crash steps, the wtype C10 comparison, the engine step that expects wtype to finish after a stop, and the wtype rounds of the held-modifier, GTK entry and dropped-paste checks. The native checks start theirs with `native`.
- Left for a later change: removing wtype, meaning its code, `status.binaries`' check for it, `recover`'s handling of a running `wtype`, the harness baseline and the docs. Until then wtype is still a run-time dependency.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: spare keys Chromium knows

- Chromium maps a key's evdev code to a DOM code with `ui/events/keycodes/dom/dom_code_data.inc` and drops a key with none. `keymap.rs` keeps the evdev codes up to 247, keycode 255, that the table gives a code string, as 22 ranges, taken from chromium/chromium `d39a16bacc2469d5d2753160b6cf1a2e666fc069` (main on 2026-10-14; the file last changed in `863ae6f44a6d93d0aaa3674b0554f4a9298d5fd1`, 2025-01-03). Rows with a null code string, such as `LOCK_SCREEN` or `KBD_ILLUM_UP`, are left out: they name system actions, and nothing was measured on them.
- Keys without symbols that Chromium knows now go first. On a US map (`xkbcli compile-keymap --layout us`, xkbcli 1.13.2) that leaves `<AB11>` and `<AE13>`, the two keys Vesktop delivered, and none of the other twelve.
- So a map may also borrow F13 to F24 (evdev 183 to 194, DOM codes `F13` to `F24`), once the keys without symbols that Chromium knows are used up. Maps define them, with `XF86Tools`, `XF86Launch5` and other XF86 symbols on a US map, and Chromium knows them. A borrowed key's entry is `replace key`, so it loses its other levels and type, and the check that every other key is unchanged now lets an added key have had symbols only if it is one of these twelve. A text with one or two missing symbols on a US map still uses only `<AB11>` and `<AE13>` and changes no key that has symbols. The US order is `<AB11>`, `<AE13>`, `<FK13>` to `<FK24>`: still 14 keys a map, all of them ones Chromium knows.
- A physical F13 to F24 press during a call still types its own symbol, not the borrowed one. niri's Smithay sends the seat's keymap, the compositor's, to clients before forwarding a physical key whenever another map is active (`KeyboardInnerHandle::input` in `src/input/keyboard/mod.rs` at `ff5fa7df`), and a virtual keyboard's map is its own (`virtual_keyboard_handle.rs`), so the device's next key sends the extension again, as with any physical key during a call. This was first read from the source. The nested suite now measures it, pressing F13 on a virtual keyboard on cage, which niri handles as a physical one, while the serving process is stopped mid-call: ``M7 physical F13 mid-call: pressed after 1 of 30 characters of an extension that borrows <FK13>, wev decoded XF86Tools for it, niri sent the compositor's keymap before it, and the call still typed all 30 exactly and ended with the compositor's keymap byte for byte (6 keymaps)``. What clients see is the borrowed symbol on those keycodes while the extension is the active map, which only the device's keys use. A key `recover` or the crash guardian releases goes out with the compositor's map, so a borrowed key's release reaches clients as a release of its own symbol, a release only, like the other spare keys'.
- Measured by the owner on the real desktop, typing into Vesktop with this change's release build and the default backend on a US layout, each read back from a screenshot and each exact: `ab é ab ß ab → ab`; `a αβγδεζηθικλμν ok`, 13 missing symbols on `<AB11>`, `<AE13>` and `<FK13>` to `<FK23>`; `αβγδεζηθικλμνξοπρστυφχψω ok ←↑→↓ done`, 28 in two parts; `café naïve façade Ærø`; `Привет, мир`. So Chromium delivers F13 to F24, and the keyboard page, the skill and the changelog no longer tell agents to paste such text. Only characters outside the Basic Multilingual Plane still need `via: "paste"` there.
- In Chromium a borrowed key arrives with the DOM `code` of its F key, and for a non-ASCII character likely with `keyCode` `VK_F13` to `VK_F24` too, which Chromium derives from the code when the keysym gives none. So a shortcut the app has on F13 to F24, such as Discord push-to-talk on F13 or a VS Code user keybinding, can run while the symbol types. This wasn't measured. The owner accepted it: it needs such a binding and a third distinct missing symbol in one keymap, and it is rarer and smaller than losing every missing symbol after the second. The keyboard page and the skill tell a user with such a binding to paste.
- Keys without symbols that Chromium doesn't know are used only by a map that has neither kind above. Taking them after the borrowed keys would give a US map 26 keys, and in a Chromium window every symbol past the fourteenth would be lost; leaving them out costs other applications one more keymap upload per 14 symbols.
- A map that puts a modifier on one of F13 to F24 makes the borrowed key change a modifier, so the check refuses the call and nothing is typed. The symbols files of xkeyboard-config 2.48 put no modifier on them.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: wtype is removed

- The native keyboard is now the only keyboard. Removed: the wtype path in `input/keyboard.rs`, the runner's stdin gate (`runner::gated`, `Gated`, `feed`, `Fed`, `open_gate`), the process helpers only wtype used (`procs::named`, `alive_as`, `real_uid`), `recover`'s ending of a marker's input child and its offer to end the user's `wtype` processes, the marker's `child` field and `running` phase, and `marker::Written::update`. `keyboard.rs` keeps the argument checks, `expect` and what a call reports; `type_input_then` calls `native::type_input`.
- `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_KEYBOARD` is no longer read, and any value is ignored rather than refused. 0.1.0's docs told users to set it to `native`, and refusing would break exactly those setups. A `wtype` setting now gets the native keyboard, which typed Discord and VS Code exactly where wtype didn't (see the two entries above).
- `status.binaries` lists only `grim` and `notify-send`; `wtype` is no longer a run-time dependency.
- `recover` releases the native keys and pointer buttons the marker names; a marker that names none has nothing to release. Either way it then asks for the manual check and clears the marker after `yes`.
- A marker 0.1.0 wrote while `wtype` ran has a `child` field, or the `running` phase, and the marker still denies unknown fields, so this version reads it as unreadable. That blocks input until `recover` as before, and `recover` then does only the human check, which is what a dead wtype child needs.
- The shared engine's hello lost its `keyboard` field and its version went from 2 to 3. A bridge and an engine of different builds already refuse each other by the binary's identity; the version marks the format change.
- The protocol tests' fakes serve no virtual keyboard, so the tests that drove a stand-in wtype were moved to unit tests against the fake compositor in `src/test_support/wayland.rs`, rewritten around a held niri action, or dropped where they only tested wtype. The stall explorer no longer calls `key` and `type_text`; it never reached the native keyboard, whose checks before each key are unit tests in `src/input/native.rs`. The harness's native checks are its keyboard checks, and `make nested` no longer runs M0's wtype checks.
- No dependency was added or removed.

## 2026-10-14: element actions read their effect back

- `set_element_text` reported `matched` when the field's `Text.CharacterCount` equalled the text's, so a field that reformats, masks or swaps characters without changing their number passed as the text set. It now reads `CharacterCount` and, only when that equals the text's, `Text.GetText` from 0 to that count, at most `MAX_TEXT` characters, in the same AT-SPI request and under the same one-second call deadline and three-second budget. `matched` now means the field holds exactly the text sent; anything else is `differs`, with the count as `element.characters`. The text read is compared and dropped: neither it nor the text sent is in the result or the audit log, as before. A count that differs needs no `GetText`.
- `activate_element` reads `Value.CurrentValue` when the element lists the `Value` interface in `Accessible.GetInterfaces`: before the action, right after the action index is found and before the last gate, and again after the settle, after the states. `element.value_changed` says whether it changed, never the value: a spin button's number can be as private as a field's text. The interfaces are asked first because the error an app answers for a property of an interface the element lacks varies, and some are among those that mean the object is gone. A value read before the call that fails or takes over `LAST_GATE`, 250 ms, is kept as why, not raised, so it never refuses the action: the comparison is then `unverified` unless something else changed. It was first read inside the last gate's pass, as old as the states the diff starts from, but the review found that an app answering `CurrentValue` in 300 ms ran every pass over `LAST_GATE` and made the activation `element_busy`, with nothing sent. Moved before the gate with only the one-second call deadlines, an app answering `GetInterfaces` in 990 ms and `CurrentValue` past its deadline left less than the second `DoAction` needs of the request's three-second budget, so the call was `deadline_exceeded` with nothing sent, as the second review found. The read as a whole now gets `LAST_GATE`, which leaves the budget to the action. The cost is a value reading a few hundred milliseconds older than the states, so a value an app changed in that window shows as `value_changed`.
- `activate_element` compares the element's name before and after, as equality only. A button that relabels itself, such as a counter or a play/pause toggle, changes no state, so states alone read it as `none_seen`. The name before is the one the element was listed with: the last gate's `identify` reads the element's own name with `Names::All` and refuses the call as `element_stale` unless it hashes the same, within `LAST_GATE` of the call, so no extra read is needed. After the settle the server reads the name once more, after the states and value, keeps only its `NameHash` (the in-memory hash the gate already compares with), and drops the name. `element.name_changed` says whether it changed; neither name is returned or logged. A name that can't be read again leaves the comparison open: `unverified` unless something else changed.
- Element action results get `effect`, beside `accepted` and `observed`. `accepted` says whether the app took the call and `observed` what the server saw afterwards; `effect` says whether that showed the action's effect:
  - `seen`: the field holds the text sent; a state, the value or the name changed; the element or its window went away or went `defunct`; or, for `activate_element` whose element read the same, niri's change report shows the element's window closed or lost focus to a window that opened.
  - `differs`: `set_element_text` only, the field holds other text.
  - `none_seen`: `activate_element` only, the element's states and name, and its value when it has one, read the same before and after, and niri's report shows its window neither closed nor losing focus to a window that opened. A button that saves a file or sends a message looks like this, so it isn't called a no-op.
  - `unverified`: nothing could be compared: the element couldn't be read afterwards (`observed: unknown`), its value couldn't be read before or after, or its name afterwards, and nothing else changed, or the text couldn't be read back. A stop or a moved lease once the call went out leaves it `unverified` too, since no look follows, whether or not the app had answered: `Gate::send` gives the desk the outcome with `element`, `effect: unverified` and `accepted: null` right before telling it the call goes out, and the app's answer replaces it with `accepted: true`. The review found that without the first, a stop before the answer gave neither `element` nor `effect`, though every element action result is documented to have `effect`. The desk's slot for an answered outcome already carried the second, so no new path was needed.
- A read-back failure never turns a sent action into an error: the action already happened. It is `unverified`, and the upstream detail goes in `detail`, which `observed: unknown` already used and which now also says why a `present` activation's effect is `unverified`. A lost reply or event stream still makes the outcome `uncertain`, and `effect` still says what the look afterwards read, so a lost reply with `effect: seen` is evidence the app acted.
- The change report upgrades only `activate_element`'s `none_seen`, and only for the element's own window closing or losing focus to a window that opened. The report starts before the element work, before `identify`, the action index and the last gate, so it covers time before the call went out. The last gate requires the window open and focused right before the call, so those two changes came after it. A title, size, urgency or workspace change, or a `changes.elements` diff, may have come before the call, as a terminal's ticking title or a clock label does; the review reproduced a retitle while the gate was held turning a button that does nothing into `seen`. Those stay in `changes` for the agent to judge. A failed read stays `unverified` whatever the report says: the report can't say what the element did. Another window changing, or focus moving to a window that was already open, isn't evidence of this action either. `set_element_text`'s effect comes from the text alone. `Changes::shows` decides what counts, and `Outcome::report` applies it where the engine adds the report. Counting only niri events after the call went out would need a send marker in the recorder; the two kept changes need none.
- `effect` is a top-level field rather than part of `element` so other tools can take it later without a second shape. The audit log keeps it beside `accepted` and `observed`; it is a closed set of names.
- The names follow trycua/cua's cua-driver `effect` contract (`confirmed`, `partial`, `unverifiable`, `suspected_noop`, `refused`) only in spirit. Refusals here are errors, never outcomes, and `none_seen` says what was read rather than suspecting a no-op.
- The text is read back right after the app answers `SetTextContents`, with no wait. GTK 4, GTK 3 and Qt set it before answering, in the nested suite, and so does the mock. An app that set it later would read as `differs`, so `differs` and the docs say only that the field holds other text right after the answer, not that the app changed it. No such app was tested.
- The protocol mock serves `GetText` and a `Value` interface, and its field can keep less or other text than it is given.
- The nested suite's Qt fixture has a `SpinBox` and a field that upper-cases its text. Qt Quick lists the spin box's `Increase` action and `Value` interface, and its read-back is `value_changed: true` with `effect: seen`; the field is `differs` at the text's own character count. The GTK entries' `matched` and 64 KiB `differs` now check `effect` too. Each of the counted buttons' 20 activations per toolkit, which relabel the button and change no state, must be `seen` with `element.name_changed: true`, with or without `element_changes`.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: tool list ceilings for `wait_for`'s element condition

- `wait_for` gets an `element` condition (next entry). Its fifth `until` variant, the five fields of its arguments and the description's new sentence add 718 bytes to every profile, since every profile lists `wait_for`: `tools/list` measures 6,876 bytes for `observe`, 23,896 for `act` and 27,127 for `full`, against 6,229, 23,249 and 26,480 on `element-readback` at `142d470`. The `act` list had 1 byte to spare.
- Existing wording was trimmed first. The `until` schema's own description, "What `wait_for` waits for.", repeated the field it sits on and is gone, and the `window` variant reads "A window with that `app_id`, title text, or both." instead of "A window with that `app_id` and a title containing that text; either or both." That saves 71 bytes. The other tools' descriptions were cut by 10 to 14% on 2026-10-13, and some of what is left was kept because eval runs tied it to agent behaviour, such as `type_text`'s "(`focus_mismatch` otherwise)". Taking another 650 bytes out of them would cut text agents use.
- So the ceilings go up, to 6,975 for `observe`, 24,000 for `act` and 27,225 for `full`, about 100 bytes above the new sizes, as the last ones were set, so the room doesn't quietly fill.

## 2026-10-14: `wait_for` waits on elements

- Most waits an agent needs are inside a window: a `Save` button becoming enabled, a `Loading…` label going away, a result row or a dialog's field appearing. Agents listed `elements` or took screenshots until it happened. `wait_for` now takes `{"element": {"window_id", "role", "name_contains", "state", "absent"}}`, after trycua/cua's cua-driver `verify_state`, which polls a condition until it holds.
- The arguments are `elements`' `window_id`, `role` and `name_contains`, unchanged, plus `state` and `absent`. `state` is one name from the table `elements` prints in `states`, so an agent can copy it from a listing. One state, not a list: the waits above each need one, and a list would cost schema bytes for a case not seen yet. `disabled` isn't accepted, because the listing makes it up for a missing `sensitive` rather than reading it; "until enabled" is `state: "enabled"`, and "until disabled" is a wait for `enabled` with `absent`. (Changed after review, below.) A condition with none of `role`, `name_contains` and `state` would be met by any element and is an argument mistake, as a `window` condition without `app_id` and `title` is.
- It matches what `elements` lists with `interactive_only: false`: showing elements with a name or an action, or every showing element of `role`. The default `elements` listing leaves out labels, and a `Loading…` label is exactly what an agent waits on. A hidden element counts as gone, since `elements` doesn't list it either.
- Each check is `elements::poll`, the `elements` walk itself with `limit` 1, so there is no second walk. The walk stops at the second match, which is all a wait needs, and keeps its one-second call deadlines, three-second budget and 2,000-object cap. A ref for the first match is issued as `elements` issues one, so its actions are named and it works with every element tool. A walk that fails is checked against niri's windows before the error is returned: an app that quits takes its window and its bus name together, and its D-Bus calls can fail before niri reports the window closed.
- Walks run one at a time, and the pause after each is as long as it took, but at least 250 ms. `elements` on the nested suite's fixture windows took 5 to 8 ms at the median and at most 90 ms (`docs/results/m9.md`), and an agent's next turn takes seconds, so four walks a second cost the app little and the agent no noticeable time. cua-driver polls every 100 ms; matching the pause to the walk keeps a slow app, whose walk can take the full three seconds, busy answering this server at most half the time. The whole loop is cut off at `timeout_ms`, mid-walk if need be: dropping a walk's future drops its pending calls, which only read.
- A walk cut short by its budget or the cap, or a listing whose action naming ran out of time, can't show that nothing matches, so it never meets an `absent` condition. A `timeout` after such a walk says `the last walk was cut short: <reason>` in `detail`, so an agent knows to narrow the condition rather than wait longer. cua-driver's `elements_complete` makes the same rule.
- A waiter on niri's event stream runs beside the walks and ends the wait as soon as niri reports the window closed, even mid-walk on an app that no longer answers. Without the stream the next walk finds the window gone. Both end as `window_gone`.
- How a wait ends fits the existing `Report`: `Ended` gets `window_gone` and `not_accessible`, with the reason in `detail` for the second, and `Report` gets `element` for an element that appeared. A window whose app isn't on the accessibility bus ends the wait at once as `not_accessible`. Waiting for it to join would hold an app that never does for the whole `timeout_ms`; an agent that just launched an app can wait for its window first. Any other walk failure, such as `deadline_exceeded` from an app that doesn't answer, is the call's error, as in `elements`. A window id niri doesn't have when the wait starts is an argument mistake, as for `title`.
- `element` has the ref, role and states, and `unmappable` and `unbound` as `elements` gives them (added after review, below), not the name or the actions. The agent already chose the name with `name_contains`, and the app's text is returned only where `elements` returns it. The audit log keeps the window id, the role and state when they are known names, otherwise a category and length, the length of `name_contains`, and `absent`.
- Like the other conditions it changes nothing, needs no lease and takes no mutex. Without the lease, `element_ref` is null, as in `elements`. An `absent` wait keeps no refs: the lease keeps the last 1,000, and a ref for each walk that still sees the element would, over a 30-second wait, push out refs the agent listed. The protocol test caught four refs issued by a one-second wait before this.
- The protocol tests run against the mock application: an element added after 400 ms ends a wait met with a ref `activate_element` takes; a budget-cut walk keeps an `absent` wait from ending met, and a 0.9 s answer per object keeps a 1 s wait within 1.5 s; a closed window ends the wait as `window_gone`; and a window off the bus is `not_accessible`. Unit tests on paused time check the pause and that walks don't overlap. The nested suite's GTK 4 `Vanish` check now waits for the removed button with `absent` instead of polling `elements`. No fixture has a control that changes on its own after a delay, so no nested case waits for one.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's review of the element wait

- An `absent` check now asks `elements::look` for `limit` 0: the walk stops at the first match, names no actions and keeps no ref, and `truncated` says whether there was a match. With `limit` 1 it named the actions of the element it still saw, which it throws away. An app slow to name them, as Chromium answers `GetName` for each action, ran the naming out of its second on every walk, and the listing came back empty with `budget_exhausted`, so a `timeout` said the last walk was cut short though it had read the whole tree and found the element. A match read now leaves the wait unmet with no `detail`. The protocol test makes `GetName` take 1.5 s and requires no `GetName` call and no `detail`.
- An appeared element now carries `unbound` and `unmappable` from the listing. Without them, a ref every tool refuses with `element_unbound`, such as a `Delete` button in an unnamed GTK list row, came back as if it worked, where `elements` would have shown `unbound:unnamed_row`.
- An appearing check now reads the whole tree (`Ask::whole`), and still names and keeps a ref for the first match only. With `limit` 1 the walk stopped at the second match, and whether an element outside any row is `unbound: repeated` depends on how many elements read share its role and name: a wait for `del` in a list put on the bus without rows stopped after `Delete all` and bound the first `Delete`, which `elements` reading on marks `repeated`. Every unmet check already read the whole tree, so in walks this costs at most one more, on the check that meets the condition; but that walk can take the full three-second budget, longer than a short wait, so its budget is cut to what is left of the wait (round 2, below). A walk cut short by its budget or the 2,000-object cap still can't count the elements with the same role and name that it didn't read. A match that sits in no row and is bound only by that count (`Walked::bound_by_count`) then gets no ref, and `detail` says why and to aim at its pixels. A ref that every tool refuses would also be safe, but would look usable until tried; a null ref with the reason is plainer. A match inside a named row, or its own row, is bound by the row's name and keeps its ref. `elements` itself is unchanged: a listing that stops at `limit` or is cut short still binds by the count of what it read.
- An app found off the accessibility bus after an earlier walk read it no longer ends the wait at once. An app that quits leaves the bus and niri at about the same time, and the registry can drop it before niri reports the window closed; the one re-check of niri's windows after a failed walk then still saw the window, and the wait ended `not_accessible` instead of `window_gone`. The walk now counts as unmet once: the event stream can report the close during the pause, and the next walk, with or without the stream, finds the window gone or the app off the bus again, which ends the wait `not_accessible`. An app off the bus on the first walk still ends it at once. A fixed grace period would also work, but the next walk is already a bounded look that decides the case itself.
- `state` now accepts `disabled`, and the docs and the error name where the other names are. The default compact listing never prints `enabled`, `focusable`, `sensitive`, `showing` or `visible`, and prints `disabled` for an element without `sensitive`, so an agent that copied the one state it saw was refused, and the advice to wait for `enabled` tested another bit than the `disabled` it read. `disabled` matches an element without `sensitive`, the compact line's own rule (`Filter::with_state`, `model::DISABLED`, which the compact line now uses too), and the other names are those in `states` of a `format: "json"` listing. The docs point "until it can be pressed" at `sensitive`, since `disabled` with `absent` is also met when the button goes away. A `disabled` filter is one bit the element lacks, so a list of states was still not needed. The schema text grows by 17 bytes: `tools/list` measures 6,893, 23,913 and 27,144 bytes, under the ceilings.
- The protocol test for a closing window removed the window from niri's list too, so a walk would have found it gone without the event stream. niri now goes on listing it and the panel answers in 0.9 s, so only the stream can end the wait, mid-walk, within 0.8 s; with the stream waiter's check stubbed out, the test times out at 10 s.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's second review of the element wait

- A walk now ends by the wait's end. The whole-tree walk an appearing check reads went on after its match until the tree, its three-second budget or the cap ran out, and the wait cut it off at `timeout_ms` with everything it had read: with two buttons first and two objects answering in 0.9 s after them, a `role: button` wait of 1 s timed out, though the buttons were read in the first 10 ms. The walk's request now gets `min(BUDGET, by − now)` and the naming request `min(NAMING, by − now)`, where `by` is 50 ms (`RESERVE`) before the wait's end, passed from `wait::watch` through `Engine::poll` and `elements::poll` to `elements::read` as `Ask::by`. A walk that runs out of it returns its partial listing as `budget_exhausted`: a match in it ends the wait `met`, with its ref withheld when the match is bound only by the count, and no match leaves the "cut short" detail on a `timeout`. That held only for a match whose actions the walk read with their names; see the third review, below. A walk out of time before it began, while finding the app or its frame, is that same cut listing, read empty, not a `deadline_exceeded` error. The 50 ms leave the wait time to take the listing before its own cut-off, which stays for a walk that doesn't stop in time, and fit under `wait_for`'s 100 ms minimum, so even the shortest wait walks once. This also fixes the older form, where a walk with one match cut at the deadline lost it. `elements` passes no `by` and is unchanged.
- The protocol test is the review's repro: `Safe` and `Odd` first, `/entry` and `/password` answering `GetState` in 0.9 s, `role: button`, `timeout_ms` 1000. It now ends `met` at about 950 ms, with no ref, since `Safe` sits in no row and the walk was cut, and `detail` says why. It timed out at 1,001 ms before. The mock's buttons list their actions named (`click`), so naming made no calls; with an action listed unnamed, as Chromium lists them, the wait still timed out until the fix below.
- Without the lease, no ref is withheld, so no `detail` says one was: `Engine::poll` clears `withheld` when there is no lease. The result said a ref was held back and told the agent to aim at pixels, which needs the lease, instead of the missing lease being the reason. The protocol test repeats the cut-walk case without the lease and requires no `detail`.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's third review of the element wait

- A match whose actions need naming now survives a walk that used the wait's time. After a walk that ran to `by`, the naming request gets almost nothing, and an app that lists its action names empty and gives them only through `Action.GetName`, as Chromium does, timed out on the first call: `look` dropped the match it had read, as `elements` does when naming runs out, and the wait timed out with "the last walk was cut short: budget_exhausted", sending the agent to narrow a condition the walk had met. With `Ask::by` set, `look` now keeps that match with its actions as the walk read them, keeps no ref for it and stops naming, and marks the listing `Withheld::Unnamed`. The wait ends `met` with no `element_ref` and a `detail` of its own: the wait ran out of time naming its actions, which a ref needs, so wait again with a longer `timeout_ms` or aim at its pixels. `Listing::withheld` became that reason, beside `Withheld::Unchecked` for the cut walk's look-alike case, and is still cleared without the lease. A ref with unnamed actions isn't issued, since the element actions refuse rather than guess an action they can't name. `elements` passes no `by` and still ends its listing before the element it couldn't name.
- The reviewer's other option, ending the walk early enough to leave `NAMING` for the naming, would cut every short wait's walk by up to a second to name actions most apps list named.
- The protocol test is the round-2 repro with `Safe`'s action listed unnamed: it ends `met` in under 1.2 s with no ref and that `detail`, where it timed out at 1,000 ms with the cut-walk detail before.
- `Withheld::Unnamed` is now only for naming that ran out of the wait's time, `by`. Naming has its own second (`NAMING`) too, and an app slower than that to answer `GetName`, with most of the wait left, was told to wait again with a longer `timeout_ms`, which would end the same way: every walk's naming stops at that second. That case is `Withheld::SlowNames`, whose `detail` says the app took longer to name its actions than the server allows and points only at the element's pixels. `look` tells them apart by whether `by` has passed when naming runs out. The protocol test is the fourth review's repro: `GetName` taking 1.5 s and `timeout_ms` 8000, which now ends `met` in about a second with that `detail` and no mention of `timeout_ms`.

## 2026-10-14: a Chromium fixture in the nested harness

- What Chromium and Electron apps receive was known only from probes at the desk, in Vesktop. `make nested-chromium` now types and pastes text through the server into a Chromium text area in the headless nested niri and compares what arrived with what was sent, after trycua/cua's cua-driver, whose end-to-end tests launch small fixture apps, Electron among them, and check what actually arrived.
- The page puts the field's value in its title after every input, and the supervisor reads it from niri's window list. A page can't write files without a server beside it, and reading the field over AT-SPI would test the accessibility bus too. The value goes out as UTF-16 code units in hex, because Chromium collapses and trims whitespace in titles and a lost space is one of the things to catch; code units also keep a lone surrogate. Each keydown's DOM `code` and `key` go to the console, which `--enable-logging=stderr` puts in the browser's log, so a run shows the keycode every symbol went out on.
- No browser is added to the repo or CI. `NCU_CHROMIUM` names one, and otherwise the first of six Chromium-based browser names on `PATH` is used. Without either the run is skipped, not failed. It is found by `harness run`, outside the nested session, whose environment keeps only `HOME`, `PATH` and `LANG`, and passed to the supervisor as an argument. The browser runs in app mode on Wayland with its profile, `HOME` and XDG directories under `TEST_DIR`, so it never touches the user's profile. Electron isn't covered: the only Electron app on the machine it was built on is Vesktop, which opens Discord, not a local page.
- The run asserts what the docs promise today: typed text without emoji, and every paste, arrive exactly. Typed emoji are logged as received and pass, since Chromium cuts characters outside the Basic Multilingual Plane to 16 bits and only paste sends them. When they arrive exactly the log says a fix landed. (Changed after review, below: they pass only on the known cut text.)
- The cases still include text that puts a space or period on wtype's Escape and Backspace codes, though wtype was removed earlier today, so the native keyboard stays checked against the losses that motivated it. The spare-key cases measured what the F13 to F24 entry above found at the desk: three, fourteen and thirty symbols a US layout lacks arrived exactly, on `IntlRo`, `IntlYen`, then `F13` to `F24`. The results are in `docs/results/chromium.md`.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's review of the Chromium fixture

- The browser gets `TMPDIR` under `TEST_DIR`. Chromium makes its singleton socket's directory, `org.chromium.Chromium.XXXXXX`, in `TMPDIR` or else `/tmp`, and doesn't remove it on exit, even after SIGTERM, so every run left one in the host's `/tmp`. A run now leaves none there.
- The emoji case names the text it is known to arrive as, `ok \u{f916} \u{f319} done`, and passes on that or on the exact text. Before, it passed on anything, so a run that lost the ASCII around the emoji, or all of it, still passed. The rest of that text is promised like any other.
- An exact result now has to stay the same for a second before it counts, as any other result does. Before, the first exact title counted, and the field was cleared right after, so a key repeated or delivered late after the text could never be seen. Each call now takes a second longer, so a run takes about 53 s instead of 30.
- The wait for the browser's window checks the browser first, and a browser that has exited fails the run at once with its exit status and its log's path. Before, it waited out the 30 s and reported a missing window.
- The browser's path is made absolute without resolving links. `/snap/bin/chromium` is a link to `/usr/bin/snap`, which picks the app from its own name, so resolving it would have run `snap`. A browser under `/snap` is refused anyway, because a snap keeps its data in the user's `~/snap/<name>` whatever `HOME` is.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: `type_text` refuses tool-call tags leaked at the end of its text

- trycua/cua's cua-driver found that models sometimes carry their own tool-call closing tags, such as `</parameter>` and `</invoke>`, into the end of a long `text` argument, and the server then types them into the document. `type_text` now refuses such text with `leaked_tag` before anything is typed or pasted. The detail names the tags, so the agent sends the text again without them.
- It refuses rather than strips. Every other check in this server refuses and reports instead of changing what goes out, and results say exactly what was sent. A stray tag typed into a chat box with `submit: true` is sent and can't be taken back, while a refusal costs one more call. Stripping and reporting would also stop the leak, but the agent would learn of it only after the shortened text went out, and a trailing tag the user wanted would be dropped without the agent choosing that.
- cua's guardrails are kept. Only known tag names count, in any ASCII case: `text`, `parameter`, `invoke`, `function_calls`, `function_call`, `tool_use`, `tool_call`, and `antml:` before `parameter`, `invoke` or `function_calls`. Only a run at the very end counts, with whitespace allowed between and after the tags. Only closing tags that no opening tag of the same name earlier in the text balances count. XML or docs that open and close these tags go out as they are, and so does such a tag anywhere but at the end. The check covers `via: "paste"` too, since long multi-line arguments are usually pasted.
- What's left is text that should end with such a tag without opening it, such as a quoted tool call. To type it, send the final `>` in a call of its own: then neither call ends with a whole tag. That workaround is in the skill's and the site's error references, not in the detail, so an agent that leaked a tag isn't told how to get it through.
- The check is the pure module `input/leaked_tags.rs`, unit-tested. It looks up each name's first opening tag once, so a 1 MiB paste made of tags stays linear. The audit log keeps `text_len` and the error name, never the text or the tags.
- The tool description is unchanged. The tool list was 87 bytes under its `act` ceiling and 81 under `full`, and the error's name and detail say what to do.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: property tests with proptest 1.11.0

- Item 5 of the ideas taken from trycua/cua's cua-driver asked for `cargo-fuzz` targets over the parsers agents feed. `cargo-fuzz` needs a nightly toolchain for its sanitizer flags, and this repo pins stable 1.99.0, so these are property tests instead: they run under `make test` on the pinned toolchain, with no second toolchain in CI.
- `proptest` is a dev-dependency only, so the published binary doesn't change. It generates inputs from strategies, including strings from regexes, and minimises a failing input before printing it. A hand-written loop over random inputs would need its own generator and shrinker. `quickcheck` was considered; it has no regex strategies.
- Version 1.11.0, published 2026-03-24. 1.12.0 came out on 2026-10-10, under the 7-day rule. Default features are off and only `std` is on, which leaves out `fork` and `timeout` (`rusty-fork`, `tempfile`, `wait-timeout`) and `bit-set`. Its MIT or Apache-2.0 license, and those of the crates it brings in, pass `cargo deny`.
- Adding it resolved `zerocopy` and `zerocopy-derive` 0.8.63 into the lock file, published 2026-10-13. They are held at 0.8.61, published 2026-10-07, the newest release at least 7 days old. The other new entries, among them `rand` 0.9.5 (2026-07-11), `rand_core` 0.9.5 (2026-01-13), `regex-syntax` 0.8.11 (2026-06-09) and `getrandom` 0.3.4 (2025-10-14), were published more than 7 days ago. `getrandom` 0.3.4 sits beside the 0.4.3 already in the lock file; `cargo deny` reports no new duplicate.
- The properties are the ones a bug would break, not restatements of the code: every ref id the server issues parses back to what it names, and no other spelling of an id names anything; every pixel of a screenshot of a whole output, at any niri scale and transform and at a `max_width` scale, aims the pointer; a zoom covers its region inside the screenshot or is `out_of_bounds`; a point on an output is always encoded and none off it is; combinations parse to their modifiers and key under any alias and case; the trailing-tag guardrails hold; an accepted `save_path` stays under the capture directory; a policy file that parses has what `launch` relies on; and no `niri_action` JSON, valid or not, puts the agent's text in the audit log or panics the gate. A mutation check, a lower pointer tolerance and a zoom rounding down, failed the three geometry properties.
- The first one found a bug: `parse::<u64>` takes a leading `+` and leading zeros, so `shot-<tag>-+1` named ref 1 while the audit log called it `invalid_ref`. It is fixed with that input as a regression test.
- Case counts are fixed per test, 256 to 1024; the property tests take about half a second in all on one thread, and the unit tests' wall time under `make test` didn't measurably change. Proptest's file of failing inputs is off, so a failing run writes nothing into the source tree.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's review of the input hardening
