- An opening tag anywhere before the trailing run let every closing tag of its name through, so text quoting a tool call, followed by the agent's own leaked closing tags, was typed. Tags now balance by count: one pass over the text before the run tracks, per name, how many opening tags are still open, a closing tag closing the latest one if any, and walking the run from its start each open tag balances one closing tag. The run is reported from its first closing tag left unbalanced. The pass looks at each `<` once against the ten names, so it stays linear. A bare `<text` followed by a space in prose still counts as an opening tag, as in cua: an opening tag may have attributes, and telling prose from markup would mean parsing it.
- `any_text_is_checked` drew from any string, which never holds a known closing tag, so its assertion never ran. It is replaced by `only_the_tags_at_the_end_are_named`, which joins free words, whitespace, known closing and opening tags in any case, and an unknown closing tag. It checks that only closing tags after the text's last other character are named, and that with no opening tag in the text all of them are. Treating whitespace between tags as the end of the run, or taking an unknown tag for a known one, makes it fail.
- The `save_path` property drew characters, so few inputs were accepted and almost none held a whole `..` part: with the `..` check removed from `SavePath::parse` it still passed. It now joins parts that are names, `.`, `..`, empty or hold a NUL, sometimes from the root, then a file name. With the `..` check removed it failed in 5 of 5 runs here and in 4 of 5 in the review: it is randomised, but it catches the bug.
- Typed text can't hold line breaks, so markup split for `text_too_long` goes out in parts of one line, and a part can end with a `text` or `parameter` closing tag whose opening tag was in an earlier part. That part is refused with `leaked_tag`, whose detail says to drop the tag. The advice to split, in `text_too_long`'s and `clipboard_unsaved`'s details, the skill and the site, now says to end no part with a `>`, so the split never lands there.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's second review of the input hardening

- The run was reported from its first closing tag left unbalanced, so in `<text>a`, then a stray `invoke` closing tag, then the document's own `text` closing tag, both were named and the agent was told to drop them. A leak is always at the very end, so a balanced tag ends any leak before it: only the tags after the last balanced one in the run are reported, and none when the last is balanced. The quoted tool call followed by leaked tags and the doubled closing tag give the same answers as before.
- The check trims whitespace after the run, so a part split at the space after a closing tag, as agents split at word boundaries, was still refused although it didn't end "right after a `>`". The advice in both details, the skill and the site now says to end no part with a `>`, even one followed by spaces.

## 2026-10-14: `elements` binds no ref by a count it didn't finish

- An element outside any list row was bound only when no other element the walk read had its role and name, and `elements` walks stopped early: at one match past `limit`, at the 2,000-object cap or when the three-second budget ran out. A control whose look-alike came later in document order was bound as unique, and its ref could act on a record whose row had been reused, or on the wrong one of two identical buttons. In a list put on the bus without rows, `Delete` (record 1), `Delete all`, `Delete` (record 2), a `role: "button"` listing with `limit` 1 stopped after `Delete all` and gave a bound ref for the first `Delete`. With `limit` 2 the walk already read the third match, the second `Delete`, so the old code marked the first `repeated`; `limit` 1 is the case that showed the bug. The 2026-10-12 entry above documented this as a known gap; the element wait closed it for `wait_for` only.
- `elements` now does both. It asks with `Ask::whole`, as an appearing wait does: the walk reads past `limit` to the end of the tree, its cap or its budget, and only the first `limit` matches are listed and named. `truncated` still says whether more matched. When the walk still stopped before the end, `Walked::row` makes an element in no row, with no look-alike among the nodes read, `Unbound::CutShort`. The rule sits where the count is, so it holds for every caller: a walk cut at its cap or budget, or stopped at its `limit` (a private `Walked::stopped`, set only when nodes were left). `Walked::bound_by_count` is gone, and `Read::unchecked` asks `row` for `CutShort`.
- `elements` lists such an element with a ref, marked `unbound: cut_short`, and every tool refuses the ref with `element_unbound`, whose detail says the listing was cut short and to aim at its pixels. That is the contract the other two reasons already have, so `unbound` keeps one meaning. A null ref would also be safe, but `-` already means "no lease" on a compact line. The wait keeps its null ref and `detail` (`Withheld::Unchecked`). `look` now picks that path by `Ask::by`, which only a wait sets, since both callers now read the whole tree. The appeared `element` also carries `unbound: cut_short`, which the wait's docs already promised ("as `elements` gives them"). Its ref and `detail` are unchanged.
- Reading past `limit` costs a whole walk on every listing. Measured in the nested headless niri with release builds, five calls each, median ms, before → after, against a scratch GTK 4 window with a `Save` button and N buttons in a box, and a scratch Chromium page (Helium 0.19.2.1, Chromium 155) with N buttons. Neither window is kept.

  | Window | objects | default | `role: button` | `limit: 5` | `name_contains` (whole walk before too) |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | GTK 4, 300 buttons | 617 | 9 → 64 | 10 → 57 | 2 → 59 | 53 → 55 |
  | GTK 4, 2,500 buttons | 2,000 (cap) | 9 → 232 | 13 → 188 | 4 → 195 | 197 → 200 |
  | Chromium, 300 buttons | 346 | 7 → 25 | 8 → 26 | 2 → 23 | 24 → 22 |
  | Chromium, 2,500 buttons | 2,000 (cap) | 48 → 172 | 48 → 169 | 2 → 168 | 169 → 154 |

  So the cost is at most about a fifth of a second per listing on these toolkits, and the three-second budget still bounds it for a slow app. The `name_contains` column already read the whole tree before, so it shows the walk itself didn't get slower. In `make nested-a11y`, whose fixtures mostly fit within `limit`, the audit log's `elements` durations matched the run before the change call for call, within a few milliseconds, the slowest list fixture's 80 ms included.
- What this costs agents: in a window over 2,000 objects, or one whose tree takes over three seconds to read, every element outside a named row is `cut_short`, so `activate_element`, `set_element_text` and pointer aims at refs work there only inside named rows. In the Chromium page above, the title bar's `Close` button became `cut_short`. The 2026-10-12 entry rejected this, since it disables element actions in windows over 2,000 objects. It is accepted now because a ref that may act on the wrong record is worse than none, and the pointer tools still reach those elements by pixels. The cap and the budget are unchanged; raising them would be a change of its own.
- The other places that issue element refs: `element_changes` hands out refs from `elements::survey`, which walks with no limit and returns `Unknown` for a walk cut short, so its count always covers the whole tree and it has no such gap. `elements::name_reported` only names actions. The wait was fixed by the element wait's reviews.
- Tests: the protocol tests run the example both ways, stopped at `limit` 1 (`repeated`) and cut by the budget, with four labels answering in 0.9 s between the two `Delete`s (`cut_short`). Both failed on the old code, where the first `Delete` was bound, and both now require `activate_element` and a `click` aimed by `element` to refuse it with `element_unbound`, with nothing sent to the app or the pointer. The other tools go through the same `identify` check. A third checks that a `Delete` in a named row keeps a working ref when the walk is cut. The protocol test that pinned the old gap (`a_control_outside_rows_whose_look_alike_elements_went_unread_keeps_its_ref`) is replaced by these. Unit tests check `Walked::row` for a cut walk and for walks stopped at and exactly at their limit. The wait's cut-walk test also checks for `unbound: cut_short`.
- The `elements` tool description now says `unbound:<why>` is for "an element" rather than "a list row's element": `tools/list` measures 6,883, 23,903 and 27,134 bytes, 10 fewer each.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after the review of `cut_short`

- A node its parent listed but that was gone before the walk read it, with whatever was under it, left `capped` and the limit flag unset, so `Walked::row` took the count for finished and bound a row-less element whose only look-alike sat in that part. Qt Quick drops and recreates a row delegate's accessible object as it goes, so a walk can miss a row that way. The flag is now `Walked::missed`: set when the walk stopped at its limit with nodes left, as before, and when a node was gone. A node is only ever read when its parent was showing, so every gone node counts. Such a walk makes row-less unique elements `cut_short`, and the `element_unbound` detail now says the listing didn't read the whole tree, cut short or with part of it gone meanwhile. `capped` stays unset, since nothing bounded the walk. The unit test walks a root whose second child is gone; it bound the first child as `Lineage` before. The wait's `detail` for a withheld ref still says the walk was cut short, which covers this case loosely; it is item 2's text and left as it is.
- With `elements` reading past `limit`, the row-binding test that listed with `limit` 1 no longer stopped its walk, so it checked named and unnamed rows only in a walk that read the whole tree. Four of the first row's static labels now answer `GetState` in 0.9 s, so the walk runs out of its three seconds before the row's label and the next row, and the test requires `budget_exhausted`. It fails if a cut walk overrides a row's binding, which a scratch change to `Walked::row` confirmed.
- The site's `elements` page still advised narrowing with `role`, `name_contains` or a small `limit` when a large tree ran out of time. None of them shortens the walk now, so the page says so and points at pixels for the elements left `cut_short`.

## 2026-10-14: the server turns the session's accessibility on for Chromium and Electron

- Chromium, Electron and CEF apps put themselves on the accessibility bus only when the session's accessibility was on when they started, and build their page's tree only once an assistive technology asks for it. `elements` gave `not_accessible` for them unless a screen reader ran, which is item 6 of the ideas taken from trycua/cua's cua-driver: it turns accessibility on itself.
- What was measured, in the nested niri with its private session and accessibility buses, at-spi2-core 2.62.1 and Helium 0.19.2.1 (Chromium 155.0.8059.39), and VS Code 1.126 once with a throwaway profile:
  - Chromium decides at startup (`ShouldEnableAccessibility` in `atk_util_auralinux.cc`): the environment variables `ACCESSIBILITY_ENABLED` and `GNOME_ACCESSIBILITY`, then `org.a11y.Status.IsEnabled` on the session bus, then GSettings `org.gnome.desktop.interface toolkit-accessibility` only if the bus call fails. A browser started with it off wasn't on the bus 20 s after it was turned on, so a running browser needs a restart. One started with it on was on the bus about 0.4 s after it started, a few milliseconds after its window appeared. Turning it off left a registered browser on the bus.
  - A registered browser shows only its application, its frame and a child `/org/a11y/atspi/null` until something calls `GetAttributes` or `GetRelationSet` on one of its objects. After `GetAttributes` on the frame, the frame's children appear within about 2 ms, and the page's tree follows: about 0.3 s for 300 table rows, 1.3 s for 2,000. `--force-renderer-accessibility` fills the tree too, but only when `IsEnabled` was on at start, and the server doesn't start the apps it reads.
  - at-spi2-core 2.62's launcher has only `IsEnabled`; `ScreenReaderEnabled` was removed in 2.61.0 (commit c674dc97). Since 2.61, `IsEnabled` is true while any client has an event listener registered with the registry, and false once none is left: the launcher watches each listener's bus name. A write still forces it. Either way of turning it on also writes `toolkit-accessibility = true` to GSettings, and nothing writes it back to false.
  - VS Code behaved the same: after the nudge, `elements` listed its 12 window-chrome elements, the document still empty at first.
- The server never writes `org.a11y.Status`. A write would stay after the server ended, and on a desktop where something else wrote it, writing it back off would take away what the user turned on. When `elements`, an element wait or an action's `element_changes` finds the window's app off the bus (they share `elements::read`), it reads `IsEnabled`. Off, it registers a listener for `window:activate`, an event apps seldom send, on a connection of its own (`a11y::Switch`) and waits up to 500 ms for `IsEnabled` to turn true. On through others' listeners, a screen reader's or another server's, it adds its own, so accessibility stays on while it needs it. On with no listener, written on by something, it leaves it alone: its listener leaving would turn it off.
- The connection lasts as long as the server, or in shared mode the engine, so it holds accessibility on for one owner and lets it go when that ends, however it ends, a crash or SIGKILL included, unless another listener keeps it on. No cleanup path is needed, and `IsEnabled` goes back off after the last server ends. The GSettings key `toolkit-accessibility`, which the launcher writes on the off-to-on change the server's listener causes, stays true in the user's dconf after the server ends; that is the launcher's doing and happens when a screen reader starts too. (Corrected after review, below: this entry first said nothing is left on.)
- An older at-spi2-core doesn't turn accessibility on for a listener. When `IsEnabled` stays false after 500 ms, the server drops its listener, remembers the launcher as unsupported for its lifetime and says so in the error's detail, with nothing written. That covers every launcher before 2.61. GNOME and Cinnamon need no special case, since `ScreenReaderEnabled` is never touched.
- `not_accessible` now says what was done: accessibility was off and the server turned it on, it is on (so the app may not support it or started while it was off), it is off and the policy file refuses, or it couldn't be read. Every case that could be an app started while it was off says apps such as Chromium and Electron check only at start, and to ask the user before restarting it. The server never restarts an app itself. The server instructions say so too, within their 1,900-character limit (1,878).
- `turn_on_accessibility = false` in the policy file keeps the server from registering the listener; it defaults to on, since a server that lists nothing for the user's browser is the more common surprise, and the change is undone when the server ends. An invalid policy file refuses too, since it might set the key to `false`. `status.policy.turn_on_accessibility` says which applies, and `status.accessibility_switch` says what the server's listener did: `off`, `on`, `turning_on` or `unsupported`. The `status` subcommand, which has no engine, reports null.
- The nudge: when the frame `elements` reads has only `/org/a11y/atspi/null` children, the server calls `GetAttributes` on it and reads its children again (`Request::frame`). The listing then has `building: true`, and the compact header says to list again for the rest, since the page's tree arrives over the next second or so. GTK and Qt frames have real children, so they are never asked.
- Costs in `make nested-chromium-a11y`: the fixture's field listed in 56 to 74 ms with 49 objects walked; the first listing after the nudge 60 to 81 ms with 44 walked and 14 listed, the browser's own window chrome. 300 rows: 0.2 to 0.25 s, 517 walked, 211 listed, 10 refs bound, 192 `unnamed_row` and 9 `repeated`. 2,000 rows: 0.7 to 0.8 s, capped at 2,000 objects, 210 listed, no ref bound (192 `unnamed_row`, 10 `cut_short`, 8 `repeated`). So the switch makes long Chromium pages listable but leaves few usable refs in them; that is the row binding's rule and outside this change.
- Found in the probe and left open: every Chromium element is `unmappable: frame_size_mismatch`, since the frame's extents (648×696 for a 616×654 window) include the client-side shadow, so the pointer tools can't aim at them; and Chromium text fields have no `EditableText`, so `set_element_text` refuses them with `invalid_arguments`. `activate_element` works.
- Tests: the protocol tests' mock launcher keeps `IsEnabled` true while a registered listener's connection lasts, unless a test makes it older. They check that the listener turns it on and that SIGKILL of the engine turns it off, that accessibility already on is left alone, the policy key, an older launcher, a registry or launcher that never answers within the call's budget, two servers holding it together in standalone mode and one listener in shared mode, and the nudge with `building`. None writes `org.a11y.Status`, and each test's bus is its own. `make nested-chromium-a11y` runs a real browser against the server on the nested session bus, after checking that the bus's socket is under the run's directory.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's review of the accessibility switch

- The review found that the launcher's `toolkit-accessibility = true` (at-spi2-core's `handle_a11y_enabled_change`, run with `notify_gsettings` on the first listener) lands in the user's dconf and stays there, and that any window off the bus can cause it, a terminal's included. The entry above said nothing is left on. The server still doesn't reset the key: that would be a second write to the user's settings, wouldn't run after SIGKILL, and would race a screen reader's own write. Instead the `not_accessible` detail for `Turned::On` names the key and says it stays, and the configuration page's `turn_on_accessibility` section says so with `gsettings reset org.gnome.desktop.interface toolkit-accessibility` to undo it, run here only with `GSETTINGS_BACKEND=memory` so it touched no settings. Whether `true` stays the default is the user's decision and is left as it is for now.
- A call that found another one turning accessibility on waited for that call's lock with no deadline of its own, so in shared mode a client's `elements`, or a `wait_for` poll near its end, could outlast its budget by the other call's. It now waits only until its own deadline and then says the server was still turning it on for another call (`Turned::Busy`). The unit test holds the lock and fails on the old code, whose call waited past its 100 ms deadline.
- `unsupported` was remembered for the engine's lifetime whenever `IsEnabled` read false at the end of the wait, even when the call's own deadline had cut the 500 ms short, and the detail then claimed 500 ms and an at-spi2-core before 2.61. A `wait_for` element poll with 150 ms left did that against a launcher that takes 350 ms. Now only a wait that ran the whole 500 ms makes the launcher `unsupported`; a cut one lets the listener go, says so and leaves the switch `off`, so the next call tries again (`Turned::Late`). The mock launcher can now take a while to count a listener (`Mock::slow_launcher`), and the protocol test, a 200 ms `wait_for` followed by `elements`, failed on the old code with `unsupported`.
- In the same wait, each `IsEnabled` read had only until the wait's end, so a read begun just before it timed out and the call reported a failed registration instead of `unsupported`: the older-launcher test failed now and then under load. Reads now have until the call's deadline, and only the polling stops at the wait's end. With reads that take 30 ms, the older-launcher test failed on every run before and passes now.
- When something wrote `IsEnabled` false while the server's listener held it on, the next off-bus call said the server left it off but kept the listener. at-spi2-core recomputes `IsEnabled` from the listeners whenever one registers or vanishes, so the next screen reader or other client to start or end would have turned it back on against that write. The switch now lets its listener go and stays `let_go` until the engine ends, and the detail and `status.accessibility_switch` say so. Another server with a listener still holds it, as it would hold it on anyway. The mock launcher now honours such a write until the next listener registers (`Mock::written_off`), and the protocol test, which checks that the listener's connection is gone, failed on the old code.
- The policy file is read per client session, but the listener belongs to the engine, so in shared mode a client whose file sets `turn_on_accessibility = false` doesn't drop a listener the engine registered for another client. That stays as it is: clients of one engine can have different policy files, and one client's file shouldn't turn accessibility off under another's session. The configuration page now says the key keeps the server from turning it on, not off, while an engine already holds it, and that it stays off once every client of the instance has closed. No test: nothing changed in what the server does.
- Every `not_accessible` detail for an app off the bus ended with "ask the user before restarting it", whatever the app, and the server instructions told agents to pass that on. A terminal, or an app behind a sandbox proxy, would get a useless restart request. The server can't tell a Chromium app from the window, so the detail now names the window's `app_id` and says a restart helps only if it is a Chromium, Electron or CEF app; the instructions say "when `not_accessible` says a Chromium or Electron app needs a restart, ask the user first" (1,879 of 1,900 characters). The skill and site say to check the `app_id`. The protocol tests require the `app_id` and the condition and failed on the old detail.
- `building: true` was set whenever the server had called `GetAttributes`, even when the frame read again still had only null children. An app whose frame never builds would get the flag, and "list again", on every call, with nothing in the skill to stop the agent. Now the flag needs a child that isn't the null reference. Chromium 155's frame had real children right after the call in every nested run, so Chromium keeps the flag. The protocol test's frame stays null-only after `GetAttributes` and got the flag on the old code.

## 2026-10-14: the accessibility switch only for Chromium-family processes

- The user decided the default after the review: `turn_on_accessibility` stays `true`, but the server registers its listener only for a Chromium-family process (Chromium, Electron, CEF), never for a terminal, a GTK or Qt app, or anything else. Only those apps check the session's accessibility before they join the bus, so only for them can turning it on help. For any other app it changed the session and the user's settings for nothing. The previous entry's `app_id` and restart condition in the detail were a stopgap for the same problem.
- What the switch means for the user's settings: turning accessibility on makes at-spi2-core's launcher write `org.gnome.desktop.interface toolkit-accessibility = true` into the user's dconf (`handle_a11y_enabled_change`, on the off-to-on change only). Nothing writes it back. at-spi2-core's own autostart condition and the `at-spi2-atk` GTK module key read it, so a session that honours them starts the accessibility bus at login and loads `at-spi2-atk` into GTK 3 apps from then on. With the scope narrowed, only a Chromium-family window off the bus, while accessibility is off, can cause that write. The detail and the configuration page say so, with `gsettings reset org.gnome.desktop.interface toolkit-accessibility` to undo it.
- Detection is from the window's process, not its `app_id`, which the app chooses: `control::procs::chromium_family` reads the `/proc/<pid>/exe` link and checks for Chromium's `icudtl.dat` or V8's `v8_context_snapshot.bin` as files beside the executable. Every Chromium build ships both, and Electron and CEF ship them as Chromium does. Checked by reading files only, never running anything on the host: `/opt/helium-browser-bin/` (Helium 0.19.2.1), Playwright's `chromium-1243/chrome-linux64/`, `/opt/vesktop/` (the `/usr/bin/vesktop` script execs `/opt/vesktop/vesktop`) and `/usr/share/code/` (VS Code) each hold both files. Reading `/proc/<pid>/exe` of the user's running Helium browser process gave `/opt/helium-browser-bin/helium`, so a browser process is readable, while pid 1, root's, is not.
- A process the server can't inspect counts as not Chromium-family: another user's, a non-dumpable one, or one whose executable isn't at that path in the server's filesystem, as in a Flatpak sandbox. (Corrected after the second review: this said Flatpak or Snap, and a Snap's executable is visible from the host. Since that review, detection reads `/proc/<pid>/maps`, below.) Such an app off the bus gets the plain `not_accessible`, as any other non-Chromium app does: no `IsEnabled` read, no listener, no restart advice.
- The `/proc` read stays in `control::procs`, which already owns process inspection; `elements::off_bus` calls it before anything on the bus and returns the plain error for any other app. `policy::off_bus` still decides only between listening, leaving and the policy refusal, for the apps the switch applies to.
- Tests: the protocol tests now run each off-bus window's app as a process of its own, a copy of `sleep` in the test's directory, with an empty `icudtl.dat` beside it for a Chromium-family one. The new test, a terminal-like app with no such file, requires a plain `not_accessible`, no listener and no `IsEnabled` read; it failed on the old code, which turned accessibility on for it. The other accessibility tests run against the Chromium-marked app and pass as before. A unit test on a fake `/proc` checks both files, a directory named like one, an executable path that doesn't exist and a missing process.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-14: fixes after Opus's second review of the accessibility switch

- Detection looked for `icudtl.dat` or `v8_context_snapshot.bin` beside the executable with a `stat` in that directory, synchronously on a runtime worker and with no deadline. The executable's directory can be on any filesystem: an AppImage's FUSE mount whose runtime is stopped, or a dead NFS home, would hold the `elements` call and the worker past every budget. `control::procs::chromium_family` now reads the process's `/proc/<pid>/maps` instead and looks for either file among the mapped paths, ` (deleted)` ones too, so it never touches the app's filesystem. It also no longer counts an unrelated install that happens to leave the files beside a program. The read is bounded twice: at most 4 MiB (a Helium browser that had run for days had 223 KB of maps, `icudtl.dat` at line 1,825 of 2,265), and the request's deadline, on a thread of its own, since a `/proc` maps read waits for the process's memory map. A plain `std::thread` rather than `spawn_blocking`, because the runtime's shutdown waits for blocking tasks and a stuck read would then hold the engine's exit. A read that fails or misses the deadline means not Chromium-family.
- What maps: a running Helium browser process (read from `/proc` only) maps `/opt/helium-browser-bin/icudtl.dat` and not `v8_context_snapshot.bin`. `make nested-chromium-a11y` now checks that the browser's window process maps one of them and logs which: `["icudtl.dat"]` for both Helium 0.19.2.1 and Playwright's Chrome (chromium-1243). Vesktop and VS Code weren't running and weren't started on the host, so their maps weren't read; they ship the same files.
- Tests: the off-bus app in the protocol tests now maps a file named `icudtl.dat`, a copy of the C library the test runs on, preloaded into its copy of `sleep` with `LD_PRELOAD`; the terminal-like app has the same file beside its executable, unmapped. That test failed on the old code, which counted the file beside the program. Unit tests check the parsing (marker names, a removed file, names that only contain a marker, anonymous mappings), a missing process and a maps read that never finishes: a FIFO in a fake `/proc`, which must count as not Chromium-family within its 100 ms deadline.
- Two paths dropped a listener the registry may already have counted, which makes at-spi2-core turn `IsEnabled` on and off again and leaves `toolkit-accessibility = true` behind, without saying so: a wait cut by the call's deadline (`Turned::Late`), and a failed `IsEnabled` read after registering, whose detail also said registering had failed. The read failure is now `Turned::Unread`, worded "reading IsEnabled after registering the server's listener failed", and it, `Late` and a failed registration (which may have reached the registry before its reply was lost) all add that at-spi2-core may have set the key and that it stays. The protocol tests check the three details; each failed on the old code.
- `Busy` gave the restart advice with accessibility still off, though the other call might end `Late` or `unsupported` and the restart be in vain. With `IsEnabled` false it now says to call again; with it true, the app started while it was off, so the restart advice stays. The protocol test runs a 200 ms `wait_for` while an `elements` call holds the switch on a registry that doesn't answer; it got the restart advice on the old code.
- The configuration and elements pages and the entry above said a process in a Flatpak or Snap sandbox gets the plain error. Neither was observed: no snapd or Flatpak is installed here, and a Snap's executable sits under `/snap`, visible from the host. With detection now reading `/proc/<pid>/maps`, a sandboxed Chromium of the same user may well be detected by its mapped files. The pages now name only a process the server can't read, such as another user's; the elements page already says Flatpak is outside the tested scope.
- `wait::a_match_whose_actions_the_wait_had_no_time_to_name_ends_it_met`, from `elements-bind`, failed now and then under load with the cut walk's detail instead of "naming its actions". The cut didn't move: the server's log showed naming started with no time left on every run. But tokio's timer rounds a deadline up to its next millisecond tick, so the mock's `GetName` reply, quicker than that, sometimes still arrived, naming succeeded, and the wait gave the cut walk's reason. Either way the element gets no ref. The test now makes the app take 500 ms to name the button's action, more than any tick. With every core busy (`sha256sum /dev/zero` × `nproc`, load average 11 to 19), it passed 20 of 20 runs; before, 3 of 15 failed under the same load. The server is unchanged.

## 2026-10-15: the walk reads up to 20,000 objects

- One big list, table or grid used up the walk's 2,000-object cap, so nothing after it was read, and since `elements-bind` every control outside a named row of such a window is `unbound: cut_short`. The 2026-10-14 Chromium measurements found no bound ref on a page of 2,000 rows. cua-driver doesn't expand containers with more than 2,048 children, so one grid can't use up its walk; ported as it is, that would leave the grid unread and every control outside rows `cut_short` all the same, so other ways were measured first.
- Measured in the nested headless niri with release builds: the windows of `harness/fixtures/big.py` (`make nested-big`), a `Save` button and `Search` entry above one big container and an `Apply` button and `Notes` entry below it, and the 300- and 2,000-row pages of `make nested-chromium-a11y` in Helium 0.19.2.1. A scratch AT-SPI client run in the nested session counted each window's whole tree as the walk reads it and timed `Collection.GetMatches`; a scratch script replayed the walk over those counts. Neither is kept. Times are `elements` calls with `name_contains`, each of which walks the whole tree and names a few elements:

  | Window | objects | where the cap went | cap 2,000 | no cap | controls bound, before → after |
  |---|---|---|---|---|---|
  | GTK 4, 3,000 buttons in a box | 6,026 | the box, a button and its label each | 0.18–0.22 s | 0.53–0.56 s | 0 of 4 → 4 |
  | GTK 4, list box of 1,500 named rows | 7,526 | the list box, five objects a row | 0.17–0.18 s | 0.65–0.66 s | 0 of 4 → 4 |
  | GTK 3, 3,000 buttons in a box | 3,020 | the box | 0.35–0.36 s | 0.40–0.41 s | 0 of 4 → 4 |
  | GTK 3, tree view of 5,000 rows × 3 | 15,022 | the table's 15,003 children, cells and headers | 0.11–0.12 s | 0.78–0.82 s | 0 of 4 → 4 |
  | Chromium, 2,000 table rows | 2,218 | the table's rows; most aren't showing, so what is in them isn't read | 0.14–0.15 s | 0.15–0.16 s | 0 → 10 of 211 listed |
  | Chromium, 300 table rows | 518 | not reached | 0.03–0.04 s | 0.03–0.04 s | 10 → 10 |

  The GTK 4 and GTK 3 fixtures of `make nested-a11y` and the two list fixtures walk 10 to 165 objects. Qt Quick put none of the 3,000 buttons of a `Repeater` in a `ScrollView` on the bus, only the window's 9 other objects, and its list views make delegates only for the rows in view, so no Qt window was measured past the cap.
- Deferring big containers, reading everything outside containers with more than N children first: replayed for N of 32, 64 and 256, it reads every control after the grid in each GTK window above, and binds none of them while the walk is still cut, as expected.
- `Collection.GetMatches`: GTK 3 (through at-spi2-core 2.62.1's ATK bridge), Chromium and Qt 6.12 answer it on the frame; GTK 4.24 has no `Collection` interface (the frame lists `Accessible`, `Action` and `Component`). A match rule has states, attributes, roles and interfaces but no name, so the app can only say that no object it holds has a control's role beyond those read. at-spi2-atk's traversal also goes into subtrees the walk skips because they aren't showing: Chromium's 2,000-row page has 2,030 push buttons and 16,147 objects by `GetMatches`, against 2,218 objects the walk reads, so it would bind none of the page's buttons. Replayed with deferral and a per-role check, it binds the GTK 3 tree's `Save`, `Search`, `Apply` and `Notes`, the entries alone in GTK 3's box of buttons, at most the entries in Chromium, and nothing in GTK 4. A call took up to 125 ms, and 256 ms for any rule on GTK 3's 3,000 buttons. Rejected: reading the tree is cheaper where it fits the budget, works in GTK 4 too, and counts by name.
- Rows: a named row binds its controls however far the walk got, so the GTK 4 list box of named rows bound 497 of the 500 elements listed at the old cap. But Chromium's table rows are unnamed (192 of the 211 elements listed are `unnamed_row`), as are GTK 4's list view rows unless the app names them, so rows don't bind most big containers' controls. Nothing changes there.
- What ended each walk was the cap, never the budget: a whole walk read 2,218 to 15,022 objects in 0.15 to 0.82 s, from 0.05 ms an object (GTK 3's tree) to 0.13 ms (GTK 3's box), 0.07 for Chromium and 0.09 for GTK 4. `NODE_CAP` is now 20,000, above the largest window measured, and 2.6 s at the slowest of those rates, so for a slow or larger app the three-second budget still ends the walk. `BUDGET` is unchanged.
- The cost: a listing, a `wait_for` element check or an `element_changes` walk of a big window takes as long as reading its tree, up to 0.8 s in these windows where 0.1 to 0.35 s used to stop at the cap, and keeps up to 20,000 nodes in memory for the call. In return every window above lists its controls with refs that act. A window over 20,000 objects or three seconds still makes its controls outside named rows `cut_short`.
- Tests: `make nested-big` lists the four GTK windows above and requires each walk to read the whole tree and `Save`, `Search`, `Apply` and `Notes` to have a bound ref. With the old cap it failed at the first window, `the walk was cut short: "node_cap"`; it passes now, with the walks taking 0.69 to 1.31 s for a listing of up to 500 elements.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: big containers' children are read last

- A walk that the cap or the budget still cuts inside a big list, table or grid stopped before the window's controls after it, so they weren't listed at all. The walk now holds back the children of any node with more than 64 children (`walk::BIG`) and reads them once nothing outside such containers is left, in the order the containers were found, with big containers inside them held back again. Everything else is read depth first in document order, as before. This is the deferral the entry above measured; alone it binds nothing, but the controls are listed.
- 64: in every window measured, the containers with more than 11 children held a list's records, from 104 (the Qt Quick list fixture's rows put on the bus without containers) to 15,003, so 64 holds back every one of them and nothing else. Replayed over those trees, 32, 64 and 256 gave the same listings and bindings.
- The binding rule is unchanged, and a count stays unfinished as long as anything is unread: a walk stopped at its limit with only a big container's children left sets `Walked::missed` like one with nodes left to read next, so a control it read early is `cut_short`, never bound. A unit test walks to the limit with only such children left, which bound `/b/1` until `Pending::is_empty` counted them.
- `elements` lists in the walk's order, so a long list's elements now come after the controls below the list. The tool description says `long lists last` (18 bytes: `tools/list` measures 6,901, 23,921 and 27,152 bytes, under the ceilings), and the site and skill say a container of more than 64 children. `Walked::lineage` and the row pass don't depend on document order: a node's parent is still read before it, so the one-pass row lookup holds.
- Measured with `make nested-big`: a GTK 3 tree view of 8,000 rows (over 24,000 objects) is cut at 20,000 objects in 1.24 s for a listing of up to 500 elements, and lists `Save`, `Search`, `Apply` and `Notes`, `Apply` and `Notes` sitting below the tree, each `unbound: cut_short`. A build without the deferral failed that check with `Apply isn't listed`, its walk ending inside the tree. The four windows read whole list as before, with their controls bound.
- Tests: unit tests in `a11y::walk` check that a grid early in the tree no longer hides `/save` from a capped walk and leaves it `cut_short`, that a big container's children come last and keep their lineage, and the stopped walk above; with deferral off the first two fail, and with the deferred queue left out of `is_empty` the third. A protocol test lists `Save` after a 65-button grid whose first buttons answer in 0.9 s, so the budget runs out inside the grid: `Save` must be listed `cut_short` and `activate_element` refused with `element_unbound`, nothing sent. Without deferral `Save` wasn't listed.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: children an app refuses to list are read as unread

- at-spi2-atk answers `GetChildren` on an object with more than 65,536 children with `org.freedesktop.DBus.Error.InvalidArgs` (`Accessible's child count 66003 exceeds the maximum of 65536 handled by GetChildren`, from a review run of a GTK 3 tree view of 22,000 rows × 3 in the nested session). The walk turned that refusal into an error for the whole `elements` call, and for a `wait_for` element check on the window; `element_changes` said `unknown: upstream_error`. A window that big is what the two entries above are for, and their docs say such a window is cut short, not failed.
- `Request::walked_node` now reads a `GetChildren` refused with `InvalidArgs` as a node with no children and `children_refused`. Any other error still fails the walk as before: a deadline, a lost connection, or the bus's `NoReply` when the app quits before it answers, which would otherwise read as a refusal the app never made. The walk reads on past it and ends with `capped_reason: children_refused`, so the window's other controls are listed, and `cut_short` outside named rows, since the unread children may hold a look-alike. Finding the application's frames (`Request::node`) still fails on the refusal, with the D-Bus error's name: without the frames there is nothing to list.
- A `capped_reason` rather than only `Walked::missed`: `missed` alone would leave `capped` false, and `capped` is what tells the agent, `wait_for`'s `absent` check and the change report's survey that the listing is not the whole window. A walk with a node gone mid-read sets `missed` too, but there the nodes are gone, while refused children are still on the app's side. `wait_for`'s absent check counts a capped walk as unchecked, and the survey says `unknown: children_refused`.
- Not changed: cua-driver reads `ChildCount` first and doesn't expand containers with more than 2,048 children. That would read nothing of any big list, against the 20,000 objects above; the refusal is read instead, after one call.
- Only a node's own child count decides deferral (`walk::BIG`). A list split into groups of 64 or fewer, such as an HTML table with one `<tbody>` per section, or a settings list of 40 groups of 50 rows, is read in place, and a walk cut inside it never reads what follows. A review's scratch unit test confirmed that at a cap of 500. It takes such a list of more than about 20,000 objects to matter; deferring by subtree size would need the size before reading the subtree, which the bus doesn't give, so it isn't done.
- The rates in the entries above are a release build's. A review ran `make nested-big`'s windows on a debug build: the listings took 1.45 to 2.73 s, and the 15,022-object tree was cut by `budget_exhausted` at 9,985 objects in 3.09 s. A debug build reads two to three times slower; so may a busy machine or app, and then every listing of such a window takes the whole three seconds and is `cut_short`.
- Tests: a unit test walks a tree whose `/refused` node's children are refused and requires `/save` after it to be read and the walk cut short with `children_refused`; it failed before the flag was set. Protocol tests make `GetChildren` on a grid fail with `InvalidArgs`, and require the listing to have `capped_reason: children_refused` and `Save` after the grid `unbound: cut_short`, and an action's `element_changes` to say `unknown: children_refused`; with the refusal still an error they got `upstream_error`. Two more require `GetChildren` failing with `NoReply` to fail the listing with `upstream_error`, and one past its one-second deadline to fail it with `deadline_exceeded`; each failed with the refusal widened to that error. `make nested-big` adds the GTK 3 tree view of 22,000 rows, whose table has 66,003 children: it lists `Save`, `Search`, `Apply` and `Notes`, each `cut_short`, after 19 objects, in 0.25 s.
- The earlier entries say each window's controls get refs that act. `make nested-big` now activates `Apply`'s ref in each of the four windows read whole and requires the fixture to count the click; each activation took about 0.3 s. Before, it only checked that the ref was there.
- Two unit tests cover what `make check` didn't: a 2,500-object window read whole at `NODE_CAP`, which fails with the cap back at 2,000, and two grids whose children come in the order the grids were found, which fails with the deferred queue read last first. The protocol suite's mock reads about 1,800 objects in three seconds in a debug build, so a protocol test past 2,000 objects would end at the budget instead.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: a Chromium page's elements get boxes in logical pixels

- Re-opened "Electron's elements stay without a box" (2026-10-13) with new evidence. Measured in the nested headless niri with Helium 0.19.2.1 and Playwright's Chrome for Testing 153 (chromium-1243), each opening a fixture page in app mode with an `<input>`, a `<textarea>`, two `contenteditable` boxes, a button and a same-origin `<iframe>` holding a button, each a solid colour at a fixed CSS place. Every case was run tiled, floating, floating after `SetWindowWidth`/`SetWindowHeight` and `MoveFloatingWindow`, and tiled again, at scales 1, 1.25, 1.5 and 2, with niri's default client-side decorations and with `prefer-no-csd`. A scratch AT-SPI client in the nested session read every object's extents in SCREEN, WINDOW and PARENT coordinates, and the true box of each element was found from its colour in a `grim` screenshot of the output. Neither the client nor the fixture is kept.
- With client-side decorations Chromium's frame was 32 by 42 logical pixels larger than the window in every run, both browsers, every scale, every layout, and with `custom_chrome_frame: false` in the profile too: its shadow is 16 left and right, 10 above and 32 below, and no object of the window's size exists. Nothing on the bus says where the window starts in the frame: those margins are known only from the shadow's shape, which Chromium may change. So such a window stays `frame_size_mismatch`, as before.
- With `prefer-no-csd`, Chromium drops its shadow and title bar and its frame is exactly the window. The frame guard passes, and at scale 1 the boxes were right, within a pixel of the screenshot's. But at 1.25, 1.5 and 2 every page element's box was in the output's pixels: at scale 1.5 the field the screenshot showed at 106,106.67 200×29.33 was listed at 126,126 300×45, so a click by its ref landed 12 pixels below its centre, and at scale 2 the button's listed centre was off the button. That was a live bug: the server sent clicks to the wrong place.
- What the extents are: Chromium's own views, the frame, `BrowserView`, `ContentsContainerView` and the infobar of Chrome for Testing among them, give logical pixels at every scale. The page's `document_web` and everything under it, the iframe's document and button included, give the object's logical place in the frame times the output's scale, as the 2026-10-13 entry saw in VS Code. PARENT coordinates mix the two (the document's parent offset was the difference of a scaled and an unscaled position), and SCREEN coordinates are WINDOW ones on Wayland, so neither settles the unit. The document's extents do: in every run its WINDOW extents were its parent view's times the output's scale, within a pixel, position and size, and at scale 1 the same as the view's.
- The rule: for an element under a frame's outermost `document_web`, the server compares the document's extents with its parent's. The parent's, within a pixel, means logical pixels; the parent's times the output's scale means the output's, and the element's extents are divided by the scale before the window's origin is added. At scale 1 nothing is compared. Neither, both (a parent so small, and so near the window's corner, that both fit), or a document or parent without extents is `unmappable: page_scale_unknown`. The outermost document counts, since a frame's document has the size of the iframe that holds it, both in the output's pixels. Replayed on every `prefer-no-csd` run above, it put every fixture element within 0.8 pixels of the screenshot's box where the window didn't clip it, and refused nothing measured. It is checked per window at listing time, from the walk's own extents, and again right before a pointer tool aims, reading the document's and parent's extents with the element's.
- The rule doesn't rest on Chromium's view classes or a title bar style: it reads only roles and extents, so another toolkit's `document_web` in logical pixels, as WebKitGTK or Firefox might give, is placed as before at scale 1, as logical when its document matches its parent, and refused when it matches neither. Those weren't measured.
- Known limit: at scale 1 the unit is taken to be logical without comparing the document with its holder, since both units are one there. If Chromium still reported a page in the output's pixels of a former scale, as it might for a moment after the output's scale changes from 1.5 to 1 or the window moves to a scale-1 output, its elements would be listed 1.5 times too large and too far from the window's corner, and a click by ref would go there. The base had the same risk, since it placed every element at scale 1 as logical, and no run reproduced the lag, so the code is unchanged. Requiring the document to match its holder at scale 1 would close it, at the cost of refusing, at scale 1, a page in another toolkit whose document doesn't fill its holder.
- End to end: with the server built from this change, a click by `element_ref` on the fixture's button reached the page, counted by the page, at scales 1, 1.25, 1.5 and 2 with `prefer-no-csd`, in both browsers; with client-side decorations at 1.5 it was refused `frame_size_mismatch`.
- Tests: unit tests place a page's field at each scale and frame shape measured, from the measured extents, against the screenshot's box; keep Chromium's own views in logical pixels; refuse a document in neither unit, without extents, or too small to tell; keep a CSD frame refused with a known page; and take the outermost document over an iframe's. A protocol test lists a page's button at scale 1.5 and aims at its centre, then changes the document's width and needs the same ref refused `page_scale_unknown`; it fails with the old placement. `make nested-chromium-a11y` floats a page and clicks its button by ref with `SSD=1`, or needs `frame_size_mismatch` without; with the page unit taken out of the server, the `SSD=1 SCALE=1.5` run failed.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: `set_element_text` on a field without EditableText says what to do

- Measured in the same runs: Chromium's `<input>`, `<textarea>`, `contenteditable` and `contenteditable` with `role="textbox"` all have `Text`, `Action` (`activate`, `showContextMenu`), `Component`, `Collection`, `Document` and `Hyperlink`, and none has `EditableText` or `Value`. Called anyway through libatspi, `EditableText.SetTextContents` and `InsertText` returned false and `Value.SetCurrentValue` did nothing, and the text stayed empty; nothing else on the bus writes text. So `set_element_text` can't set them through accessibility alone, and no keyboard fallback is added to it: typing belongs to `type_text`, with its focus checks and `expect`.
- The refusal was a plain `invalid arguments:` text, which the skill says to fix and send again. An editable field without `EditableText` now gets `text_not_settable`, whose detail says nothing was sent and to focus the field with `activate_element`, then type with `type_text`, pressing `ctrl+a` with `key` first to replace what it holds. An element that isn't editable keeps the plain mistake. The `editable` state is read only on that refusal.
- What works instead, checked through the server in both browsers: `activate_element` on each field focused it (`states_set: ["focused"]`), `type_text` then reached it exactly, and `key` `ctrl+a` followed by `type_text` replaced its whole text.
- Tests: a protocol test needs `text_not_settable` and the advice for an editable field without `EditableText`, and the plain mistake for a button; it fails on the old refusal. `make nested-chromium-a11y` now needs `text_not_settable` on the page's field.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: fixes after the review of Chromium's page boxes and text fields

- A Chromium password field (`password_text`, `editable`) has no `EditableText` either, and `set_element_text` refused it `text_not_settable`, whose detail says to type with `type_text`: that teaches typing a secret. The role is read with the states on that refusal, and a password field is `secret_field` before the `editable` state counts, as the last gate would make it. A protocol test needs `secret_field` for such a field; it failed with `text_not_settable` before.
- Chromium marks a web object offscreen only once nothing of it is left in the page, so a link scrolled half under a browser's toolbar stayed `showing`, and its box was centred on the toolbar. A page element whose centre is outside its outermost document's extents, divided by the page's unit and placed from the window's origin like the element, is now `not_showing`, at the listing and in the re-check before an aim, which reads the document again. At scale 1 a document without extents isn't checked; at other scales it is already `page_scale_unknown`. A unit test moves the measured fields a pixel either side of their document's top and left edges at every scale measured, and a protocol test scrolls a listed button above its page and needs the aim refused; both fail without the check.

## 2026-10-15: `shared = "required"` makes the shared engine mandatory

- When the bridge can't reach the shared engine, or the engine refuses it, `serve` serves its client standalone. That keeps the client working, but each such client brings back a standalone server with its own crash guardian, event stream, accessibility connection and listener, and audit writer; only the lease, the stop flag and the input marker, kept in the runtime directory, stay shared. Users who want one engine and one state for the whole instance can now require it.
- The setting extends `shared` rather than adding a second key: `true`, `false` or `"required"` in the policy file, and `1`, `0` or `required` in `NIRI_COMPUTER_USE_SHARED`. A second key, such as `require_shared`, would allow `shared = false` with `require_shared = true`, which means nothing. The variable still wins over the file. A wrong value of the variable still means standalone, except when the file says `"required"`: then the client gets `engine_unavailable` with the variable's error, as `confirm_lease` stays on after a typo, so that a typo can't drop the requirement. `status.shared` reports `setting` (`on`, `off` or `required`) and `error`; the hello carries the setting the bridge decided, so that a shared engine reports the client's own setting (see the fixes after the review, below).
- How a server that can't serve should fail was checked against the installed clients' sources, without running them: Claude Code 2.1.300 (its bundle), Codex 0.162.1 (tag `rust-v0.162.1`) and pi 1.2.0 (`packages/coding-agent/src/extensions/mcp/`, at 1.1.0-55 and the installed dist).
  - A server that exits before `initialize`, or answers it with a JSON-RPC error, is a failed server in all three. Codex shows the user one warning, "MCP client for `x` failed to start", with the server's stderr only in its log (`codex-mcp/src/connection_manager/startup.rs:131`, `rmcp-client/src/stdio_server_launcher.rs`). pi shows "MCP servers need attention" with the first line of the error, and the stderr tail only in `/mcp`. Claude Code marks it failed (`type: "failed"` in its connect code), and writes stderr only to its MCP log. Codex and pi tell the model nothing. Claude Code tells it that the server failed to connect, but only behind a feature flag Anthropic controls. None of the three retries a stdio server on its own.
  - A tool result with `isError` reaches the model in all three, with its text or, in Codex, its `structuredContent` (`core/src/mcp_tool_call.rs`, `protocol/src/models.rs`; pi `tools.ts` `convertMcpResult`), and the user sees the failed call.
  - So the server starts and fails every call: `bridge/unavailable.rs` answers `initialize` and lists the tools of the client's profile (`tools::Server::listed`, the same router the engine's sessions start from), and every call gets `engine_unavailable` with the bridge's reason, which is `bridge::connect`'s error as it was: the socket path and errno, the deadline, or the engine's refusal and its detail.
- The session doesn't try the engine again. Reaching it later would serve a tool list made without an engine: an engine leaves out `shell_panel` and `noctalia` without Noctalia, and the element tools without an accessibility bus. The detail says to restart the server instead. It answers no call with anything but `engine_unavailable`, so it reaches no engine, niri or desktop, and writes no audit line.
- Mid-session nothing changes, and nothing there can start standalone state: once relaying, the bridge already never serves standalone, whatever the setting. A lost engine gives `engine_lost`, and each later request reaches or starts a shared engine through `bridge::connect`, or gets `engine_unavailable` and tries again on the next one.
- Tests: protocol tests in `tests/protocol/shared.rs` hold `engine.lock` with the variable at `required`, put a refusing engine on the socket with the file at `"required"`, and set a wrong variable with the file at `"required"`; each requires the tools listed, `engine_unavailable` with the reason for every call tried, and no crash guardian, which a standalone server starts first. All three failed before, served standalone. The third also requires a client without the variable to be served by the shared engine, with `status.shared.setting` `required`. `without_an_engine_in_time_the_client_is_served_standalone` still checks the default fallback. A unit test covers the variable and the file in every combination.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: fixes after the review of `shared = "required"`

- An invalid policy file dropped the requirement. `parse` rejects the whole file for any mistake, some of which depend on the session (a relative `capture_dir`, a preset only `unrestricted` allows, an unknown key), and `Loaded::Invalid` counted as `shared = true`, so a file with `shared = "required"` and `capture_dir = "shots"` served its client standalone when the engine was out of reach. `Loaded::shared` now reads the top-level `shared` key on its own from an invalid file that is still TOML (`toml::Table`, then `Sharing`'s own deserializer), and keeps `"required"`. Any other value there still counts as `true`, as it did before the setting existed, so an invalid file never turns shared mode off. Text that isn't TOML, or a `shared` inside a table, requires nothing.
- A shared engine reported `status.shared.setting` `on` for a client whose policy file was over the 64 KiB the hello carries, which the engine reads as unreadable. The bridge decides from the whole file, so the hello now carries the setting the bridge decided, spelled as the variable (`1` or `required`), instead of the variable as given. A bridged client's variable was always valid or unset, since a wrong one never reaches the engine, so `status.shared.error` loses nothing.
- The unavailable session read stdin through rmcp's codec, which has no line limit. It now reads through `session::Incoming`, as standalone sessions do, so a line over 16 MiB ends it.
- Tests: a unit test reads `shared` from invalid files: `"required"` beside a relative `capture_dir`, an unknown key or a wrong type is kept, while `false`, a `"required"` under another key or inside a table, and text that isn't TOML count as `true`. Protocol tests give a refusing engine a client whose file has an unknown key beside `shared = "required"`, check `status.shared.setting` from the engine for a 70 KiB file and for an invalid one, and send a line over 16 MiB to the unavailable session. Each failed with its fix taken out: the first three without the recovery, the 70 KiB case without the hello's setting, and the last without `Incoming`, where the session stayed up.
- No dependency was added; `toml` was already one.

## 2026-10-15: the niri compatibility matrix

- `make niri-matrix` checks a niri release in one command instead of by hand. niri is built from source at each ref by `scripts/niri-build.sh`, cached by commit, and nested with `harness run --niri`; the system's niri is never replaced. A build runs only as the nested niri and as `niri validate` on the generated config, which the harness's preflight runs on the host with the nested session's environment before the session starts.
- Expectations come from the ref, not from what the server reports, so a wrong version rule shows up as a failure: a release tag with the pin's major and minor must pass the `--compat` probe and the five acting suites, any other release tag must be read-only with the observation tools answering, both with the event stream still `connected` at the probe's last `status` (an `Observe` row's later calls don't touch the stream, so a stream stopped after `wait_for` would otherwise pass), and anything else, such as `main`, is only recorded. A release that no longer builds is recorded as not built, unless it is the supported one.
- The probe's contract follows the documented order of refusals: without the lease every acting tool is `lease_required` on every niri, and `acquire_desktop` tells the versions apart, `read_only` on an unsupported one. `release_desktop` answers without the lease.
- The probe's GTK 4 fixture is drawn by GTK's Cairo renderer. On niri 25.11 in the headless cage, the window GTK's default GL renderer drew wasn't shown within the fixture's ten seconds (the output stayed magenta and niri logged `Failed to bind Framebuffer` as the window closed), while `wev`'s shared-memory window was. With `GSK_RENDERER=cairo` the same probe passed. The acting suites keep the default renderer.
- First run, on this machine: v25.08 doesn't build, since its `libdisplay-info-sys` 0.2.2 needs libdisplay-info below 0.3 and the system has 0.3.0. v25.11 is read-only and the observation tools answer: everything `niri-ipc` 26.4.0 reads from 25.11 parses, since 26.04 only added requests, events and variants. niri `main` at `fc9e8c4` still reports `26.04`, so the version rule allows acting, but it reports an `ext-image-copy-capture` session, which `grim` 1.5.0 opens for the server's screenshots when niri offers the protocol, as a cast of kind `ExtImageCopyCapture`. `niri-ipc` 26.4.0 has no such `CastKind`, so the `CastsChanged` event that follows the first screenshot stops the server's event stream (`schema_incompatible`), and from then on the server is read-only and `wait_for` fails with `upstream_error`. A niri built from `main` now, as niri-git packages are, passes the version rule and then turns read-only at the server's first screenshot. The next release will report a new version and be read-only from the start; acting on it needs a `niri-ipc` that knows the new kind.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: screencast events are dropped unread

- The 2026-10-08 entry makes two unparsable events stop the stream, because a build that can't read niri's events can't trust its state. That holds for the events the state and the waiters read. The server never reads screencasts: nothing reads niri-ipc's `casts` state, and the waiters (`waiter.rs`) map `CastsChanged`, `CastStartedOrChanged` and `CastStopped` to no change. Yet niri built from `main` after 26.04 reports each `grim` capture as a cast of kind `ExtImageCopyCapture`, which niri-ipc 26.4.0 doesn't have, so the server's first screenshot stopped its stream and left it read-only until restart (the entry above).
- The stream now drops events of the kinds in `events::IGNORED`, those three, before parsing them: they reach neither the state nor the waiters. The kind is the event object's top-level key, and an object with more than one key isn't dropped. Every other kind is parsed as before, so one that doesn't parse, including a kind niri adds later, still counts toward stopping the stream. Adding a kind to the list needs the same argument: nothing reads it.
- All of them are dropped, not only those that don't parse. The first version skipped only the unparsable ones. In the matrix's nested run on niri `main`, the skipped `CastsChanged` was followed by a `CastStopped` that parsed, and niri-ipc's reducer panics on a stopped cast it never saw (`expect("stopped cast was missing from the map")`, `state.rs` in niri-ipc 26.4.0). That panic ended the reader task inside `send_modify`, so the state froze while `status` still said `connected`, and `launch`, a layout switch while typing and a click after a window moved went unseen. A unit test with that sequence panicked before the change.
- A panic in any other part of niri-ipc's reducer froze the stream the same way, with `status` still saying `connected`. The next entry makes such a panic stop the stream.
- Tests: unit tests in `niri::events` check that the three kinds are dropped whether or not they parse, that an object with a second key isn't, that a stream given a `CastsChanged` of an unknown kind and then its `CastStopped` stays on its first connection and applies a later `WindowOpenedOrChanged`, and that an unparsable `WindowsChanged` twice still stops the stream with its name in the error. With the skip removed, the first two fail; with every unparsable event skipped, the fail-closed tests fail.
- `make niri-matrix` after the change: v26.04 and v25.11 as before. niri `main` at `fc9e8c4` keeps its stream `connected` and passes the probe and `--control`, `--actions`, `--shell` and `--a11y`. `--input` still fails in M7's checks of a keyboard layout switch during typing, three runs out of three, in different ways: `lock_stale` after a cancelled call, and `wev` left in the other layout group, either way round. niri `main` moved to Smithay's new virtual keyboard and pointer (`7da6ae44`), which is the likely cause; it isn't confirmed.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: a panic in niri-ipc's reducer stops the event stream

- niri-ipc's `EventStreamState` panics on events that don't fit the state it has: `expect` on a missing workspace, window, cast or keyboard layout list (`state.rs` in niri-ipc 26.4.0). Such a panic ended the reader task inside `send_modify`, which left the last replica in the channel: `status` said `connected`, actions went ahead on a state that no longer changed, and waiters heard nothing.
- `events::read` now applies each event inside `catch_unwind`. A panic ends the connection as `Ended::Panicked`, with the event's type name and the panic's message, and the task stops the stream at once as `schema_incompatible`, the state for "this build can't follow the running niri". `acquire_desktop` and every action are then refused with `read_only`, and `desktop_state` and `wait_for` fail with `upstream_error` carrying the message. The event goes to no waiter; they get the reset every ended connection sends.
- It stops at once, unlike an unparsable event, which gets one reconnect: the event parsed, so niri and the pinned niri-ipc disagree about what the stream means, and a fresh replica would meet the same disagreement. A new state wasn't added: `schema_incompatible` already means the stream stopped until restart because the build doesn't match niri, and the policy already refuses on it.
- Only the reducer step is caught. The rest of the task returns errors, and the release profile keeps `panic = "unwind"`, which `catch_unwind` needs. The replica a panic leaves half-updated is replaced right after, so nothing reads it.
- Test: a `WindowClosed` for a window niri never reported, which panics the reducer, stops the stream as `schema_incompatible`, with `WindowClosed` and the panic's message in `desktop_state`'s error, a reset to the waiters, and the connection closed. Without `catch_unwind` the reader task dies and `wait_for` fails on the closed channel, while the last value still says `connected`.
- No dependency was added.


## 2026-10-15: an `engine_version` refusal says which client to restart

- `make nested-upgrade` showed what a client of 0.1.0 or 0.1.1 still running after an upgrade meets once its engine ended and a client of the new build started an engine: every call fails with `engine_unavailable`, `the engine, PID …, runs another build of niri-computer-use, which it keeps until its last client leaves; the next call tries again` (0.1.1), or `the bridge speaks hello Some(2); this engine speaks 3; the next call tries again` (0.1.0). No later call gets past an engine that runs, so the advice to retry was wrong, and nothing named the client to restart.
- The engine now writes that detail (`Own::another_build`). It reads its own file again: when the file still holds its build, the bridge is the stale one, and the detail says to restart this MCP client; when the file was replaced, the engine is, and it says to restart the clients it serves. Either way it names the engine's release and file and the bridge's release. Old bridges add `; the next call tries again` after the detail, which nothing here can change, so the detail ends by saying the engine refuses the client whatever it retries.
- Bridges send their release in the hello as `release`, optional, so engines of 0.1.1 and older, whose `Hello` doesn't deny unknown fields, take it and ignore it, and a hello without it parses here. The hello version stays 3: an added optional field changes no meaning. The identity of `/proc/self/exe` still decides whether two builds match; the release only names them, since a development build and a release can share one.
- A bridge of this build tells an `engine_version` refusal apart (`Unreached::OtherBuild`) and no longer adds `the next call tries again` to it. A refusal a retry may get past, such as `engine_busy`, keeps it.
- Tests: two unit tests in `engine::hello` send 0.1.0's and 0.1.1's hello lines, as those builds send them, to an engine of this build and to an engine whose file was replaced, and require the restart advice; both fail on the old wording. A unit test in `bridge` has a fake engine refuse a reconnect with `engine_version` and with `engine_busy`, and requires the suffix only on the second; it fails with the suffix always added. `make nested-upgrade` requires an old client's `engine_unavailable` to say to restart this MCP client, from 0.1.0 and from 0.1.1.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: a client beside an older engine says which clients to restart

- In `make nested-upgrade`, a client of the new build started beside a running engine of 0.1.0 or 0.1.1 is served standalone, as designed, with `status.engine.fallback` holding only that engine's refusal: `the bridge speaks hello Some(3); this engine speaks 2` from 0.1.0, `the engine, PID …, runs another build of niri-computer-use, which it keeps until its last client leaves` from 0.1.1. Neither says what to do.
- Those engines can't be changed, so the bridge adds the advice (`bridge::OLDER_ENGINE`): that engine serves the clients started before this build was installed until the last of them ends, and restarting those clients ends it. Engines of this build give their own advice (the entry above), so the bridge must tell the two apart: a refusal now carries the engine's `release`, optional, which 0.1.0's and 0.1.1's bridges ignore as their `Reply` doesn't deny unknown fields, and the bridge adds the advice only to an `engine_version` refusal without one.
- Tests: a protocol test has a fake engine refuse as 0.1.1 does, with no release, before a client starts, and requires the client's fallback to hold the advice; it fails without it. The protocol test of a binary replaced under a running engine now requires that engine's own advice in the new client's fallback and not the bridge's, so the advice isn't given twice. `make nested-upgrade` requires the advice in the new client's fallback beside an engine of 0.1.0 and of 0.1.1.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: what an upgrade promises

- `make nested-upgrade` installs 0.1.0 and then 0.1.1 from their GitHub releases with `install.sh`, verified against their `.sha256`, into a HOME under the nested run's `TEST_DIR`, leaves two clients on the release's shared engine with the lease held, an audit log and a policy file, and installs this build over them, from a release it packs the way the release workflow does, while one of them types. `docs/development.md` lists what it checks. It runs before each release, with every published release in `UPGRADE_FROM`.
- What it found, before the fixes in the entries above and in `fix(control): read 0.1.0's running marker`: the old engine and its clients kept working on their deleted file; old and new servers shared the lease and refused each other with `lease_held`; 0.1.1's marker and this build's are the same format and each side read the other's; the audit log was appended to and kept every old line; the old policy file loaded. Three things broke: 0.1.0's `running` marker read as unreadable here, and `recover` cleared it while the `wtype` child it named still ran (and `make nested-control`, which checks that `recover` ends a marker's child, failed at `m2-recover` on this branch's base, 12dfa5e); an old client meeting the new engine was told only that the next call tries again; a new client beside an old engine wasn't told what to do.
- The promise: an upgrade from any earlier release is installed while agents run, and every MCP client is restarted afterwards. Until then old and new servers work side by side, sharing the lease, the stop flag and the input-dirty marker, each reading the other's marker as a marker; clients started meanwhile are served standalone; an old client whose engine ends starts an engine of its own build again when no engine of the new build runs, and is refused by the new engine and told to restart when one does, so restarting the old clients is what ends the old build. `recover` from the new build clears a marker any earlier release left. Restarting clients is the one step an upgrade needs. A downgrade isn't promised or tested.
- No version field in the marker, though one was considered. 0.1.0's and 0.1.1's `Marker` deny unknown fields, so any field this build added would make every 0.1.1 server still running read this build's markers as unreadable and send agents to `recover` while a new server types, the problem 0.1.1's notes describe for 0.1.0. The test requires each side to read the other's marker whole. A later release may add a marker field only once the releases it upgrades from read it, or must say in its notes that old servers must end before it types. The lease record and the hello, whose readers ignore unknown fields, can take new optional fields, as the hello's `release` does.
- Policy files: 0.1.0 and 0.1.1 deny unknown keys and go read-only on an invalid file. The test has the old build's `status` read a file with `turn_on_accessibility`, a key added since 0.1.1, as `invalid`, so the Install page says to add new keys only once no older build runs.
- The test runs in shared mode only, which covers standalone servers too: every client of the new build started beside the old engine is standalone. It needs the network for the two downloads; the build under test is installed from `file://`, which `install.sh` takes through `NCU_RELEASES_URL` unchanged.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: the restart advice reads both sides' files

- The advice in the two entries above was decided from the engine's file alone. With two installs on different paths, such as `~/.cargo/bin` and `~/.local/bin`, and clients registered against each, neither file is replaced, so the engine told the other install's client to restart, which runs the same build again and meets the same refusal; and a bridge beside a 0.1.1 engine of the other install told the user to restart that engine's clients, which start it again.
- Each side now reads the other's file: the engine from the connecting process's PID (`SO_PEERCRED`), the bridge from the listening engine's, both through `/proc/<pid>/exe` (`hello::Build::of`). `hello::restart` names what to restart: the side whose file an install has since replaced, or both. When neither was, the refusal names both files and says to point the clients at one. A process whose link can't be read counts as unknown, and the advice says to restart the client if its file was replaced and otherwise to point it at the engine's. `bridge::OLDER_ENGINE` became `hello::older_engine`.
- Tests: a unit test in `engine::hello` refuses a bridge whose file is still installed and requires the advice to point the clients at one file without saying to restart; a protocol test starts an engine and a client from two copies of the binary and requires the same in the client's fallback; the protocol test of a fake 0.1.1 engine, still installed as the test binary, requires the bridge's advice to do the same. All three fail with the advice decided from the engine's file alone. `make nested-upgrade` still requires the restart advice for both upgrade cases.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-15: `key` and `type_text` ask niri where focus is after the last key

- The workflow benchmarks found `type_text` answering `sent` with `focus: matched` while a modal dialog got the message (`docs/results/workflow-benchmarks.md`). Two different things happen there. With the `draft` fixture recording which surface each of the dialog's keys arrived on, niri sent most of the dialog's keys, in many calls all of them, to the form's own surface, and GTK passed them on to its modal window; niri mapped and focused the dialog only after it. Nothing outside the app sees that: niri sent the keys to the window `expect` named. In other calls niri focused the dialog mid-call and sent a key to the dialog's own surface before its event stream reported the move. The check before each key reads that stream, so a move in the last moments of a call went unseen; one base-build run answered `sent` that way.
- After niri has acknowledged the last key, the work now sends niri a `FocusedWindow` request, then applies the events the stream has passed on by then. niri 26.04 answers `FocusedWindow` from its event-stream state, which `State::refresh` updates right after it updates keyboard focus and before `flush_clients` sends the acknowledgement, so the answer holds every focus the keys could have gone to. Focus elsewhere in either makes the call `interrupted`, focus in niri's answer winning over a stale stream for `focused_window`. A request that fails ends the call `uncertain` with `accepted: true` and niri's message, since the keys went out. The request has the usual two-second deadline.
- The other option was waiting until the stream caught up to a point after the last key. niri's stream has no marker a client can ask for, and every event comes from a change, so the only way to know it had caught up would be a timed wait, which adds its length to every call and still guesses. The request answers from the state the stream is made of, at the cost of one round trip.
- The check before each key stays as it was. In the nested runs it is what stops typing once niri sent a key to the dialog: each of those calls ended `interrupted` after 13 to 652 characters, with one key at the dialog's surface. The request after the last key is covered by unit tests against the fake niri, whose `FocusedWindow` can name a window its stream doesn't. No nested run had niri move focus in the last moments of a call, so it hasn't been seen to catch one there.
- An `interrupted` keyboard result whose keys went out has a `detail`: the count, as a failure's detail gives it, and that some of them may have reached the window that took focus, or that the layout changed. The per-key check can't tell which of the keys before it niri routed to the new window either.
