- Cost, from `make nested-latency` with release builds, two runs each of `4e15097` and this change (runs 1792097592-2348388 and 1792097815-2354650 before, 1792097704-2351901 and 1792097926-2359591 after): `type_text` `"ab"` took 251.9 and 251.9 ms (median of 30) before and 252.4 and 252.5 ms after, about half a millisecond for the request. `key` went from 259.3 and 259.7 ms to 259.5 and 259.0, within the runs' spread. Every call answered the same `observed` as before in all 30 rounds of all four runs, so the request found no focus change that wasn't there.
- `make nested-input` gained step 19, a modal dialog taking focus mid-type (`docs/development.md`). GTK maps the dialog only after handling the keys already sent, so whether niri sends a key to it during the call varies. The fixed build needed one to three rounds in each of nine runs; a base-build run under load had none in eight, so the check runs up to twelve rounds before it fails for want of a race.
- The `type_text` and `key` descriptions grew within the `tools/list` ceilings, which were not raised: `act` measures 23,993 bytes of 24,000 and `full` 27,224 of 27,225.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a policy file that isn't TOML keeps `shared = "required"`

- A syntax error anywhere in the policy file, such as a mistyped `[[preset]]` header, dropped the requirement, since `Loaded::shared` read `shared` only from text that parses as TOML; the docs said a mistake elsewhere in the file doesn't drop it. Text that isn't TOML now requires the shared engine when a line before its first table header, a trailing comment aside, is `shared = "required"`. Such a line can only be the top-level key, and reading it fails closed: at worst a client is refused with `engine_unavailable` instead of served standalone, which is what the line asks for.
- The other option was saying in the docs that only a mistake that still parses as TOML keeps the requirement. The scan is a few lines and keeps the promise.
- Tests: the unit test of invalid files now requires `"required"` from text that isn't TOML, and nothing from such a line after a table header or with another value. It failed before the change.
- The scan first matched only the basic string. TOML also spells the value as the literal string `'required'`, which a valid file already reads as `"required"`, so the scan takes it too; mismatched quotes, as in `'required"`, still don't count. The unit test gained both lines, and failed on the first before the change.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: the key that commits a call waits for niri to confirm focus

- The 2026-10-15 change asked niri for the focused window only after the last key, so it reported a key that reached a window niri had just focused but still sent it. The submitting `Return` of `type_text` and the last key of a `key` call, such as the `Return` of `["Down", "Down", "Return"]`, are the keys that confirm a dialog or run a line in a terminal, so before that key the work now asks niri for `FocusedWindow` too, and stops `interrupted` with nothing more sent when focus moved. Once niri has answered, it applies the events the stream passed on meanwhile, as the check after the last key does, so a focus move or a denied `app_id` reported during the request stops the key as well. A failed request fails the call before that key, with niri's error and the count of what went out.
- The other keys keep the stream-only check of 2026-10-15. Asking niri before every key would add a round trip per key to every call, and a stray character typed into a window that just took focus does less harm than a key that confirms it.
- Cost: one more niri round trip in each `key` call and each `type_text` with `submit`. It was not measured with `make nested-latency`; the round trip after the last key, which is the same request, added about 0.5 ms there.
- Tests: unit tests against the fake niri send focus to another window quietly before the submitting `Return`, before a one-key call's key and before the last of three keys, report a focus move on the stream while niri answers the request before the key, and fail that request. Each checks that the key isn't among those the fake received. The four that move focus failed before their changes, with the key sent.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a walk the wait's end cut short keeps the earlier walk's verdict

- On GitHub's x86 runner, the protocol test of an `absent` wait whose match stays timed out with `the last walk was cut short: budget_exhausted` where it expects no `detail`. The walks before had read the whole tree; on the loaded runner the last one began a few milliseconds before `by`, 50 ms before `timeout_ms`, so `elements::poll` gave it almost no budget and it came back cut short. The timeout gave that walk's verdict, which tells the agent to narrow a condition whose walks had read the whole tree.
- `wait::watch` now notes whether a walk returned at or after `by`. Such a walk cut short by its budget was cut by the wait's end, not by the tree, so it keeps the verdict of the walk before it. A wait whose only walk the wait's end cut short still says so, since that walk is all it read.
- The other option was not starting a walk with less time left than the last one took. Walk times vary on a loaded machine, so a walk could still be cut, and the wait would read less often near its end.
- Tests: a unit test with paused time has a whole walk, then one that runs to `by` and comes back cut short, and requires no `detail`; with only the cut walk, it requires the `budget_exhausted` detail. It failed before the change with the CI failure's detail. The protocol test is unchanged: beside 64 CPU burners on a 32-thread machine, with eight copies running at once, it failed 20 of 120 runs before the change, all with that detail, and 0 of 320 after.
- Review: a walk that began with more than its three-second budget of the wait left could still return past `by`, and its `budget_exhausted` verdict was dropped as if the wait's end had cut it. The walk's budget is set before niri says where the window is, a request with a two-second deadline, and runs from the accessibility request made after that answer, so a slow niri moves the walk's end past `by`. (Each accessibility call's own deadline is bounded by what is left of the budget, so the walk itself doesn't overrun it.) `watch` now also requires that the walk began at most `a11y::BUDGET` before `by`, so that `elements::poll` bounded its budget by `by`. That is what `watch` can tell without a new field in `Listing`. A walk begun just over three seconds before `by`, whose budget `poll` bounded by microseconds, keeps its verdict and so says it was cut short, which errs toward reporting the app's slowness. A unit test has a whole walk, then one begun 3.86 s before `by` that comes back cut short 40 ms after it, and requires the `budget_exhausted` detail; it failed before the change with no `detail`.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: `recover` never takes init for the input child

- `recover` ends the input child a 0.1.0 marker names if `/proc/<pid>/stat` still gives the marker's start time, by its group when it leads one. The protocol test of a 0.1.0 marker named PID 1 at start time 1 as a child that had exited. Init leads group 1, and killing group 1 is `kill(-1)`, which signals every process the user may signal, so on a machine whose init started within the first tick of boot the test's `recover` would kill everything running as the test's user.
- Reproduced locally: the unchanged test, run in a user, PID and time namespace whose PID 1 leads its group and reports start time 1, killed the test binary (exit 137) and a `sleep` beside it. With init at its real start time, and with main's version of the test, both survived. Every `test-aarch64` run of this branch before the fix lost its runner after about 47 minutes with no log uploaded; the first run with the fix passed.
- `recover` now treats PID 1 as no input child and says so; no 0.1.0 server could have one there. The protocol test names PID 4,194,304, the kernel's `PID_MAX_LIMIT`, which no process gets, so it names no real process, as the 2026-10-08 entry requires of the `recover` tests.
- Tests: a unit test reads a fake `/proc` whose PID 1 and PID 7 have the same stat, and requires PID 1 to be refused and PID 7 to be ended. It calls only the decision, so a regression fails without sending a signal. The protocol test now requires `recover` to say the child has exited.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: `recover` holds the input child by a pidfd

- `recover` checked the 0.1.0 marker's child by its `/proc` start time, then signalled the PID, or the group, by number (review). It doesn't own that process, so the child could exit and be reaped in between, and the number then name an unrelated process or, once that process made a group of its own, an unrelated group.
- `recover` now opens a pidfd for the PID before it reads `/proc/<pid>/stat`. A pidfd names the process that had the PID when it was opened, for good. If the start time matches afterwards, the process with that PID at the read is the marker's child, and it is the one the pidfd names unless the PID was reused between the open and the read; then the pidfd names a process that has exited, and a signal through it fails with `ESRCH` and reaches nothing.
- The child is killed only through the pidfd, never by its process group. A pidfd pins one process, not its PID or group number after it exits, so any group kill by number could reach a group that took the number after someone else killed and reaped the child. A first version stopped the leader through the pidfd and then killed the group by number; another process could still kill the stopped leader in between (Astra's review, High 2). A pidfd kill alone is enough: 0.1.0 started `wtype` itself through `runner::gated`, not through a shell, in a process group of its own, and `wtype` starts no processes, so the child is the only process typing. A `wtype` on the client's `PATH` that is a wrapper starting the real one in a child process would leave that process typing; `recover`'s manual check still asks the user to look.
- PID 1 is still refused before anything is signalled; a pidfd for it is opened and closed unused. A PID the kernel can't have, 0 or one above `i32::MAX`, and a PID no process has (`ESRCH` from `pidfd_open`) count as exited, as they did before. Any other `pidfd_open` error, such as `ENOSYS` before Linux 5.3, keeps the marker and is reported with the error.
- Tests: a unit test spawns a `sh` leading its own group with a `sleep` in that group, points `end_child` at the leader, and requires the `sleep`, standing in for an unrelated group that took the number, to still run half a second later. It failed with the stop-then-group kill. The test that ends a marked child gives it its own group, as 0.1.0 did, and one sharing the test's group. Tests signal only processes they spawned.
- No dependency was added: rustix's `process` feature has `pidfd_open` and `pidfd_send_signal`.

## 2026-10-16: the first connection's initial state counts as a move

- The 2026-10-12 entry stamped the initial state only of a connection after the first, so the server wouldn't refuse element aims in its first second. The same reason holds at the start: the server, standalone or as the shared engine, starts reading niri's events when it starts, and a client's first call can follow right away, while niri may still be animating a switch or a scroll the user started. A plain screenshot taken then aimed as if nothing had moved, and so did an element aim. Sol's and Fable's third checks found it.
- Every connection's initial state now stamps every output with a workspace, and `everywhere`, the stamp element aims read. The pixel aim's refusal names the cause: "the server had just started reading niri's events" on the first connection, "niri's event stream reconnected" on later ones. The element refusal's detail names both.
- Cost: in the server's first second, a plain screenshot can't be aimed at by pixel, and an element aim is refused as `not_showing`. The pixel refusal's advice, a screenshot that waited for the screen to settle, passes, since its capture settled after the stamp; an element aim passes a second later.
- Tests: a protocol test takes a plain screenshot and a settled one right after the server gets niri's initial state, and requires the plain one's click to fail with the new detail and the settled one's to go out. Another lists an element right away and requires its click to fail as moving, and to go out a second later. Both failed before the change, with the clicks sent. The unit test of the replica now requires the first connection's stamp. The act and elements test desks wait out the first second, and the pointer's unit tests take their screenshot as settled.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a pixel aim's gate compares the event stream's connection

- Round 2 added no connection check to `Gate::pass`, because the reconnect stamp refuses a screenshot of the old connection. That stamp covers only the outputs the new initial state gives a workspace. After a reconnect and an unplug while the gate waited, the output had no stamp and the aim went out by the old layout. Opus's third check found it.
- `EventStream::shifted` now returns the connection its replica comes from with the stamp (`scene::Moves`), and `Shot::unmoved` refuses a connection other than the capture's. Since `unmoved` takes only `Moves`, a caller can't read a stamp without its connection.
- The refusal is `unknown_ref` with the detail the call-start check gives, "niri's event stream reconnected since the screenshot". It is the same condition, so the agent gets the same name wherever the call notices it.
- Tests: a unit test requires `unmoved` to refuse another connection's `Moves` without a stamp, the unplugged output's case; it fails when `unmoved` ignores the connection. No test drives the gate race itself: the reconnect would have to fall between the waiter's last look and the stamp's read, which neither fake can time.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a paste's key reads niri's events again once the keeper admits it

- The checks before each key, focus and layout from niri's stream, the deny list and, before the decisive key, niri's own `FocusedWindow`, ran before the native device asked the keeper to admit the paste key. The admission waits up to 500 ms for `armed`, and `Aftercare::arm` then checked only `Desk::fresh`. Focus, layout and `app_id` events that arrived meanwhile were read only after the key, so Ctrl+V could reach a window the user focused during the admission, including a denied app's (Astra's review, High 1).
- Once `arm` returns, the key loop now drains the waiter again and checks focus, layout and the deny list on what it holds, right before the stroke. A refusal tells the keeper `n` through `Aftercare::withdraw`, which `arm` already used after `armed` for a stale lock read, so the keeper restores the clipboard. A focus or layout change ends the call `interrupted` with nothing pressed, as the same change found before the `k` does, and a denied app fails it with `app_denied`, as before any other key.
- niri isn't asked `FocusedWindow` again. Its answer came right before the `k`, so what the drain can miss is a focus change niri made too close to the stroke for its stream to have passed it on, the gap every key but the decisive one already has. Asking again would add a round trip to every paste and could age the lock read past 250 ms, which would need `checked_before`'s re-read of the lock after the admission as well. Superseded by the next entry, which asks niri again.
- Tests: two unit tests against the fake niri give the call a keeper that admits the key only after the test, having seen the `k`, sends a focus change, or a window change to a denied `app_id`, on the event stream. Both require that no key reaches the compositor and that the keeper reports the clipboard restored after `n`; the first requires `interrupted` with `pressed: 0`, the second `app_denied` with nothing sent. Both failed before the change, with the key sent.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a paste's key gets the decisive checks again once the keeper admits it

- After the previous entry, the stroke after the keeper's admission was preceded only by a drain of niri's stream. niri sends keys to a window it has just focused before its stream reports the move, which is why the decisive key asks `FocusedWindow` first, so a focus change during the admission that the stream hadn't passed on still let Ctrl+V reach the new window. The drain also yields (`Waiter::catch_up`), and nothing checked the lock read's age or the stop flag after it, so the key could go out on a read older than 250 ms (Astra's recheck, High 1 partly fixed and a new Medium).
- Once `arm` returns, the key loop now runs `checked_before` with `decisive` set, then `may_press`, the same checks the decisive key gets before the `k`: the lock read again if its last read is older than `LAST_GATE`, the stream drained, niri's own `FocusedWindow` answer, the stream drained again, the lock read and niri asked once more if that answer came late, then the stop flag, the deny list, the call's time and `Desk::fresh`. Nothing is awaited between `Desk::fresh` and the stroke. A refusal or a failed query tells the keeper `n` through `Aftercare::withdraw`: focus or the layout moving ends the call `interrupted` with nothing pressed, a denied app fails it with `app_denied`, and a lock, a stop or niri's error fails it as before any key, nothing sent.
- The lock read that has aged across the wait is read again rather than refused, as `checked_before` already does when niri's answer before the decisive key comes late. `may_press` refuses with `lock_stale` only if the read has aged again by then.
- The checks run twice for a paste, before the `k` and after `armed`, rather than only after. Moving them after `arm` would write `k` before the stop flag, the deny list and focus were checked, and `arm`'s own check of the lock read's age would then come before any read the call made. Running them twice costs a paste one more `FocusedWindow` round trip.
- Tests: two unit tests against the fake niri give the call a keeper that admits the key only after the test has seen the `k`. In one, niri's `FocusedWindow` answer names window 2 from then on while its stream still says window 1; the call must end `interrupted` with `pressed: 0`. In the other, niri answers the next `FocusedWindow` 300 ms late, and Noctalia says the screen is locked from the second lock read on; the call must fail with `screen_locked`, not `lock_stale`, which shows the lock was read again after the late answer. Both require that no key reaches the compositor and that the keeper reports the clipboard restored after `n`. Both failed before the change, with the key sent. The old code's stale window was a single yield, which a test can't widen without a hook in the code, so the second test makes the lock read age across the wait this change adds.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-16: a pointer look that kept an aging lock read looks again

- CI failed `a_held_drag_stops_when_another_window_comes_over_its_end` with "the held reply was never asked for", on this branch and on dependabot branches of `main` on 2026-10-14, in both test modes. Under a CPU quota of 1 to 5% the drag's result was `lock_stale`: refused before its first input in three of the four failures and stopped partway in one, with the button released before the round trip the test holds. `Gate::read_lock` keeps a read while it is at most `LAST_GATE` old, and `Desk::fresh` at the end of the same look requires the same, so a read that turns 250 ms between the two fails with nothing slow. Mid-drag, reads are kept until they age out, and an unloaded run already had them 244 ms old at a look's end. Before the first input, the read kept is the one `point` makes before binding the pointer and writing the marker.
- `Gate::pass` now notes whether the lock read is still fresh as the look begins. If it was, and the look ends in `lock_stale`, the look is made once more, and its `read_lock` reads again since the kept read is stale by then. This is the element actions' last gate's rule: a pass the clock cut short is done once more. A look whose own read came back stale, or that ran over 250 ms after a fresh read, isn't retried here, so a slow desktop still refuses or stops as before, and the second-per-gesture budget for reads made again is unchanged.
- Reading before every look would also close the gap, at the cost of a Noctalia and `loginctl` read every 20 ms of a drag. Re-reading at a smaller age leaves the same race at a lower threshold.
- Test: a unit test against the fake compositor starts a look with a fresh read, and a task that runs when the look yields to catch up on niri's events makes that read a second old, so no clock margin decides the outcome. Before the change the look failed with `lock_stale`, before the first input and during a drag; now both pass with the lock read again. In throttled runs of the protocol test, `main`'s gate failed 3 of 130 and this branch's unfixed gate 1 of 42; with the change, 0 of 160.
- No dependency was added.

## 2026-10-17: zbus's merged crates, ignored for a week

- RUSTSEC-2026-0370, -0371 and -0372 mark `zvariant`, `zbus_names` and `zvariant_derive` unmaintained: zbus 6.0.0, published 2026-10-16, merged them into zbus, and zbus 5.19.0 still depends on them. `cargo deny check` failed on `main` with no change of ours.
- The fix is zbus 6, but the rule is a release at least seven days old, and an unmaintained notice isn't a security fix. `deny.toml` ignores the three advisories by id, each with that reason, until 2026-10-23, when the upgrade to zbus 6 removes them and the ignores together.

## 2026-10-17: `watch`, the desk as JSON lines

- A shell plugin needs to know who drives the desktop and what they do, without parsing the audit log or finding the runtime directory itself, which takes niri's socket with every symlink resolved. `niri-computer-use watch` gives it one versioned format (`format: 1` in `hello`), so the log's layout can change without breaking the plugin.
- It polls every 250 ms instead of watching with inotify. A poll reads `lease.json` and stats two files and the log, and so follows a log moved aside, a holder that died without clearing its record and a directory that appears later with no extra code; an inotify watch would need a fallback for each. A change shows within a quarter second, which is fast enough to show.
- It reads files only, so it serves standalone servers and the shared engine alike, and needs no change to the engine's protocol. The audit log already keeps typed text, titles and paths out, so `action` forwards its lines as they are.
- `project` is the last part of the working directory of the process that started the session's server, read from `/proc` by the PID in the session's label. It names the agent's project, such as `llm-usage-metrics`, which tells two Claude Code sessions apart where the label doesn't.
- tokio's `io-std` feature, for an async stdout whose writes take a deadline: a reader that stops reading for five seconds ends `watch`. It is a feature of tokio 1.53.2, already a dependency, and adds no crate.

## 2026-10-17: the Noctalia plugin

- It lives in this repository, in `noctalia/niri-computer-use`, because it reads `watch`'s format and is versioned with it. The directory has the shape of a Noctalia source's plugin directory, so it can also be offered to community-plugins.
- One `[[service]]` runs `watch` and publishes the desk as the `desk` state; the bar widget, the panel and the desktop glow only draw it. `lib/look.luau` gives each status its glyph, palette role and words, so the three surfaces never disagree, and `lib/desk.luau`, which folds the lines in and says which changes deserve a notification, makes no Noctalia call, so the tests drive it directly.
- Noctalia's `runStream` has no exit callback and no handle to end a stream. `watch` sends `alive` every five seconds, and the service starts a new one when it heard nothing for 15 seconds, at most every 15 seconds, dropping lines from the old one by generation. A gap of more than five seconds between the service's updates means the machine slept, and counts as having heard from the watch, so a wake-up doesn't start a second one beside the first.
- Noctalia's environment often lacks `~/.local/bin` and `~/.cargo/bin` on `PATH`, so the command tries `PATH`, then both places the installers use, and prints a `missing` event, exiting 127, when none has the binary. A `binary` setting overrides that. A build without `watch` answers with its usage line, which the plugin reads as "update it" rather than showing the usage.
- Noctalia has no click-through overlay for plugins. The glow is a desktop widget, on the layer behind windows, sized to the focused output in logical pixels; it shows in niri's gaps. It needs placing once in Noctalia's desktop widget editor.
- The tests run in the Luau CLI against a fake host, as in the headroom plugin, whose loader, test framework and JSON module they reuse. `scripts/test-plugin.sh` fetches Luau 0.742, published 2026-10-10, the newest release at least seven days old, checks its SHA-256 and keeps it in `target/`; `make check` and a CI job run it. `noctalia plugins lint` needs Noctalia itself, so it isn't in the gate; it passed with no warnings.

## 2026-10-17: the tool list of a required client that starts without an engine

- A server that requires the shared engine and had none at its start answers `tools/list` itself, before it knows whether the engine finds Noctalia or an accessibility bus. It leaves out what it knows without one: `noctalia` while `unrestricted` is off.
- For the rest, its handshake says `tools.listChanged`, and the first call that reaches an engine sends `notifications/tools/list_changed` before the call's answer, so the client lists the tools again and gets the engine's list. Only this server says `listChanged`: an engine's list never changes during a session.
- Considered: documenting that such a session may list tools it never serves. A call to one would fail as an unknown tool for the rest of the session, and the notification needs no change in rmcp, which relays the line as it is.

## 2026-10-17: the home page as a project page from around 2000

- The datasheet home page, with five recordings and timing diagrams, was 2,650 words and 5.6 MB of recordings in `site/public`. The new one is a 760-pixel page in the style of a hand-made project page from around 2000: a title band, bevelled buttons, tables, one still that links to one 223 KB clip, and a status bar. It has no JavaScript and is light only, as pages of that time were; the docs keep their dark theme.
- `@fontsource/tinos` 5.3.0, published 2026-07-19, the latest release. Tinos is metric-compatible with Times New Roman, the face such pages were set in, which Linux desktops mostly lack: without it the page falls back to DejaVu Serif or Liberation Serif and changes width from one machine to the next. OFL-1.1, no dependencies; the page loads its Latin regular and bold, 37 KB of WOFF2.
- Code stays in the system's monospace (DejaVu Sans Mono on most Linux desktops), with no web font.

## 2026-10-17: the site as a man page

- The project page from around 2000 was a labelling mix-up: the pick was the man page. The home page is now `niri-computer-use(1)`, about 280 words in man's sections (NAME, SYNOPSIS, DESCRIPTION, INSTALLATION, SAFETY, EXAMPLE, HISTORY, BUGS, SEE ALSO), with a running head and foot and less's reverse-video line along the bottom. It still has no JavaScript, one still and one clip.
- The docs are the rest of the manual: the same colours and monospace through Starlight's custom properties, a running head over each page title and a running foot under each page (component overrides for `PageTitle` and `Footer`, not a forked layout), and section headings in capitals. `src/lib/man.ts` gives each page its man name and section, such as `CLI(1)` or `SAFETY(7)`.
- Dark and light follow the system on the home page and the theme switch in the docs, from one set of `light-dark()` colours in `src/styles/tokens.css`. All text passes WCAG AA in both: the lowest pair is the light amber on the selection colour, 4.67:1.
- All text is set in JetBrains Mono (`@fontsource-variable/jetbrains-mono` 5.3.0, already a dependency), the Latin weight-axis file, 40 KB of WOFF2. Removed: `@fontsource/tinos`, `@fontsource-variable/archivo`, `subset-font` and `scripts/subset-font.mjs`, the pixel mark and the 88×31 buttons. A man page is set in one monospace, so a second face would only add weight.

## 2026-10-17: the install script, piped into sh

- The Install page now leads with `curl -fsSL …/install.sh | sh`, reversing the read-it-first steps. People install tools this way, and reading first is still one line below it. What made the pipe unsafe is fixed in the script instead: it is one brace group, which `sh` reads to its end before running any of it, so a download cut short runs nothing and a program that reads stdin can't take the script's lines. Its questions were already read from `/dev/tty`, so piping still asks before registering an agent. `tests/install.rs` pipes the script into `sh`, whole and cut short.

## 2026-10-17: a script on the site

- The site has one script of its own, `site/src/scripts/less.ts`, 2.3 KB gzipped, on every page. It adds less's keys, search, status line messages and help screen, niri's column focus on the home page, copy buttons, and the clip's replay in place. Without it the pages read in full and the clip opens from a link. The keys skip fields and any key held with Ctrl, Alt or Super, and the help screen turns them off, as WCAG 2.1.4 asks of one-character shortcuts. The home page also gets Astro's prefetch, 1.1 KB, which Starlight turns on for every page.
- Between the home page and the docs, pages slide sideways on niri's default spring (damping ratio 1, stiffness 800) with cross-document view transitions; `prefers-reduced-motion` turns every motion off. The docs' header was named for view transitions through `.header`, which also matched Starlight's inner header and every code block's caption, so each transition into a docs page was aborted; it is `header.header` now.

## 2026-10-17: more agents the install script registers in

- The script runs an agent's own command that adds a stdio server to the user's config, never one that writes a project's config, since it runs from any directory, and never edits a config file itself. Checked against each agent's docs and, where public, its source; no agent binary was run.
- Added, one `try_register` row each:
  - Amp (`amp`): `amp mcp add niri-computer-use -- <binary> serve`. https://ampcode.com/docs/customize/mcp shows the `--` form, and the help text in the `@ampcode/cli-linux-x64` 0.0.1792267246-g1e44a7 binary (2026-10-17) says it adds to `amp.mcpServers` in the CLI settings file, with `--workspace` for the workspace's. The binary holds "MCP server already exists with name", and Amp has no `get` for local servers, so a second install reports Amp's refusal and keeps the first registration.
  - GitHub Copilot CLI (`copilot`): `copilot mcp add niri-computer-use -- <binary> serve`. https://docs.github.com/en/copilot/how-tos/copilot-cli/customize-copilot/add-mcp-servers says it writes `~/.copilot/mcp-config.json`, the user configuration; changelog at 1.0.98 (2026-10-14). The source isn't public and the docs don't give `copilot mcp get`'s exit status for a missing name, so it isn't used as a pre-check.
  - Gemini CLI (`gemini`): `gemini mcp add --scope user niri-computer-use <binary> serve`. `packages/cli/src/commands/mcp/add.ts` at ed96fae (2026-10-16): `--scope` defaults to `project`, so the flag is required; an existing name is replaced. No `--`: with yargs' `populate--` what follows it is appended to `args`, so the command has to come before it, as in the docs' `gemini mcp add python-server python server.py -- --server-arg my-value` (`docs/tools/mcp-server.md`).
  - Qwen Code (`qwen`): `qwen mcp add --scope user niri-computer-use <binary> serve`. The same command code at QwenLM/qwen-code 39c237a (0.25.2, 2026-10-17), with `user` as the default scope; the flag keeps the command right if that default changes. Docs: `docs/users/features/mcp.md`.
- Claude Code stays the only agent with an `mcp get` pre-check: Gemini CLI and Qwen Code overwrite, and neither Amp nor Copilot has a pre-check whose result is documented, so a per-row pre-check field would have one user.
- Left to the client setup page, with their config entries:
  - OpenCode (`opencode`) and Kilo Code CLI (`kilo`, its fork): `mcp add <name> -- <command>` writes the global config in released OpenCode 1.19.3 and Kilo at 2a1903f, but OpenCode's `dev` rewrite (2.0.32, 86c7f7c) writes the project's config unless `--global` is given, a flag 1.19.3 doesn't have. One command can't be right for both, and the install script of a release outlives it.
  - Factory Droid (`droid`): https://docs.factory.com/cli/configuration/mcp gives `droid mcp add <name> "<command>"` with the command as one quoted string, and doesn't say how it is split or whether `--` works; the source isn't public.
  - Cursor CLI (`agent`): https://cursor.com/docs/cli/mcp lists `list`, `list-tools`, `login`, `enable` and `disable`, no add; servers come from `~/.cursor/mcp.json`.
  - Goose (`goose`): no add command at block/goose bfcc6ee (2026-10-17); `goose configure` is interactive and `--with-extension` lasts one session. Entry from `documentation/docs/guides/config-files.md`.
  - Crush (`crush`): no `mcp` subcommand at charmbracelet/crush 12f96b4 (2026-10-17); `mcp add` is a builtin of its `crushrc` config script (README, "MCPs").
- Tested in `tests/install.rs` with the same fake agents: each new agent gets exactly its arguments with `NCU_REGISTER=1`, and none is called, with its command printed, with `NCU_REGISTER=0`.

## 2026-10-30: tokio 1.53.3 for RUSTSEC-2026-0386

- RUSTSEC-2026-0386 (2026-10-27) marks `AsyncFd`'s safe constructors unsound: they accept any `AsRawFd`, so safe code can close or replace a registered descriptor and leave tokio's driver with a freed registration. `cargo deny check` failed on `main` with no change of ours.
- tokio 1.53.3 (2026-10-13) is the fix. It is newer than the seven-day rule allowed before the advisory, and a security fix is the rule's exception. No new crate entered `Cargo.lock`.
- 1.53.3 deprecates `AsyncFd::new` and `with_interest` in favour of `unsafe` `register` constructors. The crate forbids unsafe code, and each of its five `AsyncFd`s owns its `OwnedFd`, so nothing else can close or replace the descriptor; the five calls keep the safe constructors under `#[expect(deprecated)]` with that reason.

|---|---|---|---|---|
| `rmcp` | 3.5.1 | 3.7.0 | 2026-10-22 | Dependabot proposed 3.6.0; 3.8.0 (2026-10-26) is too new. `rmcp-macros` moves with it. No new crate entered `Cargo.lock`. |
| `astro` (site) | 7.3.8 | 7.4.2 | 2026-10-21 | Dependabot proposed 7.4.1; 7.4.3 (2026-10-28) is too new. Installed with `npm install --before 2026-10-24`. |
| `postcss-selector-parser` (site, through Starlight) | 6.1.4 | 6.1.5 | 2026-10-19 | Fixes GHSA-rj75-hqrm-r3gf, quadratic selector parsing; `npm audit` now reports nothing. |
| `Swatinem/rust-cache` | v2.9.2 | v2.9.3 | 2026-10-20 | `d1527c8f67ef9af357290475d4af4b0fab8f12e0`, in `ci.yml`. |
| `taiki-e/install-action` | v2.87.26 | v2.87.31 | 2026-10-23 | `1e29709c0ec63babbe8f266397a465f29a825f91`, in `ci.yml` and `release.yml`. Dependabot proposed v2.87.29; v2.87.32 (2026-10-26) is too new. |

Each action's commit is the one its tag names in the action's repository (`git ls-remote`, the annotated tag peeled). zbus 6 stays out: it is a migration of its own.

## 2026-10-30: argument mistakes that say what to send

In two weeks of use, 57 calls failed on their arguments, mostly from Claude Code guessing names before it loaded a tool's schema, and 46 of them never reached the audit log.

- Every argument type in `tools.rs` denies unknown fields, so `scroll` with `direction` fails naming `direction` (or whichever unknown field serde meets first; serde_json's maps are sorted) and the fields `scroll` takes, where it used to drop it and blame `notches_y`. schemars then adds `additionalProperties: false` to each of those objects, which lets clients that check a call against its schema refuse it themselves. That added 28 bytes to each such object, so the tool list's byte ceilings went up with it. A tool without arguments still ignores what it is given: rmcp never parses them.
- serde can't name the expected fields of an unknown one through `#[serde(flatten)]`, so `pointer_move` and `click` take `x`, `y` and `element` in their own arguments rather than a flattened `SpotArgs`. Their schemas are the same.
- `close_window` takes `window_id`, as every other tool names a window, and `id` as an alias, so clients of 0.1.5 and older keep working. The audit log names it `window_id` either way.
